which can be written as
\omega(X)
by the definition of the connection. Additionally,
G
-equivariance implies

\displaystyle\differential_{P}\phi_{s}(X_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{V}})=X_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{V}}\phi_{s}=\frac{\differential}{\differential t}\phi_{s}\big(u\cdot e^{\omega(X)t}\big)\Big|_{t=0}=\frac{\differential}{\differential t}\rho\big(e^{-\omega(X)t}\big)\phi_{s}(u)\Big|_{t=0}=:-\rho_{\ast}\big(\omega(X)\big)\phi_{s}

(58)

with
\rho_{\ast}
being the map
\rho_{\ast}:\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{gl}(V)
induced by the representation
\rho
. Since (
57
) holds for any vector field on
P
, it is really an equation between
V
-valued one-forms on
P
. As such we can again use our usual tool of picking a local section
\sigma:\mathcal{U}\to P
to pull it back to a
V
-valued one-form on the base manifold. Since the function
\sigma^{\ast}\phi_{s}:\mathcal{M}\to V
is just the representative of the original section
s
restricted to some local region
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathcal{M}
, we can just choose the same letter, but have to keep in mind that its definition still depends on
\sigma
. Contracting in a tangent vector field
v
we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{v}s=\differential s(v)+\rho_{\ast}(\omega_{\mathcal{U}}(v))s\qquad v\in\Gamma(T\mathcal{M}),s\in\Gamma(P\times_{\rho}V)~.

(59)

One can show that this satisfies all the properties in (
55
). In addition, under a change of gauge (the trivializing section)
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma\cdot g
we find
s^{\prime}=\rho(g^{-1})s
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{v}s^{\prime}=\rho(g^{-1})\mathcal{D}_{v}s

(60)

which is the typical behaviour of a covariant derivative.

Let us look at the example of an SU
(N)
bundle and its adjoint bundle ad
P:=(P\times_{\text{Ad}}\mathfrak{g})
. We write a local section of ad
P
as
s=s^{A}b_{A}
with a Lie algebra basis
\{b_{A}\}_{A=1,...,\text{dim}(\mathfrak{g})}
, where
s^{A}
is a collection of local functions on
\mathcal{M}
and
[b_{A},b_{B}]=f_{AB}{}^{C}b_{C}
. For the case of the adjoint bundle, we have
\rho_{\ast}(\omega_{\mathcal{U}})=\text{ad}_{\omega_{\mathcal{U}}}=[\omega_{\mathcal{U}},\cdot]
. The covariant derivative along a vector field
v\in\Gamma(T\mathcal{M})
now reads explicitly

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{v}s^{A}=v^{\mu}\big(\partial_{\mu}s^{A}+[\omega_{\mu},s]^{A}\big)=v^{\mu}\big(\partial_{\mu}s^{A}+f^{A}{}_{BC}\,\omega_{\mu}^{B}s^{C}\big)

(61)

where we also chose a local chart
x^{\mu}
on the base manifold. Under a change of gauge
s^{A}\to(g^{-1})^{A}{}_{B}s^{B}
the connection coefficients transform as

\displaystyle f^{A}{}_{CB}\,\omega^{C}_{\mu}\to(g^{-1})^{A}{}_{C}\big(\delta^{C}_{D}\,\partial_{\mu}+f^{C}{}_{ED}\,\omega^{E}_{\mu}\big)g{}^{D}{}_{B}

(62)

which is just the right transformation such that
\mathcal{D}_{v}s^{A}\to(g^{-1})^{A}{}_{B}\mathcal{D}_{v}s^{B}
. With
g^{A}{}_{B}\approx\delta^{A}_{B}+f^{A}{}_{CB}\lambda^{C}
the infinitesimal transformation
5
5

5

One can also define an adjoint version of
\omega^{A}_{\mu}
by
\omega^{B}{}_{\mu C}=f^{B}{}_{AC}\omega^{A}_{\mu}
which transforms as
\delta_{\lambda}\omega^{A}{}_{\mu B}=\partial_{\mu}\lambda^{A}{}_{B}+[\omega_{\mu},\lambda]^{A}{}_{B}
where
\lambda^{A}{}_{B}
is defined analogously and the bracket is just the commutator between matrices.
reads
\delta_{\lambda}\omega_{\mu}^{A}=\mathcal{D}_{\mu}\lambda^{A}
. The curvature two-form (
53
) also induces a curvature two-form for this associated connection typically denoted by
\Omega^{A}{}_{B}:=(\rho_{\ast}(\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}))^{A}{}_{B}=f^{A}{}_{CB}\Omega_{\mathcal{U}}^{C}
. The Bianchi identity (
51
) translates in this case to

\displaystyle\differential\Omega^{A}{}_{B}+f^{A}{}_{DC}\,\omega^{D}_{\mathcal{U}}\wedge\Omega^{C}{}_{B}-\Omega^{A}{}_{C}\wedge\omega^{D}_{\mathcal{U}}f^{C}{}_{DB}~.

(63)

We need one more ingredient before we turn to the definition of Cartan geometries.

Definition 6.6

(Solder form)
.

A
V
-valued one-form
\theta
on a principal
G
-bundle
P\to\mathcal{M}
is called a solder form if

•

V
is a linear representation space of
G
with representation
\rho:G\to\text{GL}(V)
.

•

\theta(X^{\sharp})=0
with
X^{\sharp}\in\Gamma(VP)
, i.e.
\theta
is horizontal.

•

R_{g}^{\ast}\theta=\rho(g^{-1})\circ\theta

•

\theta
provides an isomorphism
T\mathcal{M}\cong(P\times_{\rho}V)
and therefore
\dim V=\dim\mathcal{M}
.

The idea of a solder form is thus to identify the vector space
V
with the tangent space to
\mathcal{M}
for each point of the base manifold. While, in general, this is additional data one may provide for a principal bundle, there are some special cases where a canonical choice exists. Take for instance the frame bundle
F\mathcal{M}
together with the representation space
\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
. Define for any vector field
X\in\Gamma(TF\mathcal{M})

\displaystyle\theta(X)=\chi^{-1}_{u}(\pi_{\ast}(X))~,

(64)

where the map
\chi_{u}:\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\to T_{u}\mathcal{M}
just takes the components of a vector field and linearly combines them with the basis vectors of the frame at
u\in F\mathcal{M}
, i.e.,
v^{A}\mapsto v^{A}E_{A}
. Its inverse
\chi_{u}^{-1}
is given by the co-frame
e^{A}
defined by
e^{A}(E_{B})=\delta^{A}_{B}
.

Once a solder form is defined, one can construct the
torsion two-form

\Theta
on
P
,

\displaystyle\Theta=D\theta=\differential_{P}\theta+\rho_{\ast}(\omega)\wedge\theta\qquad\in\qquad\Omega^{2}(P)\otimes V

(65)

which also satisfies a Bianchi identity, as a result from taking the second exterior covariant derivative on
P
,

\displaystyle D\Theta=\rho_{\ast}(\Omega)\wedge\theta~.

(66)

Pulling
\Theta
back to
\mathcal{M}
with a section
\sigma
, its local version on the base manifold is given by

\displaystyle T=\sigma^{\ast}\Theta=\differential\theta_{\mathcal{U}}+\rho_{\ast}(\omega_{\mathcal{U}})\wedge\theta_{\mathcal{U}}\qquad\in\qquad\Omega^{2}(\mathcal{M})\otimes V~.

(67)

Under a gauge transformation
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma\cdot g
this transforms tensorially, i.e.,
T^{\prime}=\rho(g^{-1})T
.
To again look at a familiar example, for the case of the frame bundle we have
\theta_{\mathcal{U}}=\theta_{\mu}\differential x^{\mu}=e^{A}_{\mu}\differential x^{\mu}
and
T^{A}_{\mu\nu}=2\partial_{[\mu}e_{\nu]}^{A}+2\omega^{A}{}_{[\mu B}e_{\nu]}^{B}
, where
\omega^{A}{}_{\mu B}
are the components of a
\mathfrak{gl}(d+1,\mathbb{R})
-valued one-form. The operational meaning of
\theta_{\mathcal{U}}
comes out especially simple here: It just gives the components of a vector field in the frame
E_{A}
. Since we can use the solder form to locally identify sections of
P\times_{\rho}V
with sections of
T\mathcal{M}
, a Koszul connection on the former bundle automatically induces an affine connection
\nabla
on the latter bundle, which is the usual object one works with in, e.g., general relativity. In a local chart it is given in terms of connection coefficients
\Gamma^{\alpha}{}_{\mu\nu}
that encode how the basis changes as one moves from one point to an infinitesimally close neighbouring point. For a coordinate basis
\{\partial_{\alpha}\}
they are defined by

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}=\Gamma^{\alpha}{}_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\alpha}

(68)

which automatically induces the action on the co-basis
\nabla_{\mu}\differential x^{\nu}=-\Gamma^{\nu}{}_{\mu\alpha}\differential x^{\alpha}
by using that
\nabla_{\mu}
reduces to an ordinary partial derivative once acting on scalar functions. In the following, we shall use the common notation
\nabla_{\mu}v^{\nu}
by which we mean the components of the covariant derivative of a vector field
v
(instead of the covariant derivative acting on the components, which would just reduce to a partial derivative). The connection
\mathcal{D}
determines
\nabla
by requiring that taking the derivative should commute with performing the map between
\Gamma(T\mathcal{M})
and
\Gamma(P\times_{\rho}V)
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{\nu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{\mu}\theta_{\nu}+\rho_{\ast}(\omega_{\mu})\theta_{\nu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{\nu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{\alpha}{}_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{\alpha}{}_{\mu\nu}\theta_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}

which is known as the
vielbein postulate
for the case of the frame bundle but actually holds also in more general situations. Choosing a basis for
V
such that
\rho_{\ast}(\omega_{\mu})\sim\omega^{A}{}_{\mu B}
and equating the right sides of the diagram yields

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}\theta_{\nu}^{A}+\omega^{A}{}_{\mu B}\,\theta_{\nu}^{B}-\Gamma^{\alpha}{}_{\mu\nu}\theta_{\alpha}^{A}=0

(69)

which determines the coefficients
\Gamma^{\alpha}{}_{\mu\nu}
uniquely. If the connection is defined in this way, it automatically satisfies a key property: It is adapted to any tensor field induced by a
G
–invariant tensor on
V
using the map provided by the solder form. To see this, we may first define an inverse to
\theta^{A}_{\mu}
by
\theta^{\nu}_{B}\theta^{B}_{\mu}=\delta^{\nu}_{\mu}
and find from contracting with (
69
) that

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}\theta_{A}^{\alpha}-\omega^{B}{}_{A\mu}\,\theta_{B}^{\alpha}+\Gamma^{\alpha}{}_{\mu\beta}\theta^{\beta}_{A}=0~.

(70)

Suppose we have a
G
-invariant tensor
\Psi\in\otimes^{r}_{s}V
. Infinitesimally, this means for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathfrak{g}

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{A_{1}}{}_{C}\Psi^{C...A_{r}}_{B_{1}...B_{s}}+\dots+\mathbf{x}^{A_{r}}{}_{C}\Psi^{A_{1}...C}_{B_{1}...B_{s}}-\mathbf{x}^{C}{}_{B_{1}}\Psi^{A_{1}...A_{r}}_{C...B_{s}}-\dots-\mathbf{x}^{C}{}_{B_{s}}\Psi^{A_{1}...A_{r}}_{B_{1}...C}=0

(71)

where
\mathbf{x}^{A}{}_{B}\in\mathfrak{gl}(V)
is again shorthand for the representation of
\mathbf{x}
on
V
. In particular, we may choose
\mathbf{x}^{A}{}_{B}=\omega^{A}{}_{\mu B}
. Then, to any such
\Psi
we associate a spacetime tensor

\displaystyle\Psi^{\mu_{1}...\mu_{r}}_{\nu_{1}...\nu_{s}}=\theta^{\mu_{1}}_{A_{1}}\dots\theta^{\mu_{r}}_{A_{r}}\theta^{B_{1}}_{\nu_{1}}\dots\theta^{B_{s}}_{\nu_{s}}\;\Psi^{A_{1}...A_{r}}_{B_{1}...B_{s}}

(72)

that can be checked to satisfy

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha}\Psi^{\mu_{1}...\mu_{r}}_{\nu_{1}...\nu_{s}}+\Gamma^{\mu_{1}}{}_{\alpha\beta}\Psi^{\beta...\mu_{r}}_{\nu_{1}...\nu_{s}}+\dots

\displaystyle+\Gamma^{\mu_{r}}{}_{\alpha\beta}\Psi^{\mu_{1}...\beta}_{\nu_{1}...\nu_{s}}

(73)

\displaystyle-\Gamma^{\beta}{}_{\alpha\nu_{1}}\Psi^{\mu_{1}...\mu_{r}}_{\beta...\nu_{s}}-\dots-\Gamma^{\beta}{}_{\alpha\nu_{s}}\Psi^{\mu_{1}...\mu_{r}}_{\nu_{1}...\beta}=0

as a consequence of (
69
) and (
70
). This is just the covariant derivative associated to the affine connection acting on
\Psi
such that we established the mentioned relation

\displaystyle\big(\rho(g)\Psi\big)^{A_{1}...A_{r}}_{B_{1}...B_{s}}=\Psi^{A_{1}...A_{r}}_{B_{1}...B_{s}}\quad\forall g\in G\quad\Rightarrow\quad\nabla_{\alpha}\Psi^{\mu_{1}...\mu_{r}}_{\nu_{1}...\nu_{s}}=0~.

(74)

To again have an example, take
V=\mathbb{R}^{d}
in the fundamental representation of an
SO(d)
bundle over a
d
-dimensional manifold. An invariant tensor is
\delta_{AB}\in V^{\ast}\odot V^{\ast}
which, given a solder form, maps to the Riemannian metric
g_{\mu\nu}=\theta_{\mu}^{A}\theta_{\nu}^{B}\delta_{AB}
. Any principal bundle connection then induces an affine connection that satisfies
\nabla_{\mu}g_{\alpha\beta}=0
. One may specify further requirements like vanishing torsion, which makes it possible to uniquely express the connection in terms of the metric, leading to the Levi–Cività connection.

Finally, let us see how to express curvature and torsion (
67
) of
\mathcal{D}
and
\theta
in terms of the more familiar curvature and torsion tensors of the affine connection. By a choice of basis for
V
we may write
T\sim\frac{1}{2}T^{A}_{\mu\nu}\differential x^{\mu}\wedge\differential x^{\nu}
and again use the map provided by the solder form at a point
p\in\mathcal{M}
to establish

\displaystyle T_{\mu\nu}^{A}=2\Gamma^{\alpha}{}_{[\mu\nu]}\theta_{\alpha}^{A}=:T^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}\theta^{A}_{\alpha}~,

(75)

where
T^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}
is the torsion tensor associated to
\nabla
. Meanwhile, the curvature two-form, represented locally by
\Omega\sim\frac{1}{2}\Omega^{A}{}_{B\mu\nu}\differential x^{\mu}\wedge\differential x^{\nu}
, may be rewritten as

\displaystyle\Omega^{A}{}_{B\mu\nu}=\theta^{A}_{\alpha}\theta_{B}^{\beta}R^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}

(76)

where
R^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}
is just the Riemann curvature tensor

\displaystyle R^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}=\partial_{\mu}\Gamma^{\alpha}{}_{\nu\beta}+\Gamma^{\sigma}{}_{\nu\beta}\Gamma^{\alpha}{}_{\mu\sigma}-(\mu\leftrightarrow\nu)~.

(77)

Torsion and curvature tensor are related by the identity

\displaystyle[\nabla_{\mu},\nabla_{\nu}]X^{\alpha}=R^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}X^{\beta}-T^{\beta}_{\mu\nu}\nabla_{\beta}X^{\alpha}

(78)

which holds for any vector field
X^{\alpha}
. The Bianchi identities (
51
) and (
66
) can now be translated to

\displaystyle\nabla_{[\rho}R^{\alpha}{}_{\beta|\mu\nu]}

\displaystyle=-R^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\sigma[\rho}T^{\sigma}_{\mu\nu]}

(79)

\displaystyle R^{\alpha}{}_{[\rho\mu\nu]}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{[\rho}T^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu]}-T^{\sigma}_{[\rho\mu}T^{\alpha}_{\nu]\sigma}

(80)

where we used that
R^{\alpha}{}_{\beta(\mu\nu)}=0
.

6.3
Cartan geometry

We are now ready to assemble the building blocks from the last two subsections. This leads back to works by Cartan
[
80
,
81
]
, however the mathematical language he used was different from the by now more common way of a formulation in terms of principal fiber bundles
[
214
,
233
,
124
]
. Here we shall choose this more modern approach. The idea is to construct a geometry that is locally built out of a Klein pair
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})
in such a way that the algebraic information about the symmetries in
H
is encoded in the tangent spaces. The manifold
\mathcal{M}
itself, however, does not have to be a coset space anymore. This is reflected in the fact that the
H
-invariant tensors discussed in subsection
6.1
now cannot be extended to invariant tensor fields on all of
\mathcal{M}
, since, in general, there is no
G
-action on
\mathcal{M}
anymore. Instead, they are only defined on each tangent space. A well-known example is the spacetime of general relativity which still has the structure of Minkowski space imprinted on its tangent spaces but in general does not possess any Killing vector fields. As we shall see, the deviation from the Klein geometry is measured by Cartan curvature which consists of the curvature of a principal bundle connection adapted to the chosen Klein pair as well as the torsion of an adapted solder form. This subsection is meant to provide the basic kinematical variables that may be used for constructing theories of Carroll gravity.

Definition 6.7

(Cartan geometry
[
214
]
)
.

A Cartan geometry
(P,A)
on
\mathcal{M}
modelled on a Klein pair
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})
with group
H
consists of the following data:

•

A smooth manifold
\mathcal{M}

•

A principal
H
-bundle
P\to\mathcal{M}

•

A
\mathfrak{g}
-valued one-form
A
on
P
called the Cartan connection satisfying the following conditions:

i)

at each
u\in P
the map
A_{u}:T_{u}P\to\mathfrak{g}
is an isomorphism

ii)

R_{h}^{\ast}A=\text{Ad}_{h^{-1}}\circ A
for all
h\in H

iii)

A(X^{\sharp})=\mathbf{x}
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathfrak{h}

In the reductive case that we restrict to, one can always decompose
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{m}
such that
\text{ad}_{\mathfrak{h}}:\mathfrak{m}\to\mathfrak{m}
. The Cartan connection
A
then splits into two parts,

\displaystyle A=\omega+\theta

(81)

where
\omega
takes values in
\mathfrak{h}
and
\theta
takes values in
\mathfrak{m}
. We define the Cartan curvature two-form by

\displaystyle F=\differential A+\frac{1}{2}[A\wedge A]=F(\mathfrak{m})+F(\mathfrak{h})

(82)

which also splits into two parts. One can immediately check that
\omega
satisfies all the requirements for a principal bundle connection on
P
while
\theta
is a solder form that attaches the associated bundle
(P\times_{\text{Ad}_{H}}\mathfrak{m})
to the tangent bundle. The reason this works is that
\mathfrak{m}
has exactly the right number of dimensions: Since
\dim P=\dim\mathcal{M}+\dim H
it follows from property
i)
above that
\dim\mathfrak{m}=\dim\mathcal{M}
. It is also horizontal by property
iii)
which implies
\theta(X^{\sharp})=0
. The Cartan curvature
F
also satisfies a Bianchi identity

\displaystyle\differential F+[A\wedge F]=0

(83)

which, once split into its components along
\mathfrak{h}
and
\mathfrak{m}
, implies the Bianchi identities (
51
), (
66
) for curvature and torsion. With
\rho_{\ast}
being the adjoint representation of
\mathfrak{h}
they read explicitly

\displaystyle\differential\Omega+[\omega\wedge\Omega]=0

\displaystyle\differential\Theta+[\omega\wedge\Theta]=[\Omega\wedge\theta]~.

(84)

One can see that from the perspective of the Klein pair, the “gauge transformations” of this model take values in the subgroup
H
, which for kinematical spacetimes consists of the homogeneous part like the Lorentz group in the case of the pair
(\mathfrak{iso}(d,1),\mathfrak{so}(d,1))
. Of course, as long as we do not specify a dynamical theory these are just kinematical redundancies of the description in terms of local quantities on the base manifold. The true interpretation as gauge symmetries only enters once we write down an action. Nevertheless, we shall assume from now on that we chose some trivializing section
\sigma:\mathcal{M}\supset\mathcal{U}\to P
such that
A
is given in terms of a
\mathfrak{g}
-valued one-form on
\mathcal{U}
. We can then express its components in a chart over
\mathcal{U}
as
A=A_{\mu}\differential x^{\mu}
. Following the discussion in subsection
6.2
a change of the trivializing section
\sigma\mapsto\sigma\cdot h
now infinitesimally induces a change in the local description of the Cartan connection that acts as

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}A=\differential\lambda+[A,\lambda]

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}F=[F,\lambda]

(85)

where
h\approx 1+\lambda
and
\lambda
is some
\mathfrak{h}
-valued function on
\mathcal{M}
. These are just the generalization of local Lorentz transformations to any other choice of
\mathfrak{h}
.

For theories of gravity, one might then wonder whether the action of diffeomorphisms on
\mathcal{M}
can be packaged into this framework. Once all the fields are expressed in terms of local quantities on
\mathcal{M}
their action is just given by the Lie derivative along some vector field
\xi
, i.e.,
\delta_{\xi}A_{\mu}=\mathcal{L}_{\xi}A_{\mu}
. Let us consider a more general transformation
[
155
,
156
]
than (
85
) given by

\displaystyle\bar{\delta}_{(\lambda,\xi)}A=\differential\Sigma+[A,\Sigma]+i_{\xi}F

\displaystyle\Sigma=\lambda+A_{\mu}\xi^{\mu}

(86)

where the parameter
\Sigma
now also includes contributions in
\mathfrak{m}
. For
\xi=0
this just reduces to (
85
). It can be shown that

\displaystyle\bar{\delta}_{(\lambda,\xi)}A=\delta_{\lambda}A+\mathcal{L}_{\xi}A~.

(87)

Therefore, one can always relate a combined version of
\mathfrak{m}
- and
\mathfrak{h}
-transformations to spacetime diffeomorphisms. It might happen for specific theories that going on-shell sets some of the curvature components to zero, in which case the
\delta
and
\bar{\delta}
transformations coincide for the respective components of the Cartan connection. This happens, e.g., in the Palatini formulation of Einstein gravity where
F(\mathfrak{m})=0
on-shell, corresponding to vanishing torsion. Another example is provided by BF-theories (see section
15.1
), where even all the curvatures vanish on-shell. It should be mentioned, however, that the invariance under
\bar{\delta}
transformations does, in general, not imply invariance under
\mathfrak{m}
-transformations alone.

Before we look at some examples let us state in which sense Cartan geometries generalize homogeneous spacetimes. This is the essence of the

Theorem 6.1

(Fundamental theorem of Cartan geometry (
[
214
]
, p.212))
.

Let
(P,A)
be a Cartan geometry on
\mathcal{M}
modelled on a Klein pair
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})
. The curvature
F=\differential A+\frac{1}{2}[A\wedge A]
vanishes if and only if the underlying principal
H
-bundle is locally isomorphic to
G\to G/H
with
A
given by the Maurer–Cartan form on
G
.

Therefore, in the case of vanishing Cartan curvature one indeed recovers a Klein geometry associated to the pair
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})
, at least locally. For more details we refer to
[
214
]
.

6.4
Example: Gauging the (A)dS algebra

Let us look at the example of the Poincaré algebra with non-zero cosmological constant to familiarize ourselves with the formalism. We choose the Klein pair as
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})=(\mathfrak{so}(d,2),\mathfrak{so}(d,1))
such that the Cartan gometry is given by an
SO(d,1)
bundle together with a Cartan connection

\displaystyle A=\frac{1}{2}\omega^{AB}J_{AB}+e^{A}P_{A}

(88)

where we denoted the (A)dS generators like in (
14
) by
J_{AB}
spanning
\mathfrak{h}
and
P_{A}
spanning
\mathfrak{m}
. We also changed the indices from spacetime indices to internal indices. They can be pulled with the internal Minkowski metric
\eta_{AB}
, an
\mathfrak{h}
-invariant tensor on
\mathfrak{m}
that will induce a Lorentzian metric on
\mathcal{M}
.
Note that
\omega^{AB}
is already in the adjoint representation of
\mathfrak{h}
on
\mathfrak{m}
, i.e., for any vector
v=v^{A}P_{A}
it acts as
\rho_{\ast}(\omega)v=\omega^{A}{}_{B}v^{B}P_{A}
. Like introduced in subsection
6.2
, we denote its associated curvature two-form by
\Omega^{A}{}_{B}:=(\rho_{\ast}(\Omega))^{A}{}_{B}
. Infinitesimal
\mathfrak{h}
-transformations
\lambda=\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{AB}J_{AB}
act on the Cartan connection components as

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}\omega^{AB}

\displaystyle=\differential\lambda^{AB}+\lambda^{A}{}_{C}\omega^{CB}-\lambda^{B}{}_{C}\omega^{AC}

(89)

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}e^{A}

\displaystyle=-\lambda^{A}{}_{B}e^{B}

(90)

and we can work out the components of the Cartan curvature two-forms to be

\displaystyle F^{AB}(J)

\displaystyle=\differential\omega^{AB}+\omega^{A}{}_{C}\wedge\omega^{CB}-\frac{\sigma}{\ell^{2}}e^{A}\wedge e^{B}

(91)

\displaystyle F^{A}(P)

\displaystyle=\differential e^{A}+\omega^{A}{}_{B}\wedge e^{B}~.

(92)

They relate to curvature and torsion of
\omega^{AB}
and
e^{A}
as

\displaystyle F^{AB}(J)=\Omega^{AB}-\frac{\sigma}{\ell^{2}}e^{A}\wedge e^{B}

\displaystyle F^{A}(P)=T^{A}~.

(93)

Therefore, the
\mathfrak{h}
-component of the Cartan curvature gives a different result than the curvature of the
\mathfrak{h}
-component of the Cartan connection precisely when the translations
P_{A}
do not commute. The case
\Lambda=0
is special in this regard. We can now use the solder form provided by
e^{A}
to induce a connection on the tangent bundle. Solving the vielbein postulate

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}e^{A}_{\nu}+\omega^{A}{}_{\mu B}e^{B}_{\nu}-\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}e_{\alpha}^{A}=0\quad\Rightarrow\quad\Gamma^{\alpha}{}_{\mu\nu}=E^{\alpha}_{A}\big(\delta^{A}_{B}\partial_{\mu}+\omega^{A}{}_{\mu B}\big)e^{B}_{\nu}

(94)

the covariant derivative
\nabla
is automatically adapted to the spacetime metric
g_{\mu\nu}=\eta_{AB}e^{A}_{\mu}e^{B}_{\nu}
,

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mu}g_{\alpha\beta}=0~.

(95)

Often, one requires some additional properties from this connection. One example is the Einstein–Palatini action, where the equations of motion from varying with respect to
\omega^{AB}
yield a constraint of vanishing torsion,
T^{A}=0
. In that case, one can solve the equation

\displaystyle\differential e^{A}+\omega^{A}{}_{B}\wedge e^{B}=0

(96)

for the connection to find

\displaystyle\omega^{AB}_{\mu}=2E^{\nu[A}\partial_{[\mu}e_{\nu]}^{B]}-e_{\mu C}E^{\nu A}E^{\rho B}\partial_{[\nu}e_{\rho]}^{C}~.

(97)

When inserted into the vielbein postulate, one arrives at the Levi–Cività connection

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\alpha}{}_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}g^{\alpha\rho}\big(\partial_{\mu}g_{\nu\rho}+\partial_{\nu}g_{\mu\rho}-\partial_{\rho}g_{\mu\nu}\big)

(98)

which is the unique torsionfree and metric-compatible connection on a Lorentzian manifold. It is manifestly invariant under
\mathfrak{h}
-transformations. For the special case
F^{AB}(J)=0
the geometry is locally isomorphic to the Klein geometry given by (A)dS spacetime and the Riemann tensor associated to
\nabla
is

\displaystyle R_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}=\frac{\sigma}{\ell^{2}}(g_{\mu\alpha}g_{\nu\beta}-g_{\mu\beta}g_{\nu\alpha})

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle R=\Lambda\frac{2(d+1)}{d-1}

(99)

where we used (
17
) and defined the Ricci scalar
R:=R^{\mu}{}_{\nu\mu\beta}g^{\nu\beta}
.

7
Geometric anatomy of Carroll spacetimes

Let us now come to our main application of the Cartan formalism introduced in the previous subsection and set up the geometric variables used for describing general curved Carroll spacetimes. We shall directly start with the case for
\Lambda\neq 0
, since one can always revert to the flat case by setting the cosmological constant to zero. Aiming to connect with the variables introduced in subsection
5
, we pick a Cartan connection

\displaystyle A=\tau H+e^{a}P_{a}+\omega^{a}B_{a}+\frac{1}{2}\omega^{ab}J_{ab}

(100)

which is a one-form with values in the algebra (
18
). Note, that we again use abstract algebra indices instead of spacetime indices, since these generators do not have a spacetime interpretation for general manifolds. The Klein pair we choose is the (A)dS Carroll algebra (
18
) together with the homogeneous Carroll subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{iso}(d)
with basis
\{B_{a},J_{ab}\}
. Its complement is denoted by
\mathfrak{m}=\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
and has a basis
\{H,P_{a}\}
. Comparing with section
5
, we think of
\tau
as the clock one-form and of
e^{a}
as the spatial coframe, both taken together form the components of the solder form pulled back to the base manifold,

\displaystyle\theta=\tau H+e^{a}P_{a}=\theta^{A}P_{A}~.

(101)

Capital Latin indices again label the basis elements of
\mathfrak{m}
as
P_{A}=(H,P_{a})
. According to the analysis in example
6.3
, two
\mathfrak{h}
-invariant tensors on
\mathfrak{m}
are given by a degenerate metric
h_{AB}
as well as a vector
v^{A}
with components

\displaystyle h_{AB}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&\delta_{ab}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle v^{A}=(-1,0_{a})^{T}

(102)

where we chose a conventional minus sign in
v
. Clearly, we now cannot use this metric to identify
\mathfrak{m}^{\ast}
with
\mathfrak{m}
as a whole, but we can still pull indices of purely spatial objects. To derive the transformation properties of the various fields we parametrize
\mathfrak{h}
-transformations by

\displaystyle\lambda=\lambda^{a}B_{a}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{ab}J_{ab}

\displaystyle\lambda^{A}{}_{B}=(\text{ad}_{\lambda})^{A}{}_{B}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\lambda_{b}\\
0&\lambda^{a}{}_{b}\end{pmatrix}

(103)

and additionally write the connection as well as its curvature in the adjoint
\mathfrak{h}
-representation,

\displaystyle\omega^{A}{}_{B}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\omega_{b}\\
0&\omega^{a}{}_{b}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\Omega^{A}{}_{B}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\Omega_{b}\\
0&\Omega^{a}{}_{b}\end{pmatrix}~.

(104)

The action on the various gauge fields is then

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}\omega^{A}{}_{B}=\differential\lambda^{A}{}_{B}+\omega^{A}{}_{C}\lambda^{C}{}_{B}-\lambda^{A}{}_{C}\omega^{C}{}_{B}

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}\theta^{A}=-\lambda^{A}{}_{B}\theta^{B}

(105)

which reads in components

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}\omega_{\mu}^{a}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}\lambda^{a}+\omega_{\mu}^{ab}\lambda_{b}-\lambda^{a}{}_{b}\omega^{b}_{\mu}

(106a)

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}\omega_{\mu}^{ab}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}\lambda^{ab}+2\omega_{\mu}^{c[a}\lambda^{b]}{}_{c}

(106b)

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}e^{a}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=-\lambda^{a}{}_{b}e_{\mu}^{b}

(106c)

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}\tau_{\mu}

\displaystyle=-e_{\mu}^{a}\lambda_{a}~.

(106d)

In the last line one can already recognize the transformation of
\tau_{\mu}
matching with the ambiguity (
27
) if one identifies
\lambda_{\mu}=e^{a}_{\mu}\lambda_{a}
. As described at the end of subsection
6.2
, the solder form
\theta
relates the invariant tensors on
\mathfrak{m}
to tensor fields on
\mathcal{M}
,

\displaystyle h_{\mu\nu}=\theta^{A}_{\mu}\theta^{B}_{\nu}h_{AB}

\displaystyle v^{\mu}=\theta^{\mu}_{A}v^{A}

(107)

where the inverse solder form is defined by

\displaystyle\theta_{A}^{\mu}=(-v^{\mu},e_{a}^{\mu})

\displaystyle\theta^{A}_{\mu}\theta_{A}^{\nu}=\delta_{\mu}^{\nu}~.

(108)

This corresponds to the dual components of the frame being defined by the relations

\displaystyle v^{\mu}\tau_{\mu}=-1

\displaystyle e^{\mu}_{a}e^{b}_{\mu}=\delta^{b}_{a}

\displaystyle v^{\mu}e_{\mu}^{a}=0

\displaystyle\tau_{\mu}e^{\mu}_{a}=0

(109)

in accordance with subsection
5
. The inverse solder form then transforms as

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}\theta_{A}^{\mu}=\lambda^{B}{}_{A}\theta_{B}^{\mu}~.

(110)

Now that we set up the field content together with its transformation behaviour, we are ready to compute the various notions of curvature, analogously to the example
6.4
. The Cartan curvature is
\mathfrak{g}
-valued and thus expands as

\displaystyle F=F(H)H+F^{a}(P)P_{a}+F^{a}(B)B_{a}+\frac{1}{2}F^{ab}J_{ab}

(111)

with

\displaystyle F(H)

\displaystyle=\differential\tau+\omega^{a}\wedge e_{a}

(112a)

\displaystyle F^{a}(P)

\displaystyle=\differential e^{a}+\omega^{a}{}_{b}\wedge e^{b}

(112b)

\displaystyle F^{a}(B)

\displaystyle=\differential\omega^{a}+\omega^{a}{}_{b}\wedge\omega^{b}-\frac{\sigma}{\ell^{2}}\tau\wedge e^{a}

(112c)

\displaystyle F^{ab}(J)

\displaystyle=\differential\omega^{ab}+\omega^{a}{}_{c}\wedge\omega^{cb}-\frac{\sigma}{\ell^{2}}e^{a}\wedge e^{b}~.

(112d)

It should be noted that there is again a difference to the curvatures of
\omega^{A}{}_{B}
,

\displaystyle F(H)=T

\displaystyle F^{a}(P)=T^{a}

\displaystyle F^{a}(B)=\Omega^{a}-\frac{\sigma}{\ell^{2}}\tau\wedge e^{a}

\displaystyle F^{ab}(J)=\Omega^{ab}-\frac{\sigma}{\ell^{2}}e^{a}\wedge e^{b}

(113)

which only vanishes for
\Lambda=0
. Under
\mathfrak{h}
-transformations the curvatures transform as dictated by (
85
)

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}\Omega^{a}

\displaystyle=\Omega^{ab}\lambda_{b}-\lambda^{a}{}_{b}\Omega^{b}

(114a)

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}\Omega^{ab}

\displaystyle=\Omega^{ac}\lambda_{c}{}^{b}-\lambda^{a}{}_{c}\Omega^{cb}

(114b)

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}T^{a}

\displaystyle=-T^{b}\lambda^{a}{}_{b}

(114c)

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}T

\displaystyle=-T^{a}\lambda_{a}

(114d)

or, equivalently with
T^{A}=(T,T^{a})
,

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}\Omega^{A}{}_{B}

\displaystyle=\Omega^{A}{}_{C}\lambda^{C}{}_{B}-\lambda^{A}{}_{C}\Omega^{C}{}_{B}

(115)

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}T^{A}

\displaystyle=-\lambda^{A}{}_{B}T^{B}~.

(116)

Finally, the Bianchi identities (
51
), (
66
) read

\displaystyle\differential\Omega^{a}+\omega^{a}{}_{b}\wedge\Omega^{b}-\Omega^{a}{}_{b}\wedge\omega^{b}

\displaystyle=0

(117a)

\displaystyle\differential\Omega^{ab}+\omega^{a}{}_{c}\wedge\Omega^{cb}-\Omega^{ac}\wedge\omega_{c}{}^{b}

\displaystyle=0

(117b)

\displaystyle\differential T+\omega^{a}\wedge T_{a}-\Omega^{a}\wedge e_{a}

\displaystyle=0

(117c)

\displaystyle\differential T^{a}+\omega^{a}{}_{b}\wedge T^{b}-\Omega^{a}{}_{b}\wedge e^{b}

\displaystyle=0~.

(117d)

7.1
Intrinsic torsion

Often, further constraints are imposed on the geometric data that describe a curved Carroll spacetime. This might be done by hand or by the equations of motion resulting from an action principle and typically has the aim of making some gauge field components depend on others. The prime example is to impose some relations on the torsion components which is realized dynamically in the Einstein–Cartan formulation of general relativity. In that case, varying the Einstein–Cartan action with respect to the spin connection requires the torsion to vanish and allows to uniquely solve the spin connection in terms of the frame variables (see, e.g.,
[
127
]
). The action then reduces to the usual Einstein–Hilbert action.

It might, however, happen that imposing vanishing torsion is a stronger condition than needed for making some connection components depend on others. For the Carroll geometries just discussed we have

\displaystyle T=\differential\tau+\omega^{a}\wedge e_{a}

\displaystyle T^{a}=\differential e^{a}+\omega^{a}{}_{b}\wedge e^{b}

(118)

and one finds that the part

\displaystyle T^{(a}(v,e^{b)})=2v^{\mu}e^{\nu(a}\partial_{[\mu}e_{\nu]}^{b)}=-e^{\mu a}e^{\nu b}K_{\mu\nu}=:-K^{ab}

(119)

does not depend on
\omega^{a}
or
\omega^{a}{}_{b}
. Therefore, this part of the torsion is a property of the metric data itself and imposing its vanishing would be too strong a constraint if one was just interested in integrating out the connection components
\omega^{a}
and
\omega^{ab}
. In the last equality of (
119
) we introduced the extrinsic curvature

\displaystyle K_{\mu\nu}=-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{L}_{v}h_{\mu\nu}

(120)

which is an
\mathfrak{h}
-invariant and connection independent quantity. The part of the torsion that is controlled by
K_{\mu\nu}
is referred to as
intrinsic torsion
and does not have a Lorentzian analogue. It provides a means to further classify Carroll geometries into four categories
[
124
]
:

i)

K_{\mu\nu}=0
(
v
is
h
- Killing)

ii)

h^{\mu\nu}K_{\mu\nu}=0\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\mathcal{L}_{v}\,\mathfrak{e}=0
(
v
is volume form preserving)

iii)

K_{\mu\nu}=fh_{\mu\nu}
,
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
(
v
is
h
- conformal Killing)

iv)

general
K_{\mu\nu}
.

Referring to the above article for further details on this classification, we now want to see how much of the spin connection can be fixed without constraining the intrinsic part of the torsion. In other words, we choose to reduce the torsion to a “minimal” amount that cannot be changed further by restricting the connection. This amounts to solving the constraints

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle T^{[a}(v,e^{b]})

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle T^{a}(e^{b},e^{c})

\displaystyle=0

(121)

and leaving the components (
119
) untouched. The general solution is
[
61
]

\displaystyle\omega^{a}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=\tau_{\mu}v^{\nu}e^{a\sigma}\partial_{[\nu}\tau_{\sigma]}-e^{\nu a}\partial_{[\mu}\tau_{\nu]}+C^{ab}e_{\mu b}

(122)

\displaystyle\omega_{\mu}^{ab}

\displaystyle=2e^{\rho[a}\partial_{[\mu}e_{\rho]}^{b]}-e_{\mu c}e^{\rho a}e^{\nu b}\partial_{[\rho}e_{\nu]}^{c}

(123)

where
C^{ab}
is an arbitrary transverse symmetric tensor corresponding to the components
\omega^{(a}_{\mu}e^{b)\mu}
. This is in clear contrast to general relativity, where all the components can be determined uniquely.

There is one point to keep in mind when setting only the connection-dependent part of the torsion to zero. Looking again at (
121
) together with the transformation behaviour of the torsion components (
114c
), (
114d
) one can see that these constraints are not Carroll invariant. This already follows from
\delta_{\lambda}T\neq 0
when evaluated on the constraints and is also reflected in the transformation behaviour of the solution (
122
), (
123
)

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}\omega_{\mu}^{a}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}\lambda^{a}+\omega_{\mu}^{ab}\lambda_{b}-\lambda^{a}{}_{b}\omega^{b}_{\mu}+\tau_{\mu}\delta^{ab}K_{bc}\lambda^{c}

(124)

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}\omega_{\mu}^{ab}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}\lambda^{ab}+2\omega_{\mu}^{c[a}\lambda^{b]}{}_{c}-2\lambda^{[a}\delta^{b]c}K_{cd}e_{\mu}^{d}~.

(125)

Here, we used that the transformation behaviour of
C^{ab}
is dictated by the relation
C^{ab}=\omega^{(a}_{\mu}e^{b)\mu}
and reads

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}C^{ab}=-\lambda^{a}{}_{c}C^{cb}-\lambda^{b}{}_{c}C^{ca}+\partial_{\mu}\lambda^{(a}e^{b)\mu}+2v^{\mu}\lambda^{(a}e^{b)\nu}\partial_{[\mu}\tau_{\nu]}-2e^{\mu(a}\partial_{[\mu}e_{\nu]}^{b)}e^{\nu}_{c}\lambda^{c}~.

(126)

This differs from (
106
) by
K
- and
\lambda^{a}
-dependent terms and therefore makes this truncation seem inconsistent with Carroll boost invariance. However, we shall see in section
14.2
that magnetic Carroll gravity features a constraint that precisely sets the intrinsic torsion to zero. The additional terms in the transformation behaviour of the connection are then trivial in the sense that they vanish on-shell
[
163
]
. In that scenario the field
C^{ab}
acts as a Lagrange multiplier.

7.2
Affine connection

Following the construction of subsection
6.2
we introduce a connection on the tangent bundle by imposing the vielbein postulate

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}\tau_{\nu}+\omega^{a}_{\mu}e_{a\nu}-\Gamma^{\alpha}{}_{\mu\nu}\tau_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=0

(127)

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}e^{a}_{\mu}+\omega^{ab}_{\mu}e_{b\nu}-\Gamma^{\alpha}{}_{\mu\nu}e^{a}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=0

(128)

which can be solved for
\Gamma^{\alpha}{}_{\mu\nu}
by

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\alpha}{}_{\mu\nu}=-v^{\alpha}\big(\partial_{\mu}\tau_{\nu}+\omega^{a}_{\mu}e_{\nu a}\big)+e^{\alpha}_{a}\big(\delta^{a}_{b}\partial_{\mu}+\omega^{a}_{\mu}{}_{b}\big)e^{b}_{\nu}~.

(129)

This leads to an associated covariant derivative that is automatically compatible with the Carroll structure,

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mu}h_{\alpha\beta}=0

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mu}v^{\nu}=0

(130)

but in general has non-vanishing torsion. The associated Riemann curvature tensor (
77
) can be written as

\displaystyle R^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}=-v^{\alpha}e_{b\beta}\,\Omega^{b}_{\mu\nu}+e^{\alpha}_{a}e^{b}_{\beta}\,\Omega^{a}{}_{b\mu\nu}

(131)

and satisfies a number of identities as a consequence of the compatibility conditions (
130
),

\displaystyle R^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}v^{\beta}

\displaystyle=0

(132)

\displaystyle h_{\alpha(\rho}R^{\alpha}{}_{\beta)\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=0

(133)

\displaystyle R^{\alpha}{}_{\alpha\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=0~.

(134)

The first of these also implies that the Ricci tensor
R_{\mu\nu}=R^{\beta}{}_{\mu\beta\nu}
obeys

\displaystyle R_{\mu\nu}v^{\mu}=0

(135)

but in general is not symmetric if the torsion is non-zero as a consequence of the Bianchi identity (
80
). Since
\Gamma^{\alpha}{}_{\mu\nu}
is manifestly
\mathfrak{h}
-invariant at this point, we conclude that also
R^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}
as well as
R_{\mu\nu}
are. However, one needs to keep in mind that these quantities still depend on the connection coefficients
\omega^{a}
and
\omega^{ab}
. Finally, to write down gravitational actions we need a curvature scalar. Its construction is not obvious since, e.g., a term like
e^{a\mu}e_{a}^{\nu}R_{\mu\nu}
cannot be
\mathfrak{h}
-invariant
as one can check by using (
110
). Instead, one finds
\delta_{\lambda}(e^{a\mu}e_{a}^{\nu}R_{\mu\nu})=-e^{\mu a}v^{\nu}\lambda_{a}R_{\mu\nu}\neq 0
, which only vanishes if torsion is restricted to zero. We shall come back to this below. There are, however, several other
\mathfrak{h}
-invariant terms that can be built according to the classification in
[
120
]
. The most important one for this work is

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}=-2v^{\mu}e_{b}^{\nu}\Omega^{b}_{\mu\nu}+e^{\mu}_{a}e^{b\nu}\Omega^{a}{}_{b}{}_{\mu\nu}=h^{\mu\nu}\big(R_{\mu\nu}-v^{\rho}\tau_{\sigma}R^{\sigma}{}_{\mu\rho\nu}\big)

(136)

which is also obtained from a
c\to 0
limit of the Einstein–Cartan action
[
61
,
77
]
.

Let us now restrict to the minimal torsion case from subsection
7.1
. Inserting (
122
), (
123
) one finds after some manipulations

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\alpha}{}_{\mu\nu}=

\displaystyle-v^{\alpha}\partial_{(\mu}\tau_{\nu)}-v^{\alpha}\tau_{(\mu}\mathcal{L}_{v}\tau_{\nu)}-v^{\alpha}C_{\mu\nu}

(137)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}h^{\rho\alpha}\big(\partial_{\mu}h_{\rho\nu}+\partial_{\nu}h_{\rho\mu}-\partial_{\rho}h_{\mu\nu}\big)-h^{\alpha\rho}K_{\rho\mu}\tau_{\nu}

whose torsion indeed only depends on
K_{\mu\nu}
,

\displaystyle T^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}=2\Gamma^{\alpha}{}_{[\mu\nu]}=-2h^{\alpha\rho}K_{\rho[\mu}\tau_{\nu]}~.

(138)

Here, we also defined
C_{\mu\nu}=e_{a\mu}e_{b\nu}C^{ab}
. Carroll boost invariance of
\Gamma^{\alpha}{}_{\mu\nu}
is broken as a consequence of the constraints (
121
). One finds instead

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}\Gamma^{\alpha}{}_{\mu\nu}=-\tau_{\mu}K_{\nu\sigma}\lambda^{\sigma}v^{\alpha}-2h^{\alpha\sigma}K_{\mu[\nu}\lambda_{\sigma]}

(139)

and the transformation of
C_{\mu\nu}
under
\mathfrak{h}
follows from (
126
),

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}C_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\partial_{(\mu}\lambda_{\nu)}+\lambda_{(\mu}a_{\nu)}+v^{\sigma}\big(\tau_{(\mu}\partial_{\sigma}\lambda_{\nu)}-\tau_{(\mu}\partial_{\nu)}\lambda_{\sigma}\big)

(140)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{\sigma}\big(\partial_{\mu}h_{\nu\sigma}+\partial_{\nu}h_{\mu\sigma}-\partial_{\sigma}h_{\mu\nu}\big)+2\lambda^{\sigma}K_{\sigma(\mu}\tau_{\nu)}~.

Since
C_{\mu\nu}v^{\mu}=0
one can see that Carroll boost invariance of
\Gamma^{\alpha}{}_{\mu\nu}
cannot be repaired by altering the transformation behaviour of
C_{\mu\nu}
since this would require adding non-transverse terms. In general, a Carroll compatible affine connection with its torsion only given by the intrinsic part cannot be made
\mathfrak{h}
-invariant when just realized on the fields
\tau_{\mu}
,
h_{\mu\nu}
and
C_{\mu\nu}
. Only once the intrinsic torsion is set to zero
\mathfrak{h}
-invariance is recovered.
6
6

6

When realizing the
\mathfrak{h}
transformations on a different set of fields this is not true any more
[
156
]
.
Nevertheless, one can show that the curvature scalar (
136
) evaluated for this connection is invariant up to a total derivative,

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}\mathcal{R}[\tau_{\mu},h_{\mu\nu},C_{\mu\nu}]=-\frac{2}{\mathfrak{e}}\partial_{\mu}(\mathfrak{e}\lambda^{\mu}K)

(141)

which will be the fundamental building block for magnetic Carroll gravity (
14.2
). For showing this it is important to use that total divergences of
\nabla
associated to (
137
) read

\displaystyle\mathfrak{e}\nabla_{\mu}X^{\mu}=\partial_{\mu}(\mathfrak{e}X^{\mu})-\mathfrak{e}K\tau_{\mu}X^{\mu}

(142)

where
\partial_{\mu}\log\mathfrak{e}=\Gamma^{\nu}{}_{\nu\mu}+K\tau_{\mu}
and
K:=h^{\alpha\beta}K_{\alpha\beta}
. The Ricci tensor is still not symmetric, by contracting the Bianchi identity (
80
) one finds

\displaystyle R_{[\mu\nu]}=\nabla_{[\mu}(\tau_{\nu]}K)+\nabla_{\rho}(\tau_{[\mu}K^{\rho}{}_{\nu]})~.

(143)

7.3
Maximally symmetric spacetimes

The case
F=0
in (
111
) corresponds to Cartan geometries that are locally isometric to the corresponding Klein geometries, i.e., (A)dS Carroll spacetimes in this case. Written in the geometric language of the tangent bundle introduced in the previous subsection, where curvature and torsion are given in terms of the affine connection
\Gamma^{\alpha}{}_{\mu\nu}
together with the Carroll metric variables this leads to the equations

\displaystyle R^{\alpha}{}_{\beta\mu\nu}=\frac{\sigma}{\ell^{2}}\big(\delta^{\alpha}_{\mu}h_{\nu\beta}-\delta^{\alpha}_{\nu}h_{\mu\beta}\big)\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad h^{\mu\nu}R_{\mu\nu}=\Lambda\frac{2(d+1)}{d-1}

(144)

as well as

\displaystyle T^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}=2\Gamma^{\alpha}{}_{[\mu\nu]}=0~.

(145)

Here, we again used the identification (
17
) to rewrite the curvature scalar in terms of
\Lambda
. Clearly, there can be no intrinsic torsion in this case, implying that
h^{\mu\nu}R_{\mu\nu}
is a Carroll boost invariant quantity and that
R_{[\mu\nu]}=0
. The general solution of (
145
) is just the corresponding restriction of (
137
)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\alpha}{}_{\mu\nu}=

\displaystyle-v^{\alpha}\partial_{(\mu}\tau_{\nu)}-v^{\alpha}\tau_{(\mu}\mathcal{L}_{v}\tau_{\nu)}-v^{\alpha}C_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{2}h^{\rho\alpha}\big(\partial_{\mu}h_{\rho\nu}+\partial_{\nu}h_{\rho\mu}-\partial_{\rho}h_{\mu\nu}\big)

(146)

where the ambiguity associated to
C^{ab}
in (
122
) is still present. However, since
K_{\mu\nu}=0
it follows that the affine connection is boost-invariant, cf. (
139
). The compatible transformation behaviour of
C_{\mu\nu}
follows from (
140
),

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}C_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\partial_{(\mu}\lambda_{\nu)}+\lambda_{(\mu}a_{\nu)}+v^{\alpha}\big(\tau_{(\nu}\partial_{\alpha}\lambda_{\mu)}-\tau_{(\nu}\partial_{\mu)}\lambda_{\alpha}\big)

(147)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2}h^{\lambda\sigma}\lambda_{\sigma}\big(\partial_{\mu}h_{\nu\lambda}+\partial_{\nu}h_{\mu\lambda}-\partial_{\lambda}h_{\mu\nu}\big)~.

This shows that a curved Carroll spacetime with a torsion free compatible connection is not just described by the Carroll metric variables but also by the tensor
C_{\mu\nu}
that enters the connection. For obtaining the locally homogeneous spacetime we still need to solve the equations (
144
) that typically restrict
C_{\mu\nu}
further along with the metric variables.

The spacetime symmetries of the resulting geometries manifest themselves in a Carroll analogue of the Killing equations. Since part of the affine connection plays an independent role in the Carroll case they are given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\xi}v^{\mu}=0

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\xi}h_{\mu\nu}=0

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\nabla)_{\mu}=0

(148)

where the last condition expresses the invariance of the affine connection
[
191
,
54
]
and reads explicitly
7
7

7

This condition can be shown to be equivalent to the concept of Cartan symmetries defined by
\mathcal{L}_{\xi}A+\delta_{\lambda(\xi)}A=0
. It states that the Cartan connection is left invariant up to a local
\mathfrak{h}
-transformation.

\displaystyle\xi^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\Gamma^{\sigma}{}_{\mu\nu}+\Gamma^{\sigma}{}_{\alpha\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi^{\alpha}+\Gamma^{\sigma}{}_{\mu\alpha}\partial_{\nu}\xi^{\alpha}-\Gamma^{\alpha}{}_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\alpha}\xi^{\sigma}+\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\xi^{\sigma}=0~.

(149)

Solving these equations one finds Carroll Killing fields that close into
\mathfrak{g}
under the standard Lie bracket for vector fields, which in the present case is just the (A)dS Carroll algebra (
18
). Sometimes the last condition is dropped, in which case one obtains an infinite-dimensional lift of this algebra. Then, any vector of the form
\xi^{\mu}=f(x^{\nu})v^{\mu}
with
f
being an arbitrary function satisfying
v^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}f=0
solves the first two equations. Using the terminology of
[
4
]
the corresponding algebra is referred to as
\mathfrak{isocarr}_{\Lambda}(d+1)
and given by

\displaystyle[P_{a},J_{bc}]=2\delta_{a[b}P_{c]}\qquad\qquad[P_{a},P_{b}]=-\frac{\sigma}{\ell^{2}}J_{ab}\qquad\qquad[J_{ab},J_{cd}]=4\delta_{[a[c}J_{d]b]}

(150a)

\displaystyle[P_{a},M_{f}]=M_{f_{a}}\qquad\qquad[M_{f},J_{ab}]=M_{f_{ab}}

(150b)

where the functions
f_{a}
,
f_{ab}
again are annihilated by
v^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}
but otherwise depend on the specific coordinates chosen to compute the Carroll Killing vectors.

Let us illustrate this with an example in
d=2
and choose
\sigma=-1
. Consider a one-parameter family of solutions to Eqs. (
144
) and (
145
) given by

\displaystyle v=-\frac{1}{\rho(r)}\partial_{t}

\displaystyle\tau=\rho(r)\differential t

\displaystyle\differential s^{2}=\frac{\differential r^{2}}{\rho^{2}(r)}+r^{2}\differential\phi^{2}

(151a)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{t}{}_{tr}=\frac{r}{\ell^{2}\rho^{2}(r)}

\displaystyle\Gamma^{t}{}_{rr}=\frac{\gamma}{\rho^{3}(r)}

\displaystyle\Gamma^{t}{}_{\phi\phi}=\frac{\gamma\,r^{2}}{\rho(r)}

(151b)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{r}{}_{rr}=-\frac{r}{\ell^{2}\rho^{2}(r)}

\displaystyle\Gamma^{r}{}_{\phi\phi}=-r\rho^{2}(r)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\phi}{}_{r\phi}=\frac{1}{r}

(151c)

where
\rho^{2}(r)=1+\frac{r^{2}}{\ell^{2}}
and
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
. By definition, these geometries locally describe AdSC
3
spacetime but include different choices of connections parametrized by
\gamma
. The case
\gamma=0
corresponds to the naive
c\to 0
limit of AdS
3
spacetime in static coordinates. We solve the first two equations in (
148
) and obtain the vector fields

\displaystyle M_{f}

\displaystyle=f(r,\phi)\partial_{t}

\displaystyle P_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{rt}{\ell^{2}\rho(r)}\cos\phi\,\partial_{t}-\rho(r)\cos\phi\,\partial_{r}+\frac{\rho(r)}{r}\sin\phi\,\partial_{\phi}

(152a)

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle=\partial_{\phi}

\displaystyle P_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{rt}{\ell^{2}\rho(r)}\sin\phi\,\partial_{t}-\rho(r)\sin\phi\,\partial_{r}-\frac{\rho(r)}{r}\cos\phi\,\partial_{\phi}

(152b)

where
f(r,\phi)
is an arbitrary function. They close into the three-dimensional version of (
150
) where
M_{f}
represents a whole tower of supertranslations.

Solving the last condition (
149
) restricts this tower to the independent solutions

\displaystyle H=\partial_{t}

\displaystyle B_{1}=\frac{r}{\ell f(r)}\cos\phi\,\partial_{t}

\displaystyle B_{2}=\frac{r}{\ell f(r)}\sin\phi\,\partial_{t}

(153)

and makes the Carroll Killing fields form the AdS Carroll algebra (
18
) in
d=2
with
J_{ab}=J\epsilon_{ab}
. In this Carroll boost frame the tensor
C_{\mu\nu}
is given by
C_{\mu\nu}=\gamma h_{\mu\nu}
but any boost of the form

\displaystyle\lambda_{t}=0

\displaystyle\lambda_{r}=\delta\frac{r}{\ell^{2}\,\rho^{2}(r)}

\displaystyle\lambda_{\phi}=0

(154)

with
\delta\in\mathbb{R}
can be used to change
C_{\mu\nu}\to C_{\mu\nu}+\delta h_{\mu\nu}
. In particular, there is a boost frame where
C_{\mu\nu}=0
and all the fields in (
151
) are unchanged except

\displaystyle\tau=\rho(r)\differential t+\gamma\frac{r}{\ell^{2}\rho^{2}(r)}\differential r~.

(155)

8
Conformal extensions of the Carroll algebra

Before we switch gears and tackle Carroll field theories, we discuss conformal extensions of the Carroll algebra since after all these are the relevant symmetries in a holographic context. The reason is that the null boundary of asymptotically flat spacetimes only carries an equivalence class of Carroll metrics and vector fields
[
18
]
with two members
(h_{\mu\nu},v^{\mu})
,
(h^{\prime}_{\mu\nu},v{}^{\prime\mu})
being identified by

\displaystyle(h^{\prime}_{\mu\nu},v{}^{\prime\mu})\sim(\eta^{2}h_{\mu\nu},\eta^{-z}v^{\mu})\qquad\qquad z=1~.

(156)

Here,
\eta
is an arbitrary function of the coordinates and
z
is called the dynamical or anisotropy exponent. While
z=1
in special cases it may be an arbitrary real number in generic situations. The diffeomorphisms that preserve this structure are generated by vector fields
\xi
with

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\xi}h_{\mu\nu}=2\rho h_{\mu\nu}\qquad\qquad\mathcal{L}_{\xi}v^{\mu}=-z\rho v^{\mu}

(157)

for some function
\rho(\xi)
. They form an infinite lift of the conformal Carroll group which is just a different name for the BMS group, the group of asymptotic symmetries at null infinity
[
109
,
66
,
210
]
. While one may also consider further generalizations of (
156
) which lead to an even larger symmetry group (for a summary see, e.g., appendix E of
[
84
]
), we shall only stick to this simplest case here. However, we keep the dimension of the Carroll manifold
d+1
general for most of the discussion and only look at special cases in the end.

Following
[
4
]
, it is possible to extend the Carroll algebra (
13
) to a slightly bigger finite-dimensional algebra called
\mathfrak{confcarr}_{z}(d+1)
by adding generators
\{D,K\}
representing
z
-dependent dilatations and temporal special conformal transformations. The bracket relations read

\displaystyle=-\delta_{ij}H

\displaystyle[D,H]

\displaystyle=-zH

(158)

\displaystyle[P_{i},J_{jk}]

\displaystyle=2\delta_{i[j}P_{k]}

\displaystyle[D,P_{i}]

\displaystyle=-P_{i}

\displaystyle[B_{i},J_{jk}]

\displaystyle=2\delta_{i[j}B_{k]}

\displaystyle[D,B_{i}]

\displaystyle=(1-z)B_{i}

\displaystyle[J_{ij},J_{kl}]

\displaystyle=4\delta_{[i[k}J_{l]j]}

\displaystyle[D,K]

\displaystyle=(2-z)K

\displaystyle[K,P_{i}]

\displaystyle=2B_{i}

where the left column is the Carroll algebra and the right column are the additional brackets. For generic dimensions, there exists an infinite lift of this algebra called
\accentset{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{3.6}[0.7]{$\sim$}}}{\mathfrak{confcarr}}_{z}(d+1)

\displaystyle=2\delta_{i[j}P_{k]}

(159)

\displaystyle[J_{ij},J_{kl}]

\displaystyle=4\delta_{[i[k}J_{l]j]}

\displaystyle[M_{f},J_{jk}]

\displaystyle=-M_{x_{j}\partial_{k}f-x_{k}\partial_{j}f}

\displaystyle[D,P_{i}]

\displaystyle=-P_{i}

\displaystyle[P_{i},M_{f}]

\displaystyle=M_{\partial_{i}f}

\displaystyle[D,K_{i}]

\displaystyle=K_{i}

\displaystyle[D,M_{f}]

\displaystyle=M_{(x_{i}\partial_{i}-z)f}

\displaystyle[K_{i},J_{jk}]

\displaystyle=2\delta_{i[j}K_{k]}

\displaystyle[K_{i},M_{f}]

\displaystyle=M_{(2x_{i}x_{j}\partial_{j}-2zx_{i}-x_{j}x_{j}\partial_{i})f}

\displaystyle[K_{i},P_{j}]

\displaystyle=-2J_{ij}-2\delta_{ij}D

where the various generators have a spacetime realization as

\displaystyle P_{i}=\partial_{i}\qquad\qquad J_{ij}=x_{i}\partial_{j}-x_{j}\partial_{i}\qquad\qquad M_{f}=f(x_{i})\partial_{t}

(160)

\displaystyle D=zt\partial_{t}+x_{i}\partial_{i}\qquad\qquad K_{i}=2x_{i}(zt\partial_{t}+x_{j}\partial_{j})-x_{j}x_{j}\partial_{i}

(161)

and we defined the supertranslation generator
M_{f}
. As special cases it contains the generators for
\{H,B_{i},K\}=\{M_{1},M_{-x_{i}},M_{x_{i}x_{i}}\}
. This algebra can also be directly obtained by solving (
157
) for
h_{\mu\nu}=\delta_{ij}\delta^{i}_{\mu}\delta^{j}_{\nu}
and
v=-\partial_{t}
and is thus the
z
-dependent generalization of the BMS algebra in any dimension
8
8

8

The option of general
z
was also considered in
[
109
]
but called the level
N
. They are related by
z=2/N
.
. Since the left column in (
159
) is just the conformal algebra of Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we can also write

\displaystyle\accentset{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{3.6}[0.7]{$\sim$}}}{\mathfrak{confcarr}}_{z}(d+1)\cong\mathfrak{conf}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\,\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{$\uplus$}_{z}\,C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

(162)

which is the definition provided in
[
4
]
. While further extensions are still possible we shall stop here and only mention that once the
K_{i}
have been added a truncation to a finite dimensional algebra is only possible for
z\in\mathbb{N}
. The case
z=1
is the most prominent here since its finite dimensional truncation is the standard (finite-dimensional) conformal Carroll algebra
\mathfrak{cca}(d+1)\cong\mathfrak{iso}(d+1,1)
. This algebra is isomorphic to the Poincaré algebra in one dimension higher. Its bracket relations are given by (
158
) at
z=1
together with

\displaystyle[D,K_{i}]

\displaystyle=K_{i}

\displaystyle[K_{i},H]

\displaystyle=2B_{i}

\displaystyle[K_{i},P_{j}]

\displaystyle=-2J_{ij}-2\delta_{ij}D

\displaystyle[K_{i},B_{j}]

\displaystyle=\delta_{ij}K

(163)

\displaystyle[K_{i},J_{jk}]

\displaystyle=2\delta_{i[j}K_{k]}

Restricting to
d=2

Let us now focus on the case
d=2
. Since in this case we have by definition

\displaystyle\accentset{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{3.6}[0.7]{$\sim$}}}{\mathfrak{confcarr}}_{z}(2+1)\cong(\text{Witt}\oplus\text{Witt})\,\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{$\uplus$}_{z}\,C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

(164)

the algebra is now even larger as the local conformal transformations of two-dimensional Euclidean space are infinite-dimensional themselves and given by a direct sum of two Witt algebras. In a holographic context, these transformations are referred to as superrotations and play a decisive role in the formulation of celestial holography
[
50
,
217
]
. To bring the algebra in a recognizable form it is convenient to dualize the rotation generator by defining
J=\epsilon^{ij}J_{ij}
. Then, switching to complex coordinates for the spatial directions
w=x_{1}+ix_{2}
,
\bar{w}=x_{1}-ix_{2}
the algebra (
164
) reads

\displaystyle[L_{n},L_{m}]

\displaystyle=(n-m)L_{n+m}

\displaystyle[L_{n},M_{(r,s)}]

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{z}{2}(n+1)-r\Big)M_{(r+n,s)}

(165)

\displaystyle[\bar{L}_{n},\bar{L}_{m}]

\displaystyle=(n-m)\bar{L}_{n+m}

\displaystyle[\bar{L}_{n},M_{(r,s)}]

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{z}{2}(n+1)-s\Big)M_{(r,s+n)}

(166)

where
n,m,r,s\in\mathbb{Z}
and

\displaystyle M_{(r,s)}=w^{r}\bar{w}^{s}\partial_{t}

(167a)

\displaystyle L_{n}=-\frac{z}{2}(n+1)w^{n}t\partial_{t}-w^{n+1}\partial_{w}

(167b)

\displaystyle\bar{L}_{n}=-\frac{z}{2}(n+1)\bar{w}^{n}t\partial_{t}-\bar{w}^{n+1}\partial_{\bar{w}}~.

(167c)

The generators
\{P_{i},J,D,H,B_{i},K,K_{i}\}
are packaged into these for the values
n,m,r,s=-1,0,1
(for details see section 3.2 of
[
4
]
). However, it can be shown that they only form a subalgebra for
z=1
. The resulting algebra is referred to as the extended BMS algebra and admits a truncation to the standard
\mathfrak{bms}_{4}\cong\mathfrak{so}(3,1)\,\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{$\uplus$}\,C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
by restricting the superrotations
L_{n}
,
\bar{L}_{n}
to
n=-1,0,1
. Another special case is
z=0
where it was found that (
165
), (
166
) form near horizon symmetries of four-dimensional black holes
[
104
,
103
,
141
]
.
We finally mention that the Carroll conformal Killing vectors (
167
) can also be written in the concise form

\displaystyle\xi=\Big[f+\frac{zt}{2}(\partial_{w}Y^{w}+\partial_{\bar{w}}Y^{\bar{w}})\Big]\partial_{t}+Y^{w}\partial_{w}+Y^{\bar{w}}\partial_{\bar{w}}

(168)

where
f=f(w,\bar{w})
,
Y^{w}=Y^{w}(w)
and
Y^{\bar{w}}=Y^{\bar{w}}(\bar{w})
. This makes it especially simple to check (
157
) resulting in the function on the left hand side being given by
\rho=\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{w}Y^{w}+\partial_{\bar{w}}Y^{\bar{w}})
.

Restricting to
d=1

At
d=1
one finds from the definition (
162
)

\displaystyle\accentset{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{3.6}[0.7]{$\sim$}}}{\mathfrak{confcarr}}_{z}(1+1)\cong\text{Witt}\,\,\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{$\uplus$}_{z}\,C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})

(169)

which is generated by the Carroll conformal Killing vectors

\displaystyle\xi=\Big[f(x)+tz\partial_{x}Y(x)\Big]\partial_{t}+Y(x)\partial_{x}~.

(170)

They satisfy (
157
) with
\rho=\partial_{x}Y
. Here,
f
again generates the tower of supertranslations and
Y
is associated to the two-dimensional version of superrotations. They can be put again into a more recognizable form by defining

\displaystyle L_{n}

\displaystyle=-zt(n+1)x^{n}\partial_{t}-x^{n+1}\partial_{x}

\displaystyle M_{n}

\displaystyle=x^{n+z}\partial_{t}

(171)

which gives the Lie brackets

\displaystyle[L_{n},L_{m}]

\displaystyle=(n-m)L_{n+m}

(172)

\displaystyle[L_{n},M_{m}]

\displaystyle=\big(zn-m\big)M_{n+m}

(173)

\displaystyle[M_{n},M_{m}]

\displaystyle=0~.

(174)

Some special cases of this algebra are
z=1
, which yields the superrotation extended
\mathfrak{bms}_{3}
algebra
[
45
]
. Up to central extensions these are the asymptotic symmetries of asymptotically flat three-dimensional Einstein gravity. The case
z=0
corresponds to the non-centrally extended version of the warped conformal algebra where
M_{n}
are the modes of a
U(1)
current (see, e.g.
[
99
]
). Finally,
z=-1
is called the
\mathfrak{bms}_{2}
algebra. It features in studies of asymptotically flat spacetimes in two-dimensional dilaton gravity
[
6
,
8
]
.

9
Weyl-transformations

As a final point, we mention various Weyl transformation properties of Carroll geometries since they shall play an important role for various instances of Carroll field theories. Given an arbitrary local function
\rho(x)
, we define infinitesimal Weyl transformations of the metric variables as

\displaystyle\delta_{\rho}\tau_{\mu}

\displaystyle=z\rho\tau_{\mu}

\displaystyle\delta_{\rho}h_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=2\rho h_{\mu\nu}

(175)

\displaystyle\delta_{\rho}v^{\mu}

\displaystyle=-z\rho v^{\mu}

\displaystyle\delta_{\rho}h^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=-2\rho h^{\mu\nu}

(176)

where
z\in\mathbb{R}
is the anisotropy exponent. The various composite fields therefore transform as

\displaystyle\delta_{\rho}K_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=(2-z)\rho K_{\mu\nu}-h_{\mu\nu}v^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\rho

(177)

\displaystyle\delta_{\rho}K

\displaystyle=-z\rho K-dv^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\rho

(178)

\displaystyle\delta_{\rho}\,a_{\mu}

\displaystyle=zh_{\mu\alpha}h^{\alpha\nu}\partial_{\nu}\rho~.

(179)

They can be used to define the object

\displaystyle W_{\mu}=\frac{K}{d}\tau_{\mu}+\frac{1}{z}a_{\mu}

(180)

which transforms like a
U(1)
-connection,
\delta_{\rho}W_{\mu}=\partial_{\mu}\rho
.

Chapter 3
Field theories on Carroll backgrounds

\epigraphfontsize

“We can’t stop here, this is bat country!”

— Hunter S. Thompson, Fear and Loathing in Las Vegas

Now that we defined Carroll geometries, we may consider them as backgrounds for field theories. As we shall see, depending on how the fields couple to the geometry one arrives at different conclusions about the physical content these theories are able to capture. This chapter is structured as follows: In section
10
we derive the stress-energy tensor as a response to varying the background metric- and connection data. In both cases, we compute the classical Ward identities associated to the background gauge symmetries. Once a symmetric background is chosen, these allow us to construct charges and conservation laws associated to these symmetries. For deriving the brackets between these charges we need brackets between the stress-energy tensor components which are derived for a general Carroll quantum field theory in section
11
. Finally, we discuss some examples in section
12
. The material of this chapter in part is based on
[
107
]
and
[
114
]
.

10
General structure and covariant formulation

Consider a local action functional
I_{m}[\phi,h_{\mu\nu},\tau_{\mu}]
for some field
\phi
(not necessarily a scalar field) that couples to the Carroll metric data
h_{\mu\nu}
,
\tau_{\mu}
in a covariant way. The general variation of the action then takes the form

\displaystyle\delta I_{m}[\phi,h_{\mu\nu},\tau_{\mu}]=\int\differential^{d+1}x\,\mathfrak{e}\Big[\frac{1}{2}T^{\mu\nu}\delta h_{\mu\nu}+T^{\mu}\delta\tau_{\mu}+(\text{EOM})_{\phi}\,\delta\phi\Big]

(181)

where the last part are just the equations of motion for the matter fields. We take the background geometry here in the language of the second order formulation of section
7.2
, where the affine connection is chosen to be metric compatible and has only intrinsic torsion. As we shall see in chapter
4
, these are typical off-shell geometries of magnetic Carroll gravity. While they also contain independent data
C_{\mu\nu}
in the connection that the field theory could couple to, we assume here that this is not the case. This assumption is valid for a large part of the Carroll field theories discussed in the literature
[
164
,
58
,
114
]
, but there do exist counterexamples
[
39
]
, see also section
12.2
.

The independent background metric fields are chosen as the Carroll clock form
\tau_{\mu}
and Carroll metric
h_{\mu\nu}
which uniquely determine the fields
v^{\mu}
,
h^{\mu\nu}
with upper indices by (see also section
5
)

\displaystyle v^{\mu}\tau_{\mu}

\displaystyle=-1

\displaystyle v^{\mu}h_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\tau_{\mu}h^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle h^{\mu\rho}h_{\rho\nu}

\displaystyle=\delta^{\mu}_{\nu}+v^{\mu}\tau_{\nu}~.

(182)

We also use these expressions to determine the variations of the latter fields by

\displaystyle\delta v^{\mu}

\displaystyle=v^{\mu}v^{\nu}\delta\tau_{\nu}-h^{\mu(\nu}v^{\alpha)}\delta h_{\nu\alpha}

\displaystyle\delta h^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=2v^{(\mu}h^{\nu)\alpha}\delta\tau_{\alpha}-h^{\rho(\mu}h^{\nu)\alpha}\delta h_{\rho\alpha}~.

(183)

Except for the symmetry in the indices of
\delta h_{\alpha\beta}
, the variations
\delta\tau_{\mu}
and
\delta h_{\mu\nu}
are completely unconstrained which is needed for deriving the response functions unambiguously. Had we instead taken the Carroll boost invariant variations
\delta v^{\mu}
and
\delta h_{\mu\nu}
to define the response functions we would not be able to access the same information by computing the background variation, since the relation
v^{\mu}h_{\mu\nu}=0
makes these variations dependent, see also the discussion in
[
39
]
.

10.1
Spacetime symmetries and conservation laws

10.1.1
Non-conformal case

For the coupling to be consistent with the background gauge symmetries, the response functions
T^{\mu}
and
T^{\mu\nu}
need to satisfy further requirements when the equations of motion for the dynamical fields hold. These (classical) Ward identities are determined by demanding

\displaystyle\delta_{\xi}I_{m}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}I_{m}

\displaystyle=0

(184)

on-shell and up to boundary terms leading to the Carroll boost Ward identity

\displaystyle T^{\mu}h_{\mu\nu}=0

(185)

as well as the diffeomorphism Ward identity

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}\big(\mathfrak{e}\,T^{\mu}\tau_{\alpha}+\mathfrak{e}T^{\mu\rho}h_{\rho\alpha}\big)=\mathfrak{e}T^{\mu}\,\partial_{\alpha}\tau_{\mu}+\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{e}T^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\alpha}h_{\mu\nu}~.

(186)

By taking a second variation of the action one may as well determine the boost transformation behaviour of the response functions

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}T^{\mu}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}T^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=2\lambda^{(\mu}T^{\nu)}

(187)

which motivates the boost invariant definition

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}^{\mu}{}_{\nu}:=T^{\mu}\tau_{\nu}+T^{\mu\rho}h_{\rho\nu}

(188)

called the
Carroll stress-energy tensor
. With this definition, the diffeomorphism Ward identity (
186
) and Carroll boost Ward identity can be written in the covariant form

\boxed{\phantom{\Bigg(}\nabla_{\mu}\mathbf{T}^{\mu}{}_{\nu}=2\mathbf{T}^{\mu}{}_{\alpha}h^{\alpha\rho}K_{\rho[\mu}\tau_{\nu]}-K\tau_{\mu}\mathbf{T}^{\mu}{}_{\nu}\qquad\qquad v^{\nu}h_{\mu\rho}\mathbf{T}^{\mu}{}_{\nu}=0~.\phantom{\Bigg)}}

(189)

where we used the Carroll connection (
137
) associated to the chosen background which satisfies
\partial_{\mu}\log\mathfrak{e}=\Gamma^{\alpha}{}_{\alpha\mu}+\tau_{\mu}K
. One can see that on torsion free backgrounds, where
K_{\mu\nu}=0
, the diffeomorphism Ward identity reduces to the usual covariant conservation equation.

In the same manner as for Riemannian geometries, any symmetry of the background visible to the field theory leads to an on-shell conserved current density defined by

\displaystyle j^{\mu}=\mathfrak{e}\mathbf{T}^{\mu}{}_{\nu}\xi^{\nu}

(190)

where the vector field
\xi^{\mu}
is the infinitesimal generator of that symmetry. Since the field theory is only assumed to couple to the Carroll metric data
\xi^{\mu}
only needs to solve the Carroll Killing equations

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\xi}v^{\mu}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\xi}h_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=0

(191)

and not the affine Killing condition (
149
). Then, according to the discussion in section
7.3
one obtains infinitely many symmetries and thus infinitely many conservation laws. Indeed, using the diffeomorphism Ward identity one shows that

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}j^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{e}T^{\mu}\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\tau_{\mu}+\frac{\mathfrak{e}}{2}T^{\mu\rho}\mathcal{L}_{\xi}h_{\mu\rho}

(192)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{e}T^{\alpha}\tau_{\alpha}\tau_{\mu}\mathcal{L}_{\xi}v^{\mu}+\frac{\mathfrak{e}}{2}T^{\mu\rho}\mathcal{L}_{\xi}h_{\mu\rho}

(193)

where we used the Carroll boost Ward identity in the second line. Thus, on account of (
191
) we obtain
\partial_{\mu}j^{\mu}=0
. Choosing a codimension
-1
spatial hypersurface
\Sigma
with normal one-form
n^{\mu}
, one can define a charge by

\displaystyle Q(\Sigma)=\int_{\Sigma}\differential^{d}x\,n_{\mu}j^{\mu}\big|_{\Sigma}

(194)

and show that for two such hypersurfaces the change in charge is given by the flux across the spatial boundary of the region enclosed by the surfaces,

\displaystyle Q(\Sigma^{\prime})-Q(\Sigma)=-\int_{\mathcal{B}}d^{d}y\,b_{\mu}j^{\mu}

(195)

where
b_{\mu}
is the outward-pointing normal one-form to
\mathcal{B}
. Therefore, if we interpret
n_{\mu}
as giving a time direction one finds charge conservation if the flux falls off sufficiently fast.

Let us look at this scenario for the simplest background, i.e., the flat Carroll manifold as obtained from taking the limit of Minkowski space (see section
5
). We have (renaming
t=x^{0}
)

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=-\partial_{0}

\displaystyle h_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\delta_{ij}\delta^{i}_{\mu}\delta^{j}_{\nu}

\displaystyle\tau

\displaystyle=\differential x^{0}

(196)

and the Carroll Killing fields preserving this structure are given by

\displaystyle\xi_{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle M}_{f}}

\displaystyle=f(x^{i})\partial_{0}

\displaystyle\xi_{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle P_{i}}}

\displaystyle=\partial_{i}

\displaystyle\xi_{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle J_{ij}}}

\displaystyle=x_{i}\partial_{j}-x_{j}\partial_{i}~,

(197)

closing into the infinite lift
\mathfrak{isocarr}(d+1)
of the Carroll algebra given by setting
\sigma=0
in (
150
). Picking an
x^{0}=\text{cst.}
surface with
n=\differential x^{0}
one arrives at the corresponding charges

\displaystyle M_{f}

\displaystyle=\int\differential^{d}x\,\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{0}f(x^{i})

\displaystyle P_{i}

\displaystyle=\int\differential^{d}x\,\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{i}

\displaystyle J_{ij}

\displaystyle=\int\differential^{d}x\,(x_{i}\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{j}-x_{j}\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{i})~.

(198)

They denote the supertranslation charge
M_{f}
, spatial momentum charge
P_{i}
and angular momentum charge
J_{ij}
. The special cases
M_{f=-x_{i}}
and
M_{f=1}
are the charges for Carroll boosts
B_{i}
and energy
H
, respectively. Since the Carroll boost Ward identity implies vanishing energy flux,
\mathbf{T}^{i}{}_{0}=0
, one finds that on a fixed time slice
\Sigma

\displaystyle\frac{\differential}{\differential x^{0}}M_{f}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\frac{\differential}{\differential x^{0}}P_{i}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\partial\Sigma}\differential\sigma_{k}\mathbf{T}^{k}{}_{i}

\displaystyle\frac{\differential}{\differential x^{0}}J_{ij}

\displaystyle=-2\int_{\partial\Sigma}\differential\sigma_{k}x_{[i}\mathbf{T}^{k}{}_{j]}

(199)

where
\partial\Sigma=\Sigma\cap\mathcal{B}
. This shows that the total supertranslation charge, and in particular the energy, is conserved for any a given region of spacetime while linear and angular momentum charges can in principle be changed by flux through the boundary. This is the field theory avatar of saying that massive bodies are immobile in Carroll theories (see also section
4
) - no energy flux is possible. We shall see in subsection
10.2
that this statement can change once the theory couples to the connection of the background.

10.1.2
Scale invariant case

There are examples, where the set of Carroll symmetries just discussed is extended by a
z
-dependent scale symmetry. The classical covariantly coupled action is then additionally invariant under constant
z
-dependent Weyl rescalings defined in section
9
. One may construct an associated conserved current in the following way. First, covariance of the action implies that for an arbitrary, non-constant Weyl parameter we have

\displaystyle\delta_{\rho}I_{m}=\int\differential^{d+1}x\,\mathfrak{e}\,\mathcal{K}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\rho

(200)

where
\mathcal{K}^{\mu}
is some covariant expression that depends on the dynamical and background fields. Second, an on-shell Weyl-variation can always be written as

\displaystyle\delta_{\rho}I_{m}=\int\differential^{d+1}x\,\mathfrak{e}\big(zT^{\mu}\tau_{\mu}+T^{\mu\nu}h_{\mu\nu}\big)\rho

(201)

where we assumed that
\rho
falls of sufficiently fast such that boundary terms can be dropped. Putting these expressions together yields

\boxed{\phantom{\Bigg(}zT^{\mu}\tau_{\mu}+T^{\mu\nu}h_{\mu\nu}=-\mathfrak{e}^{-1}\partial_{\mu}\big(\mathfrak{e}\mathcal{K}^{\mu})\phantom{\Bigg)}}

(202)

setting the generalized trace of the stress-energy tensor to a total derivative. For a generator of anisotropic scale transformations
\xi_{\textrm{D}}
satisfying
\mathcal{L}_{\xi_{\textrm{D}}}h_{\mu\nu}=2h_{\mu\nu}
,
\mathcal{L}_{\xi_{\textrm{D}}}v^{\mu}=-zv^{\mu}
, one may then identify the associated on-shell conserved current density by

\displaystyle j_{D}^{\mu}=\mathfrak{e}\mathbf{T}^{\mu}{}_{\nu}\xi^{\nu}_{\textrm{D}}+\mathfrak{e}\mathcal{K}^{\mu}~.

(203)

where conservation is shown by using the diffeomorphism Ward identity (
186
). For a flat background, these scale transformations are just generated by

\displaystyle\xi_{\textrm{D}}=zx^{0}\partial_{0}+x^{i}\partial_{i}~.

(204)

From this discussion it becomes evident that scale invariance in general does not imply a vanishing trace of the stress-energy tensor, even for the case
z=1
. It might, however, happen for some theories that the term
\mathcal{K}^{\mu}
can be absorbed into the definition of the stress-energy tensor by an improvement

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}^{\mu}{}_{\nu}\to\mathbf{T}^{\mu}{}_{\nu}+\partial_{\rho}B^{\rho\mu}{}_{\nu}

(205)

where the tensor
B^{\rho\mu}{}_{\nu}
has to satisfy

\displaystyle B^{(\rho\mu)}{}_{\nu}=0

\displaystyle\partial_{\rho}B^{\rho\mu}{}_{\nu}v^{\nu}h_{\mu\sigma}=0

(206)

to ensure stress-energy tensor conservation and local Carroll boost invariance. If this improvement renders the stress-energy tensor traceless the theory is not just scale invariant any more but rather conformally invariant, as explained in the next point. We have not pursued this direction further but it could be worth to explore and possibly show some results like
[
199
,
73
]
for the Carroll case.

10.1.3
Conformal case

If the field theory is additionally conformally coupled to the background, it should be invariant under local Weyl rescalings of secetion
9
with anisotropy exponent
z
. This makes the quantity
\mathcal{K}^{\mu}
of the previous point vanish identically. Then, in addition to the Ward identities (
189
) one obtains a generalized trace identity

\boxed{\phantom{\Bigg(}zT^{\mu}\tau_{\mu}+T^{\mu\nu}h_{\mu\nu}=0\phantom{\Bigg)}}

(207)

which reduces to
\mathbf{T}^{\mu}{}_{\mu}=0
for the isotropic case
z=1
.
In this case, depending on the particular background, one may obtain an even larger class of symmetries and charges by solving the conformal Carroll Killing equations (
157
) (see also section
8
). For the flat background (
196
), the solutions are

\displaystyle\xi=Y^{i}(x^{j})\partial_{i}+\Big(f(x^{i})+\frac{zx^{0}}{d}\partial_{k}Y^{k}\Big)\partial_{0}

\displaystyle\partial_{(i}Y_{j)}=\frac{1}{d}\partial_{k}Y^{k}\delta_{ij}

(208)

i.e.,
Y^{i}
needs to be a conformal Killing vector of the Euclidean transverse plane. Besides the choices (
197
), these symmetries contain dilations and special conformal transformations represented by the vectors (
161
). One may again construct conserved currents associated to these charges by
j^{\mu}=\mathfrak{e}\mathbf{T}^{\mu}{}_{\nu}\xi^{\nu}
. Their conservation can be readily checked by using (
192
) together with the trace Ward identity (
207
).

Any theory that admits a covariant formulation for an arbitrary background such that Weyl and diffeomorphism invariance are manifest will automatically descend to a Carroll conformal field theory (CCFT) on a flat background. However, in general this is a stronger condition than necessary for a CCFT to exist on a flat background. A counterexample is the Carroll version of Liouville theory
[
48
]
which is not Weyl invariant on a curved background but still descends to a true CCFT once the background is flat.

10.2
Coupling to the connection

One may consider a generalization of the couplings allowed in (
181
) and write

\displaystyle\delta I_{m}[\phi,h_{\mu\nu},\tau_{\mu},C_{\mu\nu}]=\int\differential^{d+1}x\,\mathfrak{e}\Big[\frac{1}{2}T^{\mu\nu}\delta h_{\mu\nu}+T^{\mu}\delta\tau_{\mu}+\frac{1}{2}\Psi^{\mu\nu}\delta C_{\mu\nu}+(\text{EOM})_{\phi}\,\delta\phi\Big]

(209)

where
C_{\mu\nu}
is additional free data in the Carroll compatible connection that describes the background geometry. Since this is a transverse symmetric tensor there is no loss of generality in assuming the same properties for the corresponding response function
\Psi^{\mu\nu}
. The field
C_{\mu\nu}
transforms non-trivially both under diffeomorphisms and local Carroll boosts, which makes the Ward identities pick up some additional terms. For local Carroll boosts we use the transformation (
140
) and find that the requirement
\delta_{\lambda}I_{m}=0
leads to the Carroll boost Ward identity

\displaystyle\Big(T^{\mu}+\frac{1}{2}\big(\nabla_{\nu}-a_{\nu}\big)\Psi^{\mu\nu}\Big)h_{\mu\rho}=0

(210)

where we imposed the equations of motion for the matter field. Thus, due to the coupling to the connection the energy flux component acquires a non-zero value that is controlled by the response to the connection. Similarly, invariance under diffeomorphisms leads to

\frac{1}{\mathfrak{e}}\partial_{\mu}\big(\mathfrak{e}\,T^{\mu}\tau_{\alpha}+\mathfrak{e}T^{\mu\rho}h_{\rho\alpha}+\mathfrak{e}\,\Psi^{\mu\nu}C_{\nu\alpha}\big)=T^{\mu}\,\partial_{\alpha}\tau_{\mu}+\frac{1}{2}T^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\alpha}h_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{2}\Psi^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\alpha}C_{\mu\nu}

(211)

which can also be rewritten covariantly and in terms of
\mathbf{T}^{\mu}{}_{\alpha}
as

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mu}\big(\mathbf{T}^{\mu}{}_{\nu}+\Psi^{\mu\alpha}C_{\alpha\nu}\big)=2\big(

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}^{\mu}{}_{\alpha}+\Psi^{\mu\beta}C_{\beta\alpha}\big)h^{\alpha\rho}K_{\rho[\mu}\tau_{\nu]}-K\tau_{\mu}\mathbf{T}^{\mu}{}_{\nu}

(212)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{2}\Big(\Psi^{\mu\alpha}\nabla_{\nu}C_{\mu\alpha}-(\nabla_{\nu}\tau_{\mu})(\nabla_{\rho}-a_{\rho})\Psi^{\rho\mu}\Big)~.

These generalized Ward identities may again be used to construct conservation laws once the theory is put on a symmetric background. By definition, a symmetry of the background should leave all the geometric data, including the independent part of the connection invariant. We saw the general condition written in terms of Carroll boost invariant quantities in (
148
). In the present case, it is more convenient to phrase the Carroll Killing conditions in terms of the background variables the theory is chosen to couple to. They read

\displaystyle\delta_{\xi}\tau_{\mu}=0

\displaystyle\delta_{\xi}h_{\mu\nu}=0

\displaystyle\delta_{\xi}C_{\mu\nu}=0

(213)

where the variations are given by
\delta_{\xi}=\mathcal{L}_{\xi}+\delta_{\lambda(\xi)}
and include a local Carroll boost variation that depends on
\xi^{\mu}
. This is necessary to take the gauge dependence of
\tau_{\mu}
and
C_{\mu\nu}
into account where
\delta_{\lambda}C_{\mu\nu}
is given in (
140
). One can show that on the space of minimal torsion geometries these conditions are equivalent to (
148
). Consider a current density given by

\displaystyle j^{\mu}=\mathfrak{e}\mathbf{T}^{\mu}{}_{\nu}\xi^{\nu}+\mathfrak{e}\Psi^{\mu\rho}C_{\rho\nu}\xi^{\nu}+\frac{\mathfrak{e}}{2}\Psi^{\mu\nu}\lambda_{\nu}(\xi)~.

(214)

Upon inserting the Ward identities (
210
) and (
211
) one can show that

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}j^{\mu}=\mathfrak{e}T^{\mu}\delta_{\xi}\tau_{\mu}+\frac{\mathfrak{e}}{2}T^{\mu\nu}\delta_{\xi}h_{\mu\nu}+\frac{\mathfrak{e}}{2}\Psi^{\mu\nu}\delta_{\xi}C_{\mu\nu}

(215)

and thus obtains conservation once (
213
) is satisfied. In the maximally symmetric case, we know that the local solutions to these conditions form the Carroll algebra (or its
\Lambda
-deformation) under the Lie bracket. Therefore, we again obtain a conserved charge for each generator of the Carroll algebra.

This framework of coupling a Carroll field theory to the connection has recently been used to perform a holographic derivation of the flux balance laws associated to asymptotically flat Einstein gravity in four dimensions
[
125
]
. While the authors extended the available background symmetries further to also include local Weyl rescalings, the basic mechanism can already be spotted in this more simple scenario. First, we rewrite equation (
215
) such that the left hand side is just built by the responses to the metric fields,

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}(\mathfrak{e}\mathbf{T}^{\mu}{}_{\nu}\xi^{\nu})=\mathfrak{e}T^{\mu}\delta_{\xi}\tau_{\mu}+\frac{\mathfrak{e}}{2}T^{\mu\nu}\delta_{\xi}h_{\mu\nu}+\text{(flux)}

(216)

where we absorbed all terms that are proportional to
\Psi^{\mu\nu}
