into a flux-term. In a holographic setting one then chooses boundary conditions that fix some geometric properties of the boundary manifold. If one demands that the Carroll metric data is fixed completely one arrives at
\delta_{\xi}\tau_{\mu}=0
,
\delta_{\xi}h_{\mu\nu}=0
, which yields the vector fields of the supertranslation-extended Carroll algebra
9
9

9

In the conformal case superrotations are also included.
for a flat background. Equation (
216
) then reduces to a relation of the type

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}j^{\mu}=\text{(flux)}

(217)

which is a flux-balance law where the right hand side consists of the
\Psi^{\mu\nu}
dependent terms. In a holographic setting, these terms consist of the news tensor which describes the gravitational radiation passing through the boundary. This description still misses the conformal aspect of the symmetries to describe the actual situation for asymptotically flat spacetimes, but we wish to point out the difference to the philosophy we used so far: While the invariance of
C_{\mu\nu}
was a requirement for having a true variational symmetry of a matter field coupled to this background this requirement is dropped in a holographic setting. This renders the conservation laws into controlled non-conservation laws. They capture the leakiness of asymptotically flat spacetimes (see, e.g.,
[
126
]
and refs therein). In other words, a Carroll theory holographically dual to asymptotically flat four-dimensional Einstein gravity should be sourced by the data corresponding to gravitational radiation
[
223
,
101
,
102
]
.

11
Commutators and universal relations

We saw how symmetries of Carroll field theories represented by Carroll (conformal) Killing vectors lead to conservation laws and flux balance relations. As in quantum field theory, we expect these symmetries to be realized on the level of the codimension-1 charges (
194
) which, however, requires knowing the brackets between the various stress-energy tensor components that are involved. In this subsection, we shall present a way to obtain these brackets from the assumptions of covariance and locality, based on the upcoming work
[
107
]
. This reveals a certain universal structure appearing in the stress-energy tensor brackets which is intimately tied to the Carroll nature of the field theory. In the Lorentzian case, the result of a universal part in the stress-energy tensor commutators goes back to the seminal works by Dirac
[
100
]
and Schwinger
[
213
]
. Here, we shall use the slightly more modern formulation by Boulware and Deser
[
97
]
.

Given a matter theory formulated in terms of a local action
I_{m}
on a fixed Carroll background, we assume that its correlation functions can be extracted from a generating functional

\displaystyle Z[\tau_{\mu},h_{\mu\nu}]=\int\mathcal{D}\phi\,e^{iI_{m}}=e^{iW}

(218)

where
\phi
again should be seen as a dummy for any possible field content and
W
is the generating functional of connected correlation functions denoted by
\langle...\rangle_{c}
in the following. Given some local operator
\mathcal{O}(x)
, we may write, e.g., a two-point function as

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{T}\mathcal{O}(x)\mathcal{O}(y)\rangle=\frac{1}{Z}\int\mathcal{D}\phi\,\mathcal{O}(x)\mathcal{O}(y)e^{iI_{m}}

(219)

where time-ordering
\mathcal{T}
is defined in the usual way

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{O}(x)\mathcal{O}(y))=\Theta(x^{0}-y^{0})\mathcal{O}(x)\mathcal{O}(y)+\Theta(y^{0}-x^{0})\mathcal{O}(y)\mathcal{O}(x)~.

(220)

As in the previous subsection, we assume here that no coupling to the connection degrees of freedom
C_{\mu\nu}
exists. Since we are mostly interested in the representation of spacetime symmetries, we will evaluate expressions on a symmetric Carroll geometry. In the present case, this will just be the flat Carroll spacetime (
196
) but in principle one could also choose some other symmetric manifold such as a homogeneous spacetime. In any case, this evaluation always has to be performed at the very end of each calculation.
For
\mathcal{O}
given by stress-energy tensor components the correlation functions are generated by a variation of the background,

\displaystyle\langle T^{\mu}(x)\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathfrak{e}}\frac{\delta W}{\delta\tau_{\mu}(x)}

\displaystyle\langle T^{\mu\nu}(x)\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\mathfrak{e}}\frac{\delta W}{\delta h_{\mu\nu}(x)}

(221)

and likewise, for obtaining a two-point function one computes the second variation,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathfrak{e}}\frac{\delta}{\delta\tau_{\mu}(y)}\frac{1}{\mathfrak{e}}\frac{\delta}{\delta\tau_{\nu}(x)}W

\displaystyle=i\langle\mathcal{T}T^{\mu}(x)T^{\nu}(y)\rangle-i\langle T^{\mu}(x)\rangle\langle T^{\nu}(y)\rangle+\frac{1}{\mathfrak{e}(y)}\Big\langle\frac{\delta T^{\mu}(x)}{\delta\tau_{\nu}(y)}\Big\rangle

(222)

\displaystyle=i\langle\mathcal{T}T^{\mu}(x)T^{\nu}(y)\rangle_{c}+\frac{1}{\mathfrak{e}(y)}\Big\langle\frac{\delta T^{\mu}(x)}{\delta\tau_{\nu}(y)}\Big\rangle~.

(223)

This expression splits into a bilocal piece given by the connected two-point function as well as a local piece that schematically can be written as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathfrak{e}(y)}\Big\langle\frac{\delta T^{\mu}(x)}{\delta\tau_{\nu}(y)}\Big\rangle=\mathcal{D}^{\mu\nu}_{x}\delta^{d+1}(x-y)

(224)

and includes only contact terms.
\mathcal{D}^{\mu\nu}_{x}
is some derivative operator with
c
-number coefficients. The origin of this term lies in the variation of the measure as well as the variation of the stress-energy tensor component with respect to the background. As we shall see, it is precisely terms of this form that contain universal information about the Carroll nature of these field theories. One may work out analogous expressions for the other second variations but since we only want to set up the formalism here we shall leave it at this one.

In the Lorentzian case, the generating functional
W
is typically ill-defined and needs some renormalization procedure. This comes with the usual ambiguities of adding further counterterms to
W
that do not change the finiteness properties but may change the structure of the contact terms appearing on the right hand side of (
223
). For obtaining consistent equal-time commutators between stress-energy tensor components one has to partially fix these terms which in the Lorentzian case goes back to the work by Boulware and Deser
[
97
]
. The important point is that this always has to be possible in a relativistic quantum field theory as otherwise there would be no consistent way of realizing the classical symmetries on the Hilbert space of the quantum theory. We shall see that something analogous can be done for Carroll quantum field theories that admit a path integral formulation as put forward in this section.

It may happen that some classical symmetries are violated in the quantum theory, i.e., the theory exhibits an anomaly. This is a consequence of the path integral measure being not invariant under the respective symmetry
[
129
]
and there being no possibility of adding counterterms to remedy this. We shall see in an explicit example how such anomalous contributions are tied to central extensions appearing in the representation of the symmetries.

11.1
Ward identities revisited

Requiring that the generating functional is consistent with diffeomorphism invariance of the action
I_{m}[\phi,\tau_{\mu},h_{\mu\nu}]
, we can infer some differential relations between one-point functions. This is easy to see by noting that a change of variables
\phi\to\phi^{\prime}
leaves
Z
invariant. Choosing
\phi^{\prime}=\phi+\delta_{\xi}\phi
, where
\delta_{\xi}\phi=\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\phi
denotes the action of a diffeomorphism we obtain infinitesimally

\displaystyle 0=\int\mathcal{D}\phi\,\Big(i\int\differential^{d+1}x\,\frac{\delta I_{m}}{\delta\phi(x)}\delta_{\xi}\phi(x)\Big)\,e^{iI_{m}}

(225)

where it was assumed that the path integral measure is invariant under this change of variables, i.e.,
\mathcal{D}\phi=\mathcal{D}\phi^{\prime}
. Since the matter action is covariantly coupled, we can write

\displaystyle\int\differential^{d+1}x\,\frac{\delta I_{m}}{\delta\phi(x)}\delta_{\xi}\phi(x)

\displaystyle=-\int\differential^{d+1}x\,\frac{\delta I_{m}}{\delta\tau_{\mu}(x)}\delta_{\xi}\tau_{\mu}(x)-\int\differential^{d+1}x\,\frac{\delta I_{m}}{\delta h_{\mu\nu}(x)}\delta_{\xi}h_{\mu\nu}(x)

(226)

\displaystyle=-\int\differential^{d+1}x\,\mathfrak{e}\Big(T^{\mu}(x)\delta_{\xi}\tau_{\mu}(x)+\frac{1}{2}T^{\mu\nu}(x)\delta_{\xi}h_{\mu\nu}(x)\Big)

(227)

where we used the definition of the stress-energy tensor components (
181
). Assuming that
\xi
is chosen such that no boundary terms contribute, we can partially integrate and obtain the diffeomorphism Ward identity

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}\big(\mathfrak{e}\,\langle T^{\mu}\rangle\tau_{\alpha}+\mathfrak{e}\langle T^{\mu\rho}\rangle h_{\rho\alpha}\big)=\mathfrak{e}\langle T^{\mu}\rangle\,\partial_{\alpha}\tau_{\mu}+\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{e}\langle T^{\mu\nu}\rangle\partial_{\alpha}h_{\mu\nu}~.

(228)

At this point, this holds for an arbitrary background configuration. From (
227
) it also becomes clear that this statement is equivalent to
W
being invariant under a diffeomorphism variation of the background.

One may proceed in a similar fashion for the Ward identity associated to local Carroll boosts. However, we take into account the option of an anomaly for these transformations. Upon performing an infinitesimal change of variables
\phi^{\prime}=\phi+\delta_{\lambda}\phi
one obtains

\displaystyle 0=\int\mathcal{D}\phi\,\Big(-i\int\differential^{d+1}x\,\mathfrak{e}\,\mathcal{A}^{\mu}\lambda_{\mu}+i\int\differential^{d+1}x\,\frac{\delta I_{m}}{\delta\phi(x)}\delta_{\lambda}\phi(x)\Big)\,e^{iI_{m}}

(229)

where
\mathcal{A}^{\mu}
is some local expression that captures the contribution from the anomaly, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}\phi^{\prime}=\mathcal{D}\phi\,\exp\Big({-i\int\dd^{d+1}x\,\mathfrak{e}\,\mathcal{A}^\mu\lambda_\mu}\Big)~.

(230)

From this we obtain the anomalous Carroll boost Ward identity

\displaystyle\langle T^{\mu}(x)\rangle h_{\mu\nu}=\mathcal{A}^{\mu}(x)h_{\mu\nu}~.

(231)

It is a fair question to ask in which sense such an anomaly is physical since given our presentation of local Carroll boosts so far it is associated to a gauge symmetry of the background. Indeed, when such theories are coupled to Carroll gravity this leads to an inconsistency. However, in a holographic context where the dual boundary theory does not include gravity and Carroll boosts act as global symmetries it is not inconsistent per se to have anomalous Carroll boosts. In fact, this situation is even expected
[
75
,
53
,
154
]
.

11.2
Equal-time commutators of a Carroll stress-energy tensor

The commutator structure of a local quantum field theory is intimately tied to the contact terms appearing in two-point functions through the time ordering prescription. This can be observed explicitly by taking a background variation of the diffeomorphism Ward identity (
228
). Similarly to (
223
), this produces contact terms along a two-point function. The variations with respect to
\tau_{\nu}(y)
and
h_{\nu\rho}(y)
read

\displaystyle i\partial_{\mu}\big\langle\mathcal{T}\big(\mathfrak{e}\mathbf{T}^{\mu}{}_{\alpha}(x)\big)\mathfrak{e}T^{\nu}(y)\big\rangle-i\big\langle\mathcal{T}\partial_{\mu}\big(\mathfrak{e}\mathbf{T}^{\mu}{}_{\alpha}(x)\big)\mathfrak{e}T^{\nu}(y)\big\rangle=

(232)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\mathfrak{e}\langle T^{\nu}(x)\rangle\partial_{\alpha}\delta^{d+1}(x-y)-\partial_{\mu}\Big\langle\frac{\delta(\mathfrak{e}\mathbf{T}^{\mu}{}_{\alpha}(x))}{\delta\tau_{\nu}(y)}\Big\rangle+(\text{curv.})

\displaystyle i\partial_{\mu}\big\langle\mathcal{T}\big(\mathfrak{e}\mathbf{T}^{\mu}{}_{\alpha}(x)\big)\mathfrak{e}T^{\nu\rho}(y)\big\rangle-i\big\langle\mathcal{T}\partial_{\mu}\big(\mathfrak{e}\mathbf{T}^{\mu}{}_{\alpha}(x)\big)\mathfrak{e}T^{\nu\rho}(y)\big\rangle=

(233)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\mathfrak{e}\langle T^{\nu\rho}(x)\rangle\partial_{\alpha}\delta^{d+1}(x-y)-2\partial_{\mu}\Big\langle\frac{\delta(\mathfrak{e}\mathbf{T}^{\mu}{}_{\alpha}(x))}{\delta h_{\nu\rho}(y)}\Big\rangle+(\text{curv.})

where we used that the diffeomorphism Ward identity holds as an operator equation and absorbed all terms that vanish once the background is chosen to be flat on the right hand sides. The left hand sides are rewritten in terms of commutators by letting the derivatives act on the time ordering. For the first equation this yields, e.g.,

\displaystyle i\partial_{\mu}\big\langle\mathcal{T}\big(\mathfrak{e}\mathbf{T}^{\mu}{}_{\alpha}(x)\big)\mathfrak{e}T^{\nu}(y)\big\rangle-i\big\langle\mathcal{T}\partial_{\mu}\big(\mathfrak{e}

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}^{\mu}{}_{\alpha}(x)\big)\mathfrak{e}T^{\nu}(y)\big\rangle=

(234)

\displaystyle i\delta_{\mu}^{0}\big\langle[\mathfrak{e}\mathbf{T}^{\mu}{}_{\alpha}(x),\mathfrak{e}T^{\nu}(y)]\big\rangle\delta(x^{0}-y^{0})

with an analogous expression for the second equality. Evaluating on the flat background (
196
), we therefore obtain commutators between the stress-energy tensor components

\displaystyle i\delta_{\mu}^{0}\big\langle[\mathbf{T}^{\mu}{}_{\alpha}(x),T^{\nu}(y)]\big\rangle\delta(x^{0}-y^{0})

\displaystyle=\langle T^{\nu}(x)\rangle\partial_{\alpha}\delta^{d+1}(x-y)-\partial_{\mu}\Big\langle\frac{\delta(\mathfrak{e}\mathbf{T}^{\mu}{}_{\alpha}(x))}{\delta\tau_{\nu}(y)}\Big\rangle\Big|_{\text{flat }}

(235)

\displaystyle i\delta_{\mu}^{0}\big\langle[\mathbf{T}^{\mu}{}_{\alpha}(x),T^{\nu\rho}(y)]\big\rangle\delta(x^{0}-y^{0})

\displaystyle=\langle T^{\nu\rho}(x)\rangle\partial_{\alpha}\delta^{d+1}(x-y)-2\partial_{\mu}\Big\langle\frac{\delta(\mathfrak{e}\mathbf{T}^{\mu}{}_{\alpha}(x))}{\delta h_{\nu\rho}(y)}\Big\rangle\Big|_{\text{flat }}

(236)

with the left hand sides supported only on equal time slices. To isolate the corresponding pieces on the right hand sides we shall eventually integrate over a small interval
x^{0}\in[y^{0}-\epsilon,y^{0}+\epsilon]
and take the limit
\epsilon\to 0
. This effectively picks out only the contact contributions at equal times which form the commutator and also removes total time derivatives of contact terms since, e.g.,

\displaystyle\int_{y^{0}-\epsilon}^{y^{0}+\epsilon}\differential x^{0}\,\partial_{0}(\mathcal{O}(x)\delta^{d+1}(x-y))=(\mathcal{O}(x)\delta^{d+1}(x-y))\big|_{y^{0}-\epsilon}^{y^{0}+\epsilon}=0~.

(237)

For proceeding, it is convenient to combine the two equations (
235
) and (
236
) into a single one,

\displaystyle i\delta_{\mu}^{0}\big\langle[\mathbf{T}^{\mu}{}_{\alpha}(x),\mathbf{T}^{\nu}{}_{\beta}(y)]\big\rangle

\displaystyle\delta(x^{0}-y^{0})=\langle\mathbf{T}^{\nu}{}_{\beta}(x)\rangle\partial_{\alpha}\delta^{d+1}(x-y)

(238)

\displaystyle-\partial_{\mu}\Big\langle\frac{\delta(\mathfrak{e}\mathbf{T}^{\mu}{}_{\alpha}(x))}{\delta\tau_{\nu}(y)}\Big\rangle\Big|_{\text{flat }}\delta_{\beta}^{0}-2\partial_{\mu}\Big\langle\frac{\delta(\mathfrak{e}\mathbf{T}^{\mu}{}_{\alpha}(x))}{\delta h_{\nu\rho}(y)}\Big\rangle\Big|_{\text{flat }}\delta_{\rho}^{i}\delta_{\beta}^{j}\delta_{ij}~.

The second line of this expression is still somewhat impractical for evaluating the commutators. One may simplify it further by noting that the variations of the boost Ward identity (
231
) with respect to the background geometry is

\displaystyle\Big\langle\frac{\delta T^{\mu}(x)}{\delta\tau_{\alpha}(y)}\Big\rangle h_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta\mathcal{A}^{\mu}(x)}{\delta\tau_{\alpha}(y)}h_{\mu\nu}

(239)

\displaystyle\Big\langle\frac{\delta T^{\mu}(x)}{\delta h_{\alpha\beta}(y)}\Big\rangle h_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta\mathcal{A}^{\mu}(x)}{\delta h_{\alpha\beta}(y)}h_{\mu\nu}+\big(\mathcal{A}^{\mu}(x)-\langle T^{\mu}(x)\rangle\big)\delta_{(\mu}^{\alpha}\delta_{\nu)}^{\beta}\delta^{d+1}(x-y)

(240)

where we dropped all non-contact contributions by means of the time integration prescription. The anomaly
\mathcal{A}
typically vanishes when evaluated on a flat background which we shall assume here as well. However, its variations in general do not vanish. We may insert equations (
239
) and (
240
) in (
238
) to show that the brackets between the stress-energy tensor components that are relevant for the charge algebra are

\displaystyle i\big\langle[\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{0}(x)

\displaystyle,\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{0}(y)]\big\rangle=-\delta^{i}_{\mu}\delta_{\nu}^{0}\,\partial_{i}\frac{\delta\mathcal{A}^{\mu}(x)}{\delta\tau_{\nu}(y)}\Big|_{\textrm{flat}}

\displaystyle i\big\langle[\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{0}(x)

\displaystyle,\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{i}(y)]\big\rangle=\big(\langle\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{0}(y)\rangle\delta^{k}_{i}-\langle\mathbf{T}^{k}{}_{i}(x)\rangle\big)\partial_{k}\delta^{d}(x-y)-2\delta_{\rho i}\delta_{\nu}^{0}\delta_{\mu}^{j}\partial_{j}\frac{\delta\mathcal{A}^{\mu}(x)}{\delta h_{\nu\rho}(y)}\Big|_{\textrm{flat}}

(241)

\displaystyle i\big\langle[\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{i}(x)

\displaystyle,\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{j}(y)]\big\rangle=\langle\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{j}(x)\rangle\partial_{i}\delta^{d}(x-y)-\delta_{ij}\partial_{k}\big(\delta^{km}\langle\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{m}(x)\rangle\delta^{d}(x-y)\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\partial_{k}\langle\mathcal{S}^{k}{}_{i}{}^{0}{}_{j}(x,y)\rangle

where all the expressions are evaluated at
x^{0}=y^{0}
through the above-mentioned integration prescription. The operators

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{k}{}_{i}{}^{0}{}_{j}(x,y)=-2\frac{\delta T^{\mu\sigma}(x)}{\delta h_{\nu\rho}(y)}\delta_{\mu}^{k}\delta_{\nu}^{0}\delta_{\rho i}\delta_{\sigma j}

(242)

denote a part that is not uniquely fixed by symmetry and depends on the theory at hand. However, it still contains a universal part that is common to all theories. To see this we invoke antisymmetry of the last bracket which leads to the condition

\displaystyle-\langle\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{j}(x)\rangle

\displaystyle\partial_{i}^{y}\delta^{d}(x-y)-\delta_{ij}\partial_{k}\big(\delta^{km}\langle\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{m}(x)\rangle\delta^{d}(x-y)\big)+\partial_{k}\langle\mathcal{S}^{k}{}_{i}{}^{0}{}_{j}(x,y)\rangle

(243)

\displaystyle\overset{!}{=}\langle\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{i}(y)\rangle\partial_{j}\delta^{d}(x-y)+\delta_{ij}\partial_{k}^{y}\big(\delta^{km}\langle\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{m}(y)\rangle\delta^{d}(x-y)\big)-\partial_{k}^{y}\langle\mathcal{S}^{k}{}_{j}{}^{0}{}_{i}(y,x)\rangle~.

This shows that the operator
\mathcal{S}^{k}{}_{i}{}^{0}{}_{k}
is tied to the consistency of the bracket itself and may not be chosen at will without destroying the symmetry of the brackets. We may isolate the part associated to symmetry by writing the condition (
243
) as

\displaystyle\partial_{k}\Big(\langle\mathcal{S}^{k}{}_{i}{}^{0}{}_{k}(x,y)\rangle

\displaystyle-\delta_{ij}\big(\delta^{km}\langle\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{m}(x)\rangle\delta^{d}(x-y)\big)-\langle\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{i}(y)\rangle\delta^{k}_{j}\delta^{d}(x-y)\Big)

(244)

\displaystyle\overset{!}{=}-\partial_{k}^{y}\Big(\langle\mathcal{S}^{k}{}_{j}{}^{0}{}_{i}(y,x)\rangle-\delta_{ij}\big(\delta^{km}\langle\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{m}(y)\rangle\delta^{d}(x-y)\big)-\langle\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{j}(x)\rangle\delta^{k}_{i}\delta^{d}(x-y)\Big)~.

Thus, defining the operators

\displaystyle t^{k}{}_{i}{}^{0}{}_{j}(x,y):=\mathcal{S}^{k}{}_{i}{}^{0}{}_{j}(x,y)-\delta_{ij}\big(\delta^{km}\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{m}(x)\delta^{d}(x-y)\big)-\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{i}(y)\delta^{k}_{j}\delta^{d}(x-y)

(245)

the symmetry condition reduces to

\displaystyle\partial_{k}\langle t^{k}{}_{i}{}^{0}{}_{j}(x,y)\rangle+\partial_{k}^{y}\langle t^{k}{}_{j}{}^{0}{}_{i}(y,x)\rangle=0

(246)

and the functions
\langle t^{k}{}_{i}{}^{0}{}_{j}(x,y)\rangle
parametrize the additional theory- and counterterm dependence of this last bracket. We arrive at the stress-energy tensor equal time brackets of a Carroll quantum field theory on a flat background

\boxed{\begin{aligned} i[\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{0}(x)&,\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{0}(y)]=-\delta^{i}_{\mu}\delta_{\nu}^{0}\,\partial_{i}\frac{\delta\mathcal{A}^{\mu}(x)}{\delta\tau_{\nu}(y)}\Big|_{\textrm{flat}}\\
i[\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{0}(x)&,\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{i}(y)]=\big(\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{0}(y)\delta^{k}_{i}-\mathbf{T}^{k}{}_{i}(x)\big)\partial_{k}\delta^{d}(x-y)-2\delta_{\rho i}\delta_{\nu}^{0}\delta_{\mu}^{j}\partial_{j}\frac{\delta\mathcal{A}^{\mu}(x)}{\delta h_{\nu\rho}(y)}\Big|_{\textrm{flat}}\\
i[\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{i}(x)&,\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{j}(y)]=\big(\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{j}(x)\partial_{i}+\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{i}(y)\partial_{j}\big)\delta^{d}(x-y)+\partial_{k}t^{k}{}_{i}{}^{0}{}_{j}(x,y)\end{aligned}}

(247)

where the expectation values were left out since these identities hold for any state and are therefore elevated to operator identities. One can see that the Carroll boost anomaly may source non-trivial terms on the right hand sides of two of these brackets. In the two-dimensional example we shall see, however, that only the second of these survives. These brackets do not form an algebra as can be seen from the appearance of
\mathbf{T}^{k}{}_{i}
on the right hand side.

11.3
Carroll algebra from stress-energy tensor brackets

Let us use the just derived brackets to construct a representation of the Carroll algebra in terms of Hermitean operators. For this simple example, assume that no Carroll boost anomaly is present, i.e.,
\mathcal{A}^{\mu}(x)=0
. We directly promote (
198
) to operators and from evaluating (
247
) with suitable smearing we obtain the infinitely extended Carroll algebra
\mathfrak{isocarr}(d+1)
,

\displaystyle i[M_{f},M_{g}]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle i[P_{i},J_{jk}]

\displaystyle=2\delta_{i[j}P_{k]}

(248)

\displaystyle i[P_{i},M_{f}]

\displaystyle=M_{\partial_{i}f}

\displaystyle i[M_{j},J_{jk}]

\displaystyle=-M_{x_{j}\partial_{k}f-x_{k}\partial_{j}f}

(249)

\displaystyle i[J_{ij},J_{kl}]

\displaystyle=4\delta_{[i[k}J_{l]j]}~.

(250)

The operators
\partial_{k}t^{k}{}_{i}{}^{0}{}_{j}(x,y)
thereby have to satisfy

\displaystyle\int\differential^{d}x\,x_{[i}\partial_{m}t^{m}{}_{j]}{}^{0}{}_{k}(x,y)

\displaystyle=0

(251)

from requiring the bracket
i[J_{ij},J_{kl}]
to take the given form.

11.4
Carroll boost anomaly in 2d

If the Carroll theory is viewed in a holographic context, one expects a Carroll boost anomaly to be present
[
154
]
that is given by a local expression in terms of background quantities,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\mu}=\frac{c_{M}}{12\pi}\,h^{\mu\sigma}v^{\rho}\partial_{[\rho}\big(a_{\sigma]}+K\tau_{\sigma]}\big)~.

(252)

Here,
c_{M}
is some real number that plays the role of a central charge. To see this, we evaluate (
247
) leading to

\displaystyle i[\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{0}(x)

\displaystyle,\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{0}(y)]=0

(253)

\displaystyle i[\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{0}(x)

\displaystyle,\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{1}(y)]=\big(\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{0}(y)-\mathbf{T}^{1}{}_{1}(x)\big)\partial\delta(x-y)+\frac{c_{M}}{24\pi}\partial^{3}\delta(x-y)

(254)

\displaystyle i[\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{1}(x)

\displaystyle,\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{1}(y)]=\big(\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{1}(x)+\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{1}(y)\big)\partial\delta(x-y)+\partial t^{1}{}_{1}{}^{0}{}_{1}(x,y)

(255)

where we denote spatial derivatives by
\partial_{1}=:\partial
. Consider a conformally coupled theory without a Weyl anomaly such that the stress-energy tensor additionally obeys the trace Ward identity (
207
). We may construct the charges associated to symmetries of the chosen flat background which are given by the two-dimensional Carroll conformal Killing vectors, (
208
) for
d=1
. We get

\displaystyle Q_{(f,Y)}=\int dx\,\Big[Y(x)\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{1}(x)+\big(zx^{0}\partial Y(x)+f(x)\big)\mathbf{T}^{0}{}_{0}(x)\Big]

(256)

for two free functions
Y(x)
and
f(x)
, and it can be checked that the stress-energy tensor brackets imply

\displaystyle i[Q_{(f_{1},0)},Q_{(f_{2},0)}]

\displaystyle=0

(257)

\displaystyle i[Q_{(0,Y_{1})},Q_{(f_{2},0)}]

\displaystyle=Q_{(Y_{1}\partial f_{2}-zf_{2}\partial Y_{1},0)}-\frac{c_{M}}{24\pi}\int\differential x\,\partial^{3}Y_{1}f_{2}

(258)

\displaystyle i[Q_{(0,Y_{1})},Q_{(0,Y_{2})}]

\displaystyle=Q_{(0,Y_{1}\partial Y_{2}-Y_{2}\partial Y_{1})}+\int\differential x\differential y\,Y_{1}(x)Y_{2}(y)\partial t^{1}{}_{1}{}^{0}{}_{1}(x,y)~.

(259)

To bring this into a more recognizable form from the perspective of the Carroll CFT literature, one compactifies the spatial domain to a circle and defines Fourier modes

\displaystyle L_{n}:=Q_{(0,e^{-inx})}

\displaystyle M_{n}:=-Q_{(e^{-inx},0)}

(260)

such that

\displaystyle=(n-m)L_{n+m}

(261)

\displaystyle[L_{n},M_{m}]

\displaystyle=(zn-m)M_{n+m}+\frac{c_{M}}{12}n^{3}\delta_{n+m,0}

\displaystyle[M_{n},M_{m}]

\displaystyle=0~.

Here, we set the model dependent terms
t^{1}{}_{1}{}^{0}{}_{1}(x,y)
to zero for simplicity. For
z=1
, this is the centrally extended
\mathfrak{bms}_{3}
algebra (see also section
8
) which was previously derived from a bulk perspective as the asymptotic symmetry algebra of three-dimensional asymptotically flat Einstein gravity
[
45
]
where
c_{M}=3/G_{N}
. The derivation above shows that the central extension appearing in the mixed commutator is intimately tied to the Carroll boost anomaly and is therefore a quantum effect.

While we set the model-dependent terms
t^{1}{}_{1}{}^{0}{}_{1}(x,y)
to zero in this case, there are indeed situations where they contribute in a non-trivial way to the brackets. A notable example is the case where they are just given by the c-numbers

\displaystyle t^{1}{}_{1}{}^{0}{}_{1}=-\frac{c_{L}}{24\pi}\partial^{2}\delta(x-y)

(262)

which leads to the typical Virasoro-like central term in the
[L_{n},L_{m}]
bracket,

\displaystyle[L_{n},L_{m}]

\displaystyle=(n-m)L_{n+m}+\frac{c_{L}}{12}n^{3}\delta_{n+m,0}~.

(263)

From a bulk perspective this central charge is non-zero for the asymptotic symmetries of topologically massive gravity
[
28
]
while in an intrinsically Carroll context one example is the Carroll–Ising model, see
[
140
]
and references therein.

12
Examples of Carroll field theories

We proceed by presenting several examples for classical Carroll field theories. Since the literature on this subject is already quite extensive, we are only able to focus on a small cross section. In particular, we shall start with the simplest yet non-trivial case of Carroll scalar fields in subsection
12.1
, followed by a discussion of Carroll Maxwell fields in subsection
12.3
. The last subsection
12.4
discusses Carroll swiftons
[
114
]
, which is a class of interacting models.

In a canonical formulation, each of these models provides us with the classical version of the stress-energy tensor commutators (
247
) in the previous section. They hold, provided the theory is put on a flat background, which one may check explicitly in each case.

12.1
Minimally coupled scalar fields

Carroll scalar fields were first studied in
[
164
]
and further developed in their conformal aspects in 2d
[
24
]
and higher dimensions
[
39
,
95
,
203
]
. We shall also use them as a toy model for coupling to a Carroll black hole background
[
10
]
in chapter
6
.

In fact there are currently two inequivalent scalar theories known that are referred to as “magnetic” and “electric”. This terminology is borrowed from the respective case in electrodynamics and refers to two different ways of taking the Carroll limit starting from a Lorentzian scalar field. In the following, it is shown how one arrives at these limits, for which we find it most instructive to proceed in a Hamiltonian formulation. Additionally, we shall put the theory on an arbitrary background first which makes it especially easy to read off the canonical expressions for energy and momentum density.

Starting from the Lorentzian scalar field

\displaystyle I_{\textrm{Lorentz}}[\phi]=-\frac{1}{2}\int\differential^{d+1}x\,\sqrt{G}\,\Big(G^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial_{\nu}\phi+V(\phi)\Big)

(264)

with some potential
V(\phi)
we parametrize the Lorentzian metric
G_{\mu\nu}
with ADM-like variables by

\displaystyle G_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}N^{i}N_{i}-c^{2}N^{2}&N_{i}\\
N_{i}&g_{ij}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle G^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{1}{c^{2}N^{2}}&\frac{N^{i}}{c^{2}N^{2}}\\
\frac{N^{i}}{c^{2}N^{2}}&g^{ij}-\frac{N^{i}N^{j}}{c^{2}N^{2}}\end{pmatrix}

(265)

where, in contrast to the usual ADM split, we absorbed a factor of
c
into the shift vector
N^{i}
for convenience. The volume element in this case becomes
\sqrt{G}=cN\sqrt{g}
and
g^{ij}
is defined as the inverse of
g_{ij}
. Performing a Legendre transformation, one finds the canonical momentum

\displaystyle\pi=\frac{\sqrt{g}}{Nc}\big(\dot{\phi}-N^{i}\partial_{i}\phi)

(266)

and the scalar field action in Hamiltonian form for a general background

\displaystyle I_{\textrm{Lorentz}}[\phi,\pi]=\int\differential t\,\differential^{d}x\,\big(\pi\dot{\phi}-N\mathcal{H}-N^{i}\mathcal{H}_{i}\big)~.

(267)

Here, we denote
t
-derivatives with a dot and set
x^{0}=t
as compared to the previous section. The energy and momentum densities read explicitly

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}=\frac{c}{2\sqrt{g}}\pi^{2}+\frac{c\sqrt{g}}{2}\big(g^{ij}\partial_{i}\phi\partial_{j}\phi+V\big)\qquad\qquad\mathcal{H}_{i}=\pi\partial_{i}\phi

(268)

and the fields
\phi(x)
,
\pi(x)
obey a canonical equal-time Poisson bracket
\{\phi(x),\pi(y)\}=\delta^{d}(x-y)
. For taking a limit
c\to 0
the action (
267
) is one viable starting point, albeit not a very interesting one: The energy density just vanishes in the limit. One may, however, perform a canonical transformation before taking the limit. If this canonical transformation depends on
c
, it transforms between inequivalent starting points as far as taking the limit is concerned: Two theories related by a canonical transformation before the limit do not have to be related by one afterwards.

To give the two prominent examples, the electric action is obtained by performing the transformation
\phi\to\phi\sqrt{c}
,
\pi\to\pi/\sqrt{c}
which in the limit yields

\displaystyle I_{E}[\phi,\pi]=\int\differential t\,\differential^{d}x\,\big(\pi\dot{\phi}-N\mathcal{H}_{E}-N^{i}\mathcal{H}_{i}\big)\approx\frac{1}{2}\int\differential^{d+1}x\,\mathfrak{e}\big(v^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\phi\big)^{2}

(269)

with the electric energy density

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{E}=\frac{\pi^{2}}{2\sqrt{g}}~.

(270)

In the second equality, we integrated out
\pi
and translated the ADM variables back into Carroll metric data by

\displaystyle h_{\mu\nu}=\lim_{c\to 0}G_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle-v^{\mu}v^{\nu}=\lim_{c\to 0}c^{2}G^{\mu\nu}~.

(271)

We thus choose the Carroll ADM variables as

\displaystyle v^{\mu}=\Big(-\frac{1}{N},\frac{N^{i}}{N}\Big)

\displaystyle\tau_{\mu}=(N,0)

\displaystyle h_{\mu\nu}=\begin{pmatrix}N^{i}N_{i}&N_{i}\\
N_{i}&g_{ij}\end{pmatrix}

(272)

such that
h^{00}=h^{i0}=0
,
h^{ij}=g^{ij}
and
\mathfrak{e}=N\sqrt{g}
. One can see that on a flat background where
v=-\partial_{t}
this action only keeps the part of the Lorentzian free scalar that involves the temporal derivatives. This is why it is sometimes also referred to as the “timelike” action
[
39
,
95
,
203
]
.

Alternatively, doing the opposite transformation
\phi\to\phi/\sqrt{c}
,
\pi\to\pi\sqrt{c}
yields the magnetic action

\displaystyle I_{M}[\phi,\pi]=\int\differential t\,\differential^{d}x\,\big(\pi\dot{\phi}-N\mathcal{H}_{M}-N^{i}\mathcal{H}_{i}\big)

(273)

with energy density

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{M}=\frac{\sqrt{g}}{2}\big(g^{ij}\partial_{i}\phi\partial_{j}\phi+V\big)

(274)

and where we assumed that the coupling constants in the potential
V
scale in such a way that it remains finite in the limit. In this case one cannot integrate out the momentum anymore since it only features linearly in the action. It acts as a Lagrange multiplier setting the time dependence of
\phi
to zero. One may bring this action into a covariant form by pulling out the density weight of
\pi
and defining a scalar field
\chi
by
-\sqrt{g}\chi=\pi
. Using the same ADM split as above one obtains

\displaystyle I_{M}[\chi,\phi]=\int\differential^{d+1}x\,\mathfrak{e}\,\Big(\chi v^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\phi-\frac{1}{2}h^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial_{\nu}\phi-\frac{1}{2}V(\phi)\Big)

(275)

which can be checked to be Carroll boost invariant under the infinitesimal version of the transformations (
27
) together with

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}\chi=-h^{\mu\nu}\lambda_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\phi~.

(276)

Thus, the magnetic action is a two-field realization of a Carroll field theory. The field
\chi
is an essential part of the action and ensures Carroll boost invariance.

12.2
Non-minimally coupled scalar fields

Once written in a second order formulation, one may explore a few further possibilities to couple the two types of scalar fields just discussed to a Carroll geometry. We shall restrict to the minimal-torsion geometries from section
7.2
which are obtained from a Cartan perspective by “integrating out” the boost- and rotation connections. As we shall see in section
14.2
these are typical off-shell geometries of magnetic Carroll gravity. The independent background fields are
\tau_{\mu}
,
h_{\mu\nu}
and
C_{\mu\nu}
which are used to define a metric compatible affine connection (
137
). Under the assumption of maintaining local Carroll boost invariance, the fields
\phi
and
\chi
of the magnetic action may for example be coupled as

\displaystyle I_{M}^{\xi,\eta}[\chi,\phi]=\int\differential^{d+1}x\,\mathfrak{e}\,\Big(\chi(v^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\phi+2\eta\xi K\phi^{\eta-1})-\frac{1}{2}h^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial_{\nu}\phi+\xi\phi^{\eta}\mathcal{R}\Big)

\displaystyle\xi,\eta\in\mathbb{R}~.

(277)

Here,
\mathcal{R}[\tau_{\mu},h_{\mu\nu},C_{\mu\nu}]=h^{\mu\nu}\big(R_{\mu\nu}-v^{\rho}\tau_{\sigma}R^{\sigma}{}_{\mu\rho\nu}\big)
is the Carroll curvature scalar associated to the connection (
137
). Under local Carroll boosts it transforms as (
141
). Due to the coupling to
C_{\mu\nu}
this theory falls outside the class of models we restricted to initially. Indeed, the corresponding response function as defined in section
10.2
reads

\displaystyle\Psi^{\mu\nu}=\frac{2}{\mathfrak{e}}\frac{\delta I_{M}^{\xi,\eta}}{\delta C_{\mu\nu}}=4\phi^{\eta}K^{\mu\nu}+4h^{\mu\nu}v^{\alpha}(\partial_{\alpha}+\tau_{\alpha}K)\phi^{\eta}~.

(278)

Once coupled to magnetic Carroll gravity (see section
14.2
), this theory sources intrinsic torsion since the field
C_{\mu\nu}
then becomes dynamical and introduces an equation of motion that couples
K^{\mu\nu}
to
\Psi^{\mu\nu}
. For
\eta=2
a similar action was proposed in
[
39
]
where the field
C_{\mu\nu}
translates to an additional Lagrange multiplier
\chi_{\mu\nu}
that is part of the dynamical field content. In the present case, however, this field is part of the background structure and thus should not be varied when computing equations of motion. An interesting open question is whether the action (
277
) can be rendered Weyl-invariant for some choices of
\xi
and
\eta
.

As a final example we also propose a non-minimally coupled version of the electric scalar (
269
),

\displaystyle I_{E}^{\xi}[\phi]=\frac{1}{2}\int\differential^{d+1}x\,\mathfrak{e}\,\Big(v^{\mu}(\partial_{\mu}-\xi W_{\mu})\phi\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle\xi\in\mathbb{R}

(279)

where

\displaystyle W_{\mu}=\frac{K}{d}\tau_{\mu}+\frac{1}{z}a_{\mu}~.

(280)

The action can be checked to be Carroll boost invariant and is additionally invariant under anisotropic Weyl transformations of subsection
9
for
\xi=\frac{z-d}{2}
with the scalar field transforming as
\delta_{\rho}\phi=\xi\rho\phi
. From its transformation property
\delta_{\rho}W_{\mu}=\partial_{\mu}\rho
one observes that
W_{\mu}
behaves like a Weyl connection. Such a field was previously introduced in the Carroll hydrodynamics context
[
89
]
. In this case there is no coupling to the field
C_{\mu\nu}
.

12.3
Carroll–Maxwell fields

A Carroll limit of the Maxwell field was first investigated in
[
108
]
and further developed in
[
164
]
. Like in the case of the free scalar field, there are again at least two distinct contractions which project out either the magnetic or the electric components of the electromagnetic field, hence the name. Proceeding again on an arbitrary background parametrized by (
265
) we start from the Maxwell action

\displaystyle I_{\textrm{Lorentz}}[A]=-\frac{1}{4}\int\differential^{d+1}x\sqrt{G}\,G^{\mu\alpha}G^{\nu\beta}F_{\mu\nu}F_{\alpha\beta}

(281)

with
F_{\mu\nu}=\partial_{\mu}A_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}A_{\mu}
. Performing a Legendre transformation in the sector
(A_{i},\pi^{i})
leads to its Hamiltonian form
10
10

10

This is a practical shortcut to the fully-fledged canonical analysis that already solves the primary constraint
\pi^{t}\approx 0
, see chapter 19 of
[
163
]
.

\displaystyle I_{\textrm{Lorentz}}[\pi^{i},A_{i},A_{t}]=\int\differential t\,\differential^{d}x\,\Big(\pi^{i}\dot{A}_{i}-N\mathcal{H}-N^{i}\mathcal{H}_{i}+A_{t}\mathcal{G}\Big)

(282)

where
\mathcal{G}=\partial_{i}\pi^{i}
is the Gauss constraint and
A_{t}
the corresponding Lagrange multiplier. Energy and momentum densities are given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}=\frac{c}{2\sqrt{g}}\pi^{i}\pi_{i}+\frac{c\sqrt{g}}{4}g^{ik}g^{jl}F_{ij}F_{kl}

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{i}=F_{ij}\pi^{j}

(283)

with the canonical variables satisfying the Poisson bracket
\{A_{i}(x),\pi^{j}(y)\}=\delta_{i}^{j}\delta^{d}(x-y)
.

The electric limit is obtained by first performing the canonical transformation
A_{i}\to\sqrt{c}A_{i}
,
\pi^{i}\to\pi^{i}/\sqrt{c}
and then sending
c\to 0
which leads to

\displaystyle I_{E}[A_{i},\pi^{i},A_{t}]=\int\differential t\,\differential^{d}x\,\Big(\pi^{i}\dot{A}_{i}-N\mathcal{H}_{E}-N^{i}\mathcal{H}_{i}+A_{t}\mathcal{G}\Big)\approx\frac{1}{2}\int\differential^{d+1}x\mathfrak{e}\,\big(v^{\mu}F_{\mu\nu}\big)^{2}~.

(284)

In the second equality we again integrated out the momenta
\pi^{i}
and used that
v^{\mu}F_{\mu\nu}
can be squared in a Carroll boost invariant way using
h^{\mu\nu}
because it is a transverse quantity. The translation between ADM and second order variables is again given by (
272
). The energy density in this case reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{E}=\frac{\pi^{i}\pi_{i}}{2\sqrt{g}}=\frac{\sqrt{g}}{2}E^{i}E_{i}

(285)

which only involves the electric field. The translation into second order geometric variables works in a similar fashion as in the scalar case described around equation (
271
).

For the magnetic contraction we perform the converse transformation
A_{i}\to A_{i}/\sqrt{c}
,
\pi^{i}\to\pi^{i}\sqrt{c}
and obtain the action

\displaystyle I_{M}[A_{i},\pi^{i},A_{t}]=\int\differential t\,\differential^{d}x\,\Big(\pi^{i}\dot{A}_{i}-N\mathcal{H}_{M}-N^{i}\mathcal{H}_{i}+A_{t}\mathcal{G}\Big)

(286)

with

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{M}=\frac{\sqrt{g}}{4}g^{ik}g^{jl}F_{ij}F_{kl}~.

(287)

Just like for the magnetic scalar field we again cannot integrate out the canonical momenta which instead act as Lagrange multipliers. Together with the Gauss constraint they set the electric field to zero on-shell. In four spacetime dimensions we may write the energy density as
\mathcal{H}_{M}=\sqrt{g}B^{i}B_{i}/2
which, as already mentioned, consists only of the magnetic part.

12.4
Scalar Carroll swiftons

Following
[
114
]
we now come to a class of interacting models called Carroll swiftons. In a sense they are a generalization of the electric/magnetic scalar- and Maxwell models in that their actions are at least of second order in both temporal and spatial derivatives. We first focus on the scalar case and then come to the electromagnetic case in subsection
12.5
.

Consider a “bi-scalar” model that couples two scalar fields
\phi,\chi
with canonically normalized kinetic terms and coupling constant
g
, thereby generalizing the model introduced in
[
38
]
(see their eq. (2.1) as well as
[
174
]
) to any Carroll background. Its action in covariant form

\displaystyle I_{\textrm{bi-scalar}}[\phi,\chi]=\frac{1}{2}\int\differential^{d+1}x\,\mathfrak{e}\,\left(\left(v^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\phi\right)^{2}+\left(v^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\chi\right)^{2}+\alpha\,B_{\mu}B^{\mu}\right)

(288)

contains the manifestly transverse covariant vector

B_{\nu}\equiv v^{\mu}\left(\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial_{\nu}\chi-\partial_{\mu}\chi\partial_{\nu}\phi\right)\equiv 2v^{\mu}\partial_{[\mu}\phi\partial_{\nu]}\chi\,

(289)

and a coupling constant
\alpha
. Because
B_{\nu}
involves simultaneously time and spatial derivatives, the model allows propagation off the Carroll lightcone (see below). Antisymmetry of the coefficient of
v^{\mu}
in
B_{\nu}
is crucial for transversality, which would not hold if
\phi=\chi
since then
B_{\nu}
would be identically zero. By contrast, two distinct scalar fields can “mutualize” their derivatives in a non-trivial way through the identically transverse vector
B_{\nu}
. Each scalar field is crucial for its mutualistic partner in this construction.

The Hamiltonian for this model can be derived straightforwardly, noting that the above Lagrangian density can be rewritten in terms of a doublet
\phi^{A}\equiv(\phi,\chi)
as

\mathcal{L}=\frac{N\sqrt{g}}{2}H_{AB}(v^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}{\phi}^{A})(v^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}{\phi}^{B})\quad\,\;\;H_{AB}=\begin{pmatrix}1+\alpha(\partial\chi)^{2}&-\alpha\partial\phi\cdot\partial\chi\\
-\alpha\partial\phi\cdot\partial\chi&1+\alpha(\partial\phi)^{2}\end{pmatrix}

(290)

where we used the notation
\partial\phi^{A}\cdot\partial\phi^{B}\equiv g^{ij}\partial_{i}\phi^{A}\partial_{j}\phi^{B}
and the same ADM split as in the previous subsections (
272
). The inverse matrix
H^{AB}
is
H^{AB}=\frac{1}{D}\left(\delta^{AB}+\alpha(\partial\phi^{A})\cdot(\partial\phi^{B})\right)
where
D
is the determinant

\displaystyle D=1+\alpha(\partial\phi)^{2}+\alpha(\partial\chi)^{2}+\alpha^{2}\left((\partial\phi)^{2}(\partial\chi)^{2}-(\partial\phi\cdot\partial\chi)^{2}\right)~,

(291)

which obeys
D\geq 1
for
\alpha\geq 0
, implying that the field space metric
H_{AB}
has Euclidean signature. The Hamiltonian is
N\mathcal{H}+N^{i}\mathcal{H}_{i}
where the energy and momentum densities are

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{g}}\,H^{AB}\,\pi_{A}\pi_{B}

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{i}=\pi_{A}\partial_{i}\phi^{A}~.

(292)

We note that
\mathcal{H}
is bilinear in the conjugate momenta
\pi_{A}
. Since the quadratic form
H^{AB}\,\pi_{A}\pi_{B}
is positive definite, the energy density is bounded from below by zero.

The equations of motion following from the mutualistic scalar action (
288
)

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}\Big(\mathfrak{e}\,\big(v^{\mu}v^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}\chi-\alpha h^{\alpha\nu}B_{\nu}v^{\mu}\partial_{\alpha}\phi+\alpha h^{\mu\nu}B_{\nu}v^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\phi\big)\Big)

\displaystyle=0

(293a)

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}\Big(\mathfrak{e}\,\big(v^{\mu}v^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}\phi+\alpha h^{\alpha\nu}B_{\nu}v^{\mu}\partial_{\alpha}\chi-\alpha h^{\mu\nu}B_{\nu}v^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\chi\big)\Big)

\displaystyle=0

(293b)

are coupled non-linear partial differential equations, and we have not tried to devise a general method to solve them.

On a flat background the scalar swifton model is not just Carroll invariant but also exhibits an anisotropic scaling symmetry (see section
10.1
). This is readily seen by computing an infinitesimal Weyl transformation

\displaystyle\delta_{\rho}I_{\textrm{bi-scalar}}=\frac{1}{2}\int\differential^{d+1}x\,\mathfrak{e}\Big[\big((v^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\phi)^{2}

\displaystyle+(v^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\chi)^{2}\big)(d-z+2\Delta)\rho

(294)

\displaystyle+\alpha B^{\mu}B_{\mu}(d-z-2+4\Delta)\rho+2\mathcal{K}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\rho\Big]

where we used the transformations of section
9
together with

\displaystyle\delta_{\rho}\phi=\Delta\rho\phi

\displaystyle\delta_{\rho}\chi=\Delta\rho\chi

(295)

for
\Delta\in\mathbb{R}
. The vector
\mathcal{K}^{\mu}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{\mu}=v^{\mu}\Delta\big(\phi v^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\phi+\chi v^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\chi+

\displaystyle\alpha\phi B^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\chi-\alpha\chi B^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\phi\big)

(296)

\displaystyle+\alpha\Delta B^{\mu}\big(\chi v^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\phi-\phi v^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\chi\big)~.

(297)

Thus, for the choice

\displaystyle\Delta=1

\displaystyle z=d+2

(298)

this action is left invariant by constant Weyl rescalings. According to the discussion in section
10.1
, the term
\mathcal{K}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\rho
prevents the stress-energy tensor from satisfying the generalized traceless condition and poses an obstruction for the scale symmetry to lift to a conformal symmetry. Indeed, we find on-shell

\displaystyle(d+2)T^{\mu}\tau_{\mu}+T^{\mu\rho}h_{\mu\rho}=-\frac{1}{\mathfrak{e}}\partial_{\mu}(\mathfrak{e}\mathcal{K}^{\mu})

(299)

where the components
T^{\mu}
and
T^{\mu\rho}
are given after the definition (
181
) by

\displaystyle T^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}v^{\mu}\big(\left(v^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}\phi\right)^{2}+\left(v^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}\chi\right)^{2}+\alpha\,B_{\nu}B^{\nu}\big)

(300)

\displaystyle T^{\mu\rho}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}h^{\mu\rho}\big(\left(v^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\phi\right)^{2}+\left(v^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\chi\right)^{2}+\alpha\,B_{\nu}B^{\nu}\big)-\alpha B^{\mu}B^{\rho}

(301)

\displaystyle\qquad-2v^{(\rho}h^{\mu)\beta}\partial_{\beta}\phi v^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\phi-2v^{(\rho}h^{\mu)\beta}\partial_{\beta}\chi v^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\chi+\alpha v^{(\rho}h^{\mu)\beta}B^{\alpha}(\partial_{\alpha}\phi\partial_{\beta}\chi-\partial_{\alpha}\chi\partial_{\beta}\phi)~.

The scale current is constructed by

\displaystyle j^{\mu}=\mathfrak{e}\mathbf{T}^{\mu}{}_{\nu}\xi^{\nu}_{\textrm{D}}+\mathfrak{e}\mathcal{K}^{\mu}

(302)

and satisfies
\partial_{\mu}j^{\mu}=0
on-shell provided
\xi_{\textrm{D}}
generates a scale transformation for the given background., i.e.,
\mathcal{L}_{\xi_{\textrm{D}}}\tau_{\mu}=(d+2)\tau_{\mu}
,
\mathcal{L}_{\xi_{\textrm{D}}}h_{\mu\nu}=2h_{\mu\nu}
.

12.4.1
Taylor expanded Swiftons

To gain insight into the solutions of the scalar swifton model, we use perturbative methods. A soluble case arises when one of the scalar fields, say
\chi=\chi_{\textrm{\tiny BG}}+{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{2})
, is a background field in addition to the geometric background (which we assume to be static) and the other one,
\phi=\epsilon\,\varphi
, is a small fluctuation on top (
\epsilon\ll 1
). To leading order we obtain from (
293
) the background solution

\chi_{\textrm{\tiny BG}}=\chi_{0}(x^{i})+\chi_{1}(x^{i})\,t

(303)

where we used adapted coordinates such that
v=f(x^{i})\,\partial_{t}
. For simplicity, we set
\chi_{0}=0
and
\chi_{1}=1
.

Inserting this solution back into the mutualistic scalar action (
288
) yields a quadratic action

I^{\textrm{Taylor}}_{\textrm{Swifton}}[\varphi]=\frac{1}{2}\int\differential^{d+1}x\,\mathfrak{e}\,\Big(\epsilon^{2}\,\big(v^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\varphi\big)^{2}+\epsilon^{2}\,\alpha\,h^{\mu\nu}\,(\partial_{\mu}\varphi)(\partial_{\nu}\varphi)+1\Big)

(304)

for the fluctuations
\varphi
on such a background.

To get hyperbolic equations of motion, we need a negative coupling constant
\alpha
, which may seem at odds with positivity of energy density. However, even for negative
\alpha
, the energy density remains positive as long as the coupling constant obeys the inequality

\displaystyle\alpha>-1/[(\partial\phi)^{2}+(\partial\chi)^{2}]~.

(305)

In our perturbative context where both
(\partial\phi)^{2}
and
(\partial\chi)^{2}
are small the bound on the coupling constant is very weak.

Up to a cosmological constant term and conventions, the action (
304
) coincides precisely with the one proposed in
[
90
]
, i.e., it combines electric and magnetic Carroll scalar field actions to a single electromagnetic one. However, as opposed to
[
90
]
, the full theory maintains Carroll boost invariance.

12.4.2
Multi-scalar models

The bi-scalar model (
288
) can be generalized by the same “mutualization trick” to a multi-scalar theory. For instance, with three scalars we have the action [
\phi^{A}\equiv(\phi,\chi,\psi)
]

I_{\textrm{tri-scalar}}[\phi^{A}]=\frac{1}{2}\int\differential^{d+1}x\,\mathfrak{e}\,\Bigg(\sum_{A=1}^{3}\left(v^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\phi^{A}\right)^{2}+\alpha\,B_{\mu\nu}B^{\mu\nu}\Bigg)

(306)

with the transverse tensor

B_{\mu\nu}\equiv v^{\rho}B_{\mu\nu\rho}\qquad\qquad B_{\mu\nu\rho}\equiv\partial_{[\mu}\phi\partial_{\nu}\chi\partial_{\rho]}\psi\,.

(307)

The interaction term in (
306
) is of order six in the derivatives, but only quadratic in the time derivatives.

Yet another generalization is by imposing internal
O(N)
symmetry and working with the multiplett
\phi^{A}
,
A=1,...,N
. After defining the transverse tensor

\displaystyle B^{AB}_{\mu}=v^{\nu}(\partial_{\nu}\phi^{A}\partial_{\mu}\phi^{B}-\partial_{\nu}\phi^{B}\partial_{\mu}\phi^{A})

(308)

we can write an
O(N)
-vector model

\displaystyle I_{O(N)}[\phi^{A}]=\frac{1}{2}\int\differential^{d+1}x\,\mathfrak{e}\,\Big((v^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\phi^{A})^{2}+\frac{\alpha}{2}h^{\mu\nu}B^{AB}_{\mu}B^{AB}_{\nu}\Big)

(309)

which is still of order four in spatial derivatives and order two in time derivatives. Once written in this formulation one may perform a large-
N
expansion and try to quantize the theory in that limit
[
93
]
. See also
[
172
]
for an example of this procedure in the context of fractonic systems.

12.5
Electromagnetic Carroll Swiftons

A non-trivially interacting electromagnetic model can also be constructed using the same idea as in the scalar case. Consider

C_{\mu\nu\rho}\equiv v^{\sigma}C_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\qquad\qquad C_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\equiv F_{[\mu\nu}F_{\rho\sigma]}

(310)

where
F_{\mu\nu}=\partial_{\mu}{A}_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}{A}_{\mu}
is the electromagnetic field. This is a transverse three-form,
C_{\mu\nu\rho}\nu^{\rho}=0
. Hence, again its square
C_{\mu\nu\rho}C^{\mu\nu\rho}
is well-defined.

The electromagnetic Carroll swifton action

\boxed{\phantom{\Big(}I_{\textrm{\tiny EM--swifton}}=\frac{1}{2}\int\differential^{d+1}x\,\mathfrak{e}\,\Big(\big(v^{\mu}F_{\mu\nu}\big)^{2}+\alpha\,C_{\mu\nu\rho}C^{\mu\nu\rho}\Big)\phantom{\Big)}}

(311)

yields the equations of motion

\partial_{\lambda}\Big(\mathfrak{e}\big(v^{\mu}v^{[\sigma}h^{\lambda]\nu}+\alpha v^{\nu}C^{\sigma\lambda\mu}+\alpha v^{[\lambda}C^{\sigma]\mu\nu}\big)F_{\mu\nu}\Big)=0

(312)

that are again coupled non-linear partial differential equations.

The energy density is given by a similar expression as in the scalar case

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{g}}H_{ij}\pi^{i}\pi^{j}

(313)

where
H_{ij}
is the inverse of the symmetric matrix

\displaystyle H^{ij}=g^{ij}+\frac{2\alpha}{3}\Big(g^{il}g^{mk}g^{nj}+\frac{1}{2}g^{ij}g^{lk}g^{mn}\Big)F_{lm}F_{kn}~.

(314)

In four dimensions the determinant of the latter is given by
D=g^{-1}(1+2\alpha B^{2}/3)
with
B^{2}=g^{ik}g^{jl}F_{kl}F_{ij}/2
which makes
\mathcal{H}
positive definite if
\alpha>-3/(2B^{2})
, allowing in particular again negative values of
\alpha
in a perturbative context.

For the special case of a four-dimensional flat Carroll background we linearize the equations of motion (
312
) with a constant electric background field plus fluctuations on top,

F_{ij}\equiv\epsilon\,\mathcal{B}_{ij}\qquad\qquad F_{ti}=-\delta_{i}^{x}\,E-\epsilon\,\mathcal{E}_{i}\,.

(315)

The linearized equations of motion are solved by plane waves

\mathcal{E}_{i}=A_{i}e^{i(k_{y}y+k_{z}z-\omega t)}\qquad\mathcal{B}_{ij}=B\,\big(\delta_{i}^{y}\delta_{j}^{z}-\delta_{i}^{z}\delta_{j}^{y}\big)e^{i(k_{y}y+k_{z}z-\omega t)}

(316)

subject to the dispersion relation
\omega^{2}=c_{\textrm{\tiny{eff}}}^{2}\,(k_{y}^{2}+k_{z}^{2})
with the effective speed of light

\displaystyle c_{\textrm{\tiny{eff}}}^{2}=-\frac{2\alpha}{3}\,E^{2}~,

(317)

the transversality condition
k_{y}A_{y}+k_{z}A_{z}=0=A_{x}
, and the normalization
B^{2}=A_{y}^{2}+A_{z}^{2}
. As in the bi-scalar model, we need negative
\alpha
to have hyperbolic equations of motion with a real effective propagation speed for the swiftons.

Denoting
J_{1}=(v^{\mu}{F}_{\mu\nu})^{2}
and
J_{2}=C_{\mu\nu\rho}C^{\mu\nu\rho}
, this model can be generalized to Lagrange densities of the form
\mathcal{L}=\mathfrak{e}\,f(J_{1},J_{2})
. It would be interesting to determine which choice of
f
leads to the duality-invariant theory constructed in
[
72
]
.

Finally, we mention that the electromagnetic model can be directly coupled to the bi-scalar action (
288
). Indeed, in terms of the complex field
\Phi=\phi+i\chi
the bi-scalar action in (
288
) takes the simple form

\displaystyle I_{\textrm{bi-scalar}}[\Phi]=\frac{1}{2}\int\differential^{d+1}x\,\mathfrak{e}\,\Big(|v^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\Phi|^{2}+\alpha B^{\mu}B_{\mu}\Big)

(318)

where

B_{\mu}=(v^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\Phi^{\ast}\partial_{\nu}\Phi-v^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\Phi\partial_{\nu}\Phi^{\ast})/2i
.
Thus, the global
U(1
) symmetry
\Phi\to e^{i\alpha}\Phi
of the model can be made local by introducing standard minimal coupling
\partial_{\mu}\rightarrow\partial_{\mu}-iA_{\mu}
.

Chapter 4
Carroll gravity

\epigraphfontsize

“Jazz isn’t dead. It just smells funny.”

— Frank Zappa
aaaaaaaaaa

So far, we worked on fixed Carroll background geometries that provided the stage for investigating specific Carroll field theories. One of the strongest motivations for this was the holographic principle, which, applied to asymptotically flat spacetimes, predicts a duality between quantum gravity and some conformal Carroll field theory. While we already had a brief detour into the quantum world, defining Carroll quantum field theories from scratch turns out to be a thorny problem even for the simplest Carroll field theories like the electric or magnetic scalar field
[
96
,
93
]
, and the last word on this matter might be far from being spoken.

We now switch gears and come to gravitational theories built on Carroll symmetries. This takes us a step away from holographic applications but instead opens up a different angle for understanding quantum gravity in a simplified setting. In section
13
, we shall propose a specific limit, coined the
tantum gravity limit
which is understood to be directly taken in a putative fully-fledged theory of quantum gravity. This limit is defined such that it preserves specific semi-classical features like a sensible notion of thermodynamics and a finite Schwarzschild radius. The classical approximation of this limit is described by magnetic Carroll gravity, which we shall introduce in section
14
. In section
15
, we then focus on a class of two-dimensional Carroll gravity theories which provide a useful arena for investigating Carroll black hole solutions together with their thermodynamics. The main original contributions to this chapter are
[
113
,
112
]
.

13
Corners of the Bronstein cube

So far, quantum gravity is best understood in scaling limits where either Newton’s constant vanishes (quantum field theory) or Planck’s constant vanishes (Einstein gravity). As a further simplification, a large speed of light is often assumed (Galilean limit), which suppresses particle creation in quantum field theories and reduces Einstein gravity to Newton gravity.

Figure 3:
Bronstein cube with tantum gravity limit highlighted as TG and its antipodal as TG
∗
.

These various limits can be captured by the Bronstein cube, depicted in Fig.
3
. The three axes in the Bronstein cube are Newton’s constant
G_{N}
, Planck’s constant
\hbar
, and the inverse vacuum speed of light
c^{-1}
. Unlike traditional renderings, we have compactified all axes so that the faces, edges, and corners of the cube correspond to zero or infinite values of the corresponding coupling constants. Quantum gravity fills the volume of the cube, and each of the limiting cases corresponds to a face, a double-scaling limit to an edge, and a triple-scaling limit to a corner. This means that we have (at least)
6+12+8=26
limiting theories of quantum gravity: the latter are listed in Table
1
, where the entries
0
,
1
, or
\infty
mean that the corresponding quantity is zero, finite, or infinite.

The first nine limiting theories have common acronyms,
i.e.
GM stands for Galilean mechanics, SR for special relativity, QM for quantum mechanics, NG for Newtonian gravity, QFT for quantum field theory, GR for general relativity, GQG for Galilean quantum gravity, CM for Carroll mechanics and CQM for Carroll quantum mechanics. The limits 10-24 have no common names yet. The final two entries are tantum gravity (denoted as
TG
) and its dual limit (denoted as TG
∗
).
