Table 1:
26 limits of quantum gravity.

It can be rewarding to go through the 26 entries in Table
1
and specify how the limits are taken, which combinations of
G_{N}
,
\hbar
, and
c
remain finite, which sector of quantum gravity this theory maintains, which physical effects can be described by the limiting theory,
etc
. In this manner, one may find more than one way of taking such limits for the same entry in the Table, so there could be more than 26 limiting theories, though not of equal interest for applications. Extending the Bronstein cube by additional axes (cosmological constant
\Lambda
, Boltzmann constant
k_{\textrm{\tiny B}}

[
91
]
, number of degrees of freedom
N

[
195
]
,
etc
.) can also be fruitful but is unnecessary for our purposes.

Here we focus on the highlighted entry in Table
1
or, equivalently, the corner in Fig.
3
labeled “TG,” corresponding to infinite Planck’s constant and vanishing Newton’s constant and speed of light. We call this theory “tantum gravity,” where the Latin word “tantum” means “that much!” — a fitting answer to the question “quantum?”.

However, it is not obvious that such a limit leads to a gravity theory. After all, we send Newton’s constant to zero. To investigate this issue, we need to be more specific about these limits. We employ an inductive approach.

Our main motivation comes from black hole thermodynamics, which lies at the heart of many quantum gravity mysteries, including the information paradox, black hole microstates, and the holographic principle (see,
e.g.
,
[
237
,
219
,
183
,
145
,
236
,
207
,
189
,
201
,
13
]
and references therein). Thus, we check under which conditions the Schwarzschild radius, entropy, temperature, and energy remain finite.

Let us start with the latter. For a given black hole of mass
M
, its energy
E
is given by
E=Mc^{2}
. Thus, we leave
c
and
M
finite or scale
M
inversely to
c^{2}
. Written in terms of energy, the formula on Hawking’s tombstone (with Boltzmann’s constant set to one)

T=\frac{\hbar\,c^{5}}{8\pi\,G_{N}\,E}

(319)

shows that any single scaling limit in the Bronstein cube leads to infinite or zero Hawking temperature. Since we want to avoid this scenario, we deduce that our desired theory can only live at the edges or corners of the Bronstein cube and not at one of its faces. This eliminates, in particular, GR and QFT.

At this stage, we still have many possibilities for double- and triple-scaling limits that maintain finite Hawking temperature (for instance, the Newton gravity limit
\hbar\to 0
,
c\to\infty
). Finiteness of the Bekenstein–Hawking entropy does not add any new conditions due to the Smarr formula
E=2TS
or the first law
\delta E=T\,\delta S
, see for instance
[
43
]
.

Finiteness of the Schwarzschild radius
\mathsf{r}_{\textrm{\tiny S}}=2G_{N}\,E/c^{4}
imposes a second constraint, namely finiteness of
G_{N}/c^{4}
, implying that
\hbar\,c
needs to remain finite to maintain finite Hawking temperature (
319
). This condition removes all the edges from the Bronstein cube and leaves us with the two marked points in Fig.
3
, one of which is the tantum gravity limit
G_{N},c\to 0
,
\hbar\to\infty
and the other its dual version where all limits are taken oppositely. In the Bronstein cube, the latter corresponds to the antipodal point labeled as TG
∗
in Fig.
3

11
11

11

We keep finite the Planck length and energy in both limits but send the Planck time and mass to
\infty
in tantum gravity and to
0
in TG
∗
.

.

We stress that the two points TG and TG
∗
are the only points on the whole Bronstein cube (besides its quantum gravity interior) where all three thermodynamic quantities and the Schwarzschild radius are finite. So from the perspective of black hole thermodynamics, the tantum gravity limit is unique, up to dualization.

In summary, to obtain tantum gravity from quantum gravity, we have to take the Carroll limit
c\to 0
and retain finite combinations

G_{M}:=G_{N}\,c^{-4}\qquad\qquad\kappa:=\hbar\,c~.

(320)

The first one is the gravitational coupling constant in front of the Einstein–Hilbert action. So it is not automatically true that sending
G_{N}\to 0
removes gravity — it is still there if simultaneously the speed of light is sent to zero while keeping
G_{M}
fixed, which is precisely what is done to reach the so-called magnetic Carroll gravity limit
[
151
,
77
]
, hence the notation
G_{M}
. We shall describe this theory in more detail in the coming subsections. This elementary observation justifies calling our limit theory “gravity.” Similarly, the limit
\hbar\to\infty
does not automatically imply a breakdown of the semi-classical approximation since we still have two combinations of the coupling constants (
320
) that could be either large or small. We shall demonstrate this explicitly in section
19
. Thus, one should consider the points in the Bronstein cube labeled as TG and TG
∗
as two-dimensional planes spanned by the coupling constants
G_{M}
and
\kappa
.

An efficient way to parametrize the triple scaling limit leading to TG is to rescale

c\to c\,\epsilon\qquad\qquad\hbar\to\hbar/\epsilon\qquad\qquad G_{N}\to G_{N}\,\epsilon^{4}

(321)

and then take the limit
\epsilon\to 0
. This has the added advantage that the limiting parameter is dimensionless, and we shall use this procedure implicitly below. The parameter
\epsilon
drops out in the TG coupling constants (
320
).

By construction, the tantum gravity limit not only leads to a gravity theory, but the limiting theory also contains black-hole-like states with finite entropy, temperature, and energy, related to each other by the first law. At first glance, this is again surprising since we take a Carroll limit,
c\to 0
, for which the lightcone collapses
[
180
,
148
]
and makes obsolete all notions of horizons. However, as we have seen above, one has to be careful with naive arguments based on singular limits. Indeed, there can be “Carroll black holes,” though one needs to extend the notion of black holes and avoid basing them on event horizons. Instead, they are defined by their thermal properties and the existence of a so-called Carroll extremal surface, which is reminiscent of the bifurcation sphere of the Schwarzschild black hole
[
113
]
. The details of these definitions shall be spelt out in chapter
5
.

From a quantum gravity perspective, it is an asset that we are forced to go beyond the classical notion of a black hole in tantum gravity, since quantum black holes should not be defined in terms of event horizons either — after all, quantum black holes evaporate
[
157
,
158
]
, so event horizons are artifacts of the classical approximation. This is one of several ways in which tantum gravity is closer to quantum gravity than classical gravity.

14
General relativity in the limit
c\to 0

Tantum gravity admits a semi-classical description in terms of gravitational theories based on Carroll symmetries. In this section, we shall give an overview on how to arrive at these by taking appropriate scaling limits of Einstein gravity. Just like for the matter theories discussed in the last chapter, there are two distinct ways to take the limit
c\to 0
. One corresponds to the scaling (
321
) and is often termed “magnetic” after the corresponding limit in electrodynamics. The other “electric” limit also leads to a theory of Carroll gravity but does not maintain a well-defined notion of thermodynamics. In the literature one finds essentially two equivalent approaches for deriving these two limits. Either one starts with the Einstein–Hilbert action and does a manifestly covariant expansion for small
c

[
151
]
, or one starts with the Hamiltonian formulation of general relativity and takes the limit there
[
164
]
. Since the first path is slightly more involved, we shall present the second one here. The action of general relativity reads
[
16
,
15
]

\displaystyle I_{\text{\tiny ADM}}[g_{ij},\pi^{ij},N,N^{i}]=\int\differential t\,\differential^{d}x\;\big(\pi^{ij}\dot{g}_{ij}-N\mathcal{H}-N^{i}\mathcal{H}_{i}\big)

(322)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}

\displaystyle=\frac{16\pi G_{N}}{c^{2}}G_{ijkl}\pi^{ij}\pi^{kl}-\frac{c^{4}\sqrt{g}}{16\pi G_{N}}(R^{(d)}[g]-2\Lambda)

(323)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{i}

\displaystyle=-2\hat{\nabla}_{j}\pi^{j}{}_{i}

(324)

are Hamiltonian constraint and the momentum constraints, respectively.
\hat{\nabla}
denotes the Levi–Civitá connection associated to the
d
-dimensional metric
g_{ij}
on a given
t=\text{cst.}
hypersurface and
t
-derivatives are denoted by a dot. To emphasize the parallels with the simple matter models of the previous section we introduced the supermetric

\displaystyle G^{ijmn}=\sqrt{g}\big(g^{i(m}g^{n)j}-g^{ij}g^{mn}\big)

\displaystyle G_{ijmn}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{g}}\Big(g_{i(m}g_{n)j}-\frac{1}{d-1}g_{ij}g_{mn}\Big)

(325)

which satisfies
G_{ijkl}G^{ijmn}=\delta_{(k}^{m}\delta_{l)}^{n}
. It becomes evident that the Hamiltonian constraint consists of two parts, a term quadratic in
\pi^{ij}
reminiscent of a kinetic term, as well as a momentum independent term that behaves like a potential. The two different Carroll limits described above each suppress one of these terms. However, they both agree in the way they change the constraint algebra. Indeed, from the fundamental equal-time Poisson bracket

\displaystyle\{g_{ij}(x),\pi^{mn}(y)\}=\delta_{(i}^{m}\delta_{j)}^{n}\delta^{d}(x-y)

(326)

one deduces

\displaystyle\{\mathcal{H}(x),\mathcal{H}(y)\}

\displaystyle=c^{2}\big(g^{ij}(x)\mathcal{H}_{i}(x)+g^{ij}(y)\mathcal{H}_{i}(y)\big)\partial_{j}\delta^{d}(x-y)

(327)

\displaystyle\{\mathcal{H}(x),\mathcal{H}_{i}(y)\}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{H}(y)\partial_{i}\delta^{d}(x-y)

(328)

\displaystyle\{\mathcal{H}_{i}(x),\mathcal{H}_{j}(y)\}

\displaystyle=\big(\mathcal{H}_{i}(y)\partial_{j}+\mathcal{H}_{j}(x)\partial_{i}\big)\delta^{d}(x-y)

(329)

such that in the limit
c\to 0
one finds the algebra of first class constraints of Carroll gravity,

\boxed{\begin{aligned} \{\mathcal{H}(x),\mathcal{H}(y)\}&=0\\
\{\mathcal{H}(x),\mathcal{H}_{i}(y)\}&=\mathcal{H}(y)\partial_{i}\delta^{d}(x-y)\\
\{\mathcal{H}_{i}(x),\mathcal{H}_{j}(y)\}&=\big(\mathcal{H}_{i}(y)\partial_{j}+\mathcal{H}_{j}(x)\partial_{i}\big)\delta^{d}(x-y)\end{aligned}}

(330)

Since the scaling of the gravitational constant does not enter these brackets it follows that this result holds both for the electric and the magnetic theory.

Before we turn to these two examples, let us remark that once such a theory of Carroll gravity is coupled to matter in a covariant way, all of these constraints acquire contributions from the matter field, i.e., we have
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{\textrm{grav}}+\mathcal{H}_{m}
and likewise for
\mathcal{H}_{i}
. The algebra of first class constraints (
330
) is, however, still satisfied. This is analogous to the Lorentzian case as discussed, e.g., in
[
178
]
but does not in general imply that the matter parts alone satisfy (
330
) since on curved backgrounds there might be mixed brackets
\{\mathcal{H}_{\textrm{grav}},\mathcal{H}_{m}\}
which contribute in a nontrivial way. Typically, this happens when the matter field couples to the canonical gravitational momenta
\pi^{ij}
.

14.1
Electric Carroll gravity

The electric limit of general relativity is obtained by keeping fixed the combination
G_{E}=c^{-2}G_{N}
. As mentioned previously, this is in conflict with the scaling that maintains finite energy, temperature, and Schwarzschild radius and therefore does not describe a valid semi-classical description of tantum gravity. The Hamiltonian action reduces to

\displaystyle I_{E}[g_{ij},\pi^{ij},N,N^{i}]=\int\differential t\,\differential^{d}x\;\Big(\pi^{ij}\dot{g}_{ij}-N\mathcal{H}_{E}-N^{i}\mathcal{H}_{i}\Big)

(331)

where the momentum constraints are unchanged, and the Hamiltonian constraint reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{E}

\displaystyle=16\pi G_{E}\,G_{ijkl}\pi^{ij}\pi^{kl}~.

(332)

In consistency with (
330
), this can be checked to satisfy
\{\mathcal{H}_{E},\mathcal{H}_{E}\}=0
. This action was in fact the first instance of a theory of Carroll gravity going back to the original work
[
165
]
. Because of its ultra-local dynamics, it was used to model spacetime close to a singularity in, e.g., a black hole or the big bang
[
194
]
. It also has a covariant second order form, which is obtained by integrating out
\pi^{ij}
. The corresponding solution is

\displaystyle\pi^{ij}=\frac{1}{16\pi G_{E}}G^{ijmn}K_{mn}

\displaystyle K_{mn}=\frac{1}{2N}\big(\dot{g}_{mn}-2\hat{\nabla}_{(m}N_{n)}\big)

(333)

and upon reinsertion into the action one finds

\displaystyle I_{E}[g_{ij},N^{i},N]=\frac{1}{16\pi G_{E}}\int\differential t\,\differential^{d}x\,\sqrt{g}N\big(K^{ij}K_{ij}-K^{2}\big)~.

(334)

Finally, we reorganize the ADM variables in terms of the Carroll metric fields as

\displaystyle v^{\mu}=\Big(-\frac{1}{N},\frac{N^{i}}{N}\Big)

\displaystyle\tau_{\mu}=(N,0)

\displaystyle h_{\mu\nu}=\begin{pmatrix}N^{i}N_{i}&N_{i}\\
N_{i}&g_{ij}\end{pmatrix}

(335)

such that
K_{\mu\nu}
is just the extrinsic curvature of the Carroll structure,
K_{\mu\nu}=-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{L}_{v}h_{\mu\nu}
. The action can now be written in covariant form
[
165
,
164
]

\boxed{\begin{aligned} I_{2^{\textrm{nd}}}[h_{\mu\nu},\tau_{\mu}]=\frac{1}{16\pi G_{E}}\int\differential^{d+1}x\,\mathfrak{e}\,\big(K^{\mu\nu}K_{\mu\nu}-K^{2}\big)\end{aligned}}

(336)

which is equivalently obtained from a direct
c\to 0
limit of the Einstein–Hilbert action
[
151
]
or from a gauging perspective
[
156
]
. It is functionally dependent on the data
h_{\mu\nu}
and
\tau_{\mu}
which are the independent variables that allow to fix
v^{\mu}
and
h^{\mu\nu}
uniquely by orthonormality and completeness relations.

Clearly, (
336
) is manifestly invariant under local Carroll boosts. One may wonder why these gauge transformations did not appear as an additional set of first class constraints, besides the Hamiltonian and momentum constraints. Doing a Legendre transformation of (
336
) indeed reveals additional first class constraints
\pi^{i}\approx 0
, where
\pi^{i}
are the canonical momenta associated to the components
\tau_{i}
. Since they generate precisely those transformations that shift
\tau_{i}
, one can see that the Hamiltonian action of electric Carroll gravity is just a gauge-fixed version of the full theory where the gauge condition
\tau_{i}=0
chooses a specific Carroll boost-frame. At the same time, this ensures that the space transverse to
v^{\mu}
is tangent to the
t=\text{cst.}
surfaces. These surfaces then correspond to the integrated distribution generated by
\tau
(see also (
29
)).

Finally, we remark that there is presently no known way to describe electric Carroll gravity within the framework of the Cartan formalism. There does exist a first order formulation in terms of Cartan-like variables
[
196
]
which, however, needs to assume a different action of Carroll boosts than dictated by Cartan geometry.

14.2
Magnetic Carroll gravity

Applying the scaling (
321
) the
\epsilon\to 0
limit leads to the Hamiltonian action of magnetic Carroll gravity

\displaystyle I_{M}[g_{ij},\pi^{ij},N,N^{i}]=\int\differential t\,\differential^{d}x\,\Big(\pi^{ij}\dot{g}_{ij}-N\mathcal{H}_{M}-N^{i}\mathcal{H}_{i}\Big)

(337)

where the Hamiltonian constraint reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{M}=-\frac{\sqrt{g}}{16\pi G_{M}}(R^{(d)}[g]-2\Lambda)~.

(338)

Since it does not depend on the canonical momenta, the vanishing of the Poisson bracket with itself is trivial. The canonical momenta in this case act as Lagrange multipliers for the constraint

\displaystyle\dot{g}_{ij}-2\hat{\nabla}_{(i}N_{j)}=2NK_{ij}\approx 0

(339)

which sets the extrinsic curvature to zero. We now show that (
337
) is equivalent to an action obtained from a Cartan perspective by gauging a Klein pair associated to the Carroll (A)dS algebra as in section
7
. Following
[
77
]
, the Cartan action reads

\displaystyle I_{1^{\mathrm{st}}}[\tau,e^{a},\omega^{a},\omega^{ab}]=\frac{1}{16\pi G_{M}}\int\differential^{d+1}x\,\mathfrak{e}\,\big(\mathcal{R}-2\Lambda\big)

(340)

where
\mathcal{R}
is the invariant curvature scalar defined in (
136
).
One first needs to integrate out the connection components by their equations of motion. The latter are computed most easily in a formulation in terms of differential forms where

\displaystyle I_{1^{\mathrm{st}}}=\frac{1}{16\pi G_{M}(d-2)!}\int\Big(\frac{2}{d-1}\Omega^{a_{1}}\wedge e^{a_{2}}+\tau\wedge\Omega^{a_{1}a_{2}}\Big)\wedge e^{a_{3}}\wedge\dots\wedge e^{a_{d}}\,\epsilon_{a_{1}...a_{d}}

(341)

with the orientation chosen such that
\differential^{d+1}x\,\mathfrak{e}=\tau\wedge e^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge e^{d}
.
Varying (
341
) with respect to the connections yields the equations

\displaystyle\delta\omega^{a}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle T^{[a_{1}}\wedge e^{a_{2}}\wedge\cdots\,\wedge e^{a_{d-1}]}

\displaystyle=0

(342)

\displaystyle\delta\omega^{ab}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle\Big(T\wedge e^{[a_{1}}-(d-2)T^{[a_{1}}\wedge\tau\Big)\wedge e^{a_{2}}\wedge\cdots\,\wedge e^{a_{d-2}]}

\displaystyle=0

(343)

and by expanding the various torsion components in terms of the coframe one can show that they imply
T^{a}=0=T
. We now follow the considerations of section
7.1
and solve only the part of these equations that depends on the connection, leaving the part associated to intrinsic torsion arbitrary. This makes
\omega^{a}
and
\omega^{ab}
dependent variables given in terms of the coframe and the arbitrary symmetric tensor
C^{ab}
by (
122
), (
123
). Inserting this back into the curvature scalar and using (
136
) yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}\big|_{C_{\mu\nu}=0}+2\big(C^{\mu\nu}-h^{\mu\nu}C\big)K_{\mu\nu}-\frac{2}{\mathfrak{e}}\partial_{\mu}(\mathfrak{e}v^{\mu}C)

(344)

where we pulled out the part that depends on
C_{\mu\nu}
with
C=h^{\mu\nu}C_{\mu\nu}
, and the affine connection in the curvature is given by (
137
). Thus, the action of magnetic Carroll gravity in a second order form reads up to boundary terms
[
61
]

\boxed{\begin{aligned} I&{}_{2^{\textrm{nd}}}[\tau_{\mu},\,h_{\mu\nu},C_{\mu\nu}]=\\[5.0pt]
&\frac{1}{16\pi G_{M}}\int\differential^{d+1}x\,\mathfrak{e}\,\Big(h^{\mu\nu}\big(R_{\mu\nu}-v^{\rho}\tau_{\sigma}R^{\sigma}{}_{\mu\rho\nu}\big)\big|_{C^{\mu\nu}=0}+2\big(C^{\mu\nu}-h^{\mu\nu}C\big)K_{\mu\nu}-2\Lambda\Big)\end{aligned}}

(345)

One can check that, again up to boundary terms, this action is invariant under local Carroll boosts and diffeomorphisms, with the former acting as

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}I_{2^{\textrm{nd}}}=\frac{1}{8\pi G_{M}}\int\differential^{d+1}x\,\partial_{\mu}\big(\mathfrak{e}v^{\mu}\partial_{\lambda}C-\mathfrak{e}\lambda^{\mu}K\big)

(346)

where we used (
140
). The equivalence to the Hamiltonian form now proceeds similarly to the electric case. One fixes a boost frame as well as an ADM-like split for the geometry given by (
335
) such that

\displaystyle I_{2^{\textrm{nd}}}[N,N^{i},g_{ij},C_{ij}]=\frac{1}{16\pi G_{M}}\int\differential t\,\differential^{d}x\,\sqrt{g}N\,\big(R^{(d)}[g]-2\Lambda+2(C^{ij}-g^{ij}C)K_{ij}\big)~.

(347)

Finally, after renaming

\displaystyle\pi^{ij}=\frac{\sqrt{g}}{16\pi G_{M}}(C^{ij}-g^{ij}C)

(348)

it is straightforward to show that (
347
) reduces to the Hamiltonian action (
337
), again up to a total derivative. For more details on this computation we refer to
[
77
]
.

15
Two-dimensional Carroll dilaton gravity

We now come to two-dimensional Carroll gravity theories, which shall be our main tool for describing Carroll black holes. Similarly to the Lorentzian case (see e.g.
[
134
]
), there is not just one of these theories but rather an infinite number of different models. Some of them indeed describe a subsector of magnetic Carroll gravity in
d+1
dimensions obtained by imposing spherical symmetry. We shall come to this class of spherically reduced models and how to construct them in subsection
15.4
. Others have to be regarded as intrinsically two-dimensional but may also provide interesting examples for Carroll black holes. What they all have in common is that their dynamical field content does not only consist of the Carroll metric data but also of additional scalar fields such as the dilaton
X
in order to make the actions non-trivial. The resulting theories are of topological type in the sense that they do not have any local propagating degrees of freedom.

15.1
BF theories

The simplest class of 2d Carroll dilaton gravity theories can be written as certain two-dimensional gauge theories called BF theories. Their field content consists of a Cartan connection
A
(see section
6.3
) for a specific Klein pair
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})
as well as a scalar field
\mathcal{X}
taking values in the dual of
\mathfrak{g}
with an action given by

\displaystyle I_{\mathrm{BF}}[A,\mathcal{X}]=\frac{k}{2\pi}\int\,\mathcal{X}(F)=\frac{k}{2\pi}\int\,X^{I}F_{I}~.

(349)

Here,
F
is the curvature two-form of
A
and
k
a coupling constant. For the second equality we chose a basis
\{f^{I}\}
for
\mathfrak{g}
with
[f^{I},f^{J}]=c^{IJ}{}_{K}f^{K}
such that, e.g.,
A=A_{I_{\mu}}f^{I}\differential x^{\mu}
. The action (
349
) is gauge invariant under
\mathfrak{g}
-valued transformations
\lambda=\lambda_{I}f^{I}
which act as

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}A=\differential\lambda+[A,\lambda]

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}\mathcal{X}(\cdot)=-\mathcal{X}([\cdot,\lambda])

(350)

and the equations of motion from varying with respect to
\mathcal{X}
and
A
are

\displaystyle\delta X^{I}:

\displaystyle\qquad F_{I}=0

(351a)

\displaystyle\delta A_{I}:

\displaystyle\qquad\differential X^{I}+c^{IJ}{}_{K}X^{K}A_{J}=0~.

(351b)

The first of these implies that all the solutions to these models are locally isomorphic to the homogeneous spacetime associated to the Klein pair (see Theorem
6.1
). Let us further specify these geometries by taking
\mathfrak{g}
to be the 2d AdS Carroll algebra (see section
4.1
) with generators
\{B,H,P\}
satisfying the non-zero brackets

\displaystyle[B,P]=H

\displaystyle[H,P]=\frac{1}{\ell^{2}}B~.

(352)

The subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}
is the homogeneous Carroll algebra spanned by
B
. We write the components as

\displaystyle A_{I}=(\omega,\tau,e)

\displaystyle X^{I}=(X,X_{\textrm{\tiny H}},X_{\textrm{\tiny P}})

\displaystyle\lambda_{I}=(\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny B}},\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny H}},\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny P}})

(353)

such that the action becomes

\displaystyle I_{\mathrm{BF}}=\frac{k}{2\pi}\int X\,\differential\omega+X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,\big(\differential\tau+\omega\wedge e\big)+X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}\,\differential e+\frac{X}{\ell^{2}}\,\tau\wedge e

(354)

which is referred to as the Carroll–Jackiw–Teitelboim (CJT) model
[
136
,
133
]
.
The
1
-forms are spatial einbein
e
,
temporal einbein
\tau
and Carroll boost connection
\omega
. The
scalar fields are dilaton
X
and Lagrange multipliers for torsion
constraints
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}
,
X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}
. This identification of variables gives the BF model the interpretation of a Carroll gravitational theory. Indeed, comparing with the cases from section
14
it is of “magnetic” type as a consequence of the constraint generated by
X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}
setting the intrinsic torsion to zero. What remains is to show that the gauge transformations can be interpreted as internal local Carroll boosts together with spacetime diffeomorphisms. For this we follow the discussion around (
86
) and take
\lambda=\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny B}}B
. Then, with
\Sigma=\lambda+i_{\xi}A
, the action is left invariant by the transformations

\displaystyle\bar{\delta}_{(\lambda,\xi)}A_{I}

\displaystyle=\differential\Sigma_{I}+c^{JK}{}_{I}A_{J}\Sigma_{K}+i_{\xi}F_{I}

(355a)

\displaystyle\bar{\delta}_{(\lambda,\xi)}X^{I}

\displaystyle=-c^{IJ}{}_{K}X^{K}\Sigma_{J}+i_{\xi}(\differential X^{I}+c^{IJ}{}_{K}X^{K}A_{J})

(355b)

as a consequence of

\displaystyle\bar{\delta}_{(\lambda,\xi)}A_{I}=\mathcal{L}_{\xi}A_{I}+\delta_{\lambda}A_{I}

\displaystyle\bar{\delta}_{(\lambda,\xi)}X^{I}=\mathcal{L}_{\xi}X^{I}+\delta_{\lambda}X^{I}~.

(356)

But, since the rightmost terms in (
355
) are just the equations of motion (
351
) themselves, one can see that on-shell there is no difference between the
\delta
and
\bar{\delta}
transformations. Thus, the parameters
\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny H}}
and
\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny P}}
directly generate spacetime diffeomorphisms for the choices
(\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny H}},\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny P}})=(i_{\xi}\tau,i_{\xi}e)
. This should be put in contrast to the higher-dimensional Cartan actions where
\bar{\delta}
- and
\delta
-transformations typically do not differ by terms that are proportional to the equations of motion, see (
86
).

Clearly, having
F=0
as an equation of motion is quite restrictive, and it is desirable to generalize these models. We shall come to this in the next subsection.

15.2
First-order formulation

Generic 2d Carroll dilaton gravity was first constructed in
[
136
]
and includes BF-type theories as a special case. The 2d Carroll dilaton gravity bulk action is given by
[
136
]

\displaystyle I_{1^{\mathrm{st}}}[\omega,\,\tau,\,e,\,X,\,X_{\textrm{\tiny H}},\,X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}]=\frac{k}{2\pi}\,\int_{\mathcal{M}}{\mathcal{L}}

(357)

on a 2d manifold
\mathcal{M}
with coupling
k
and the Lagrange-2-form

\boxed{\phantom{\Bigg(}{\mathcal{L}}=X\,\differential\omega+X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,\big(\differential\tau+\omega\wedge e\big)+X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}\,\differential e+{\mathcal{V}}(X,\,X_{\textrm{\tiny H}})\,\tau\wedge e\phantom{\Bigg(}}

(358)

where the potential
{\mathcal{V}}(X,\,X_{\textrm{\tiny H}})
is an arbitrary function. Whenever it is linear in either of its arguments the theory reduces to a BF-type model. The
composite 2-forms are curvature
\Omega=\differential\omega
, torsion

T=\differential\tau+\omega\wedge e
, and intrinsic torsion
T_{\text{int}}=\differential e

where the latter is defined as the part of the torsion independent of
the boost connection. Just as in the BF case, we summarily refer to the scalar fields as

X^{I}=(X,X_{\textrm{\tiny H}},X_{\textrm{\tiny P}})
and to the
1
-forms as
A_{I}=(\omega,\tau,e)
.

The Lagrange-2-form (
358
) [and hence also the action
(
357
)] is invariant under local Carroll boosts

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny B}}}X

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny B}}}X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny B}}}X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}

\displaystyle=X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny B}}

(359a)

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny B}}}\omega

\displaystyle=\differential\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny B}}

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny B}}}\tau

\displaystyle=-e\,\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny B}}

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny B}}}e

\displaystyle=0\,.

(359b)

The transformations (
359
) show that the dilaton
X
, the
field
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}
and the spatial einbein
e
are Carroll boost invariant,
while the 1-form
\omega
is the Carroll boost connection. The
non-invariances of the temporal einbein
\tau
and the scalar
X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}

conspire such that the sum of the middle two terms in the
Lagrange-2-form is Carroll boost-invariant. To simplify the notation a bit from now on, we shall denote Carroll boosts just by
\delta_{\lambda}:=\delta_{\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny B}}}
and make it explicit everywhere where something else is implied.

Moreover, the action (
357
) is invariant under two
additional gauge symmetries
\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny H}}
and

\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny P}}
(we define

\partial_{X}:=\partial/\partial X
and

\partial_{\textrm{\tiny H}}:=\partial/\partial X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}
)

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny H}}}X

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny H}}}X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny H}}}X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}

\displaystyle={\mathcal{V}}\,\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny H}}

(360a)

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny H}}}\omega

\displaystyle=-(\partial_{X}{\mathcal{V}})\,e\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny H}}

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny H}}}\tau

\displaystyle=\differential\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny H}}-(\partial_{\textrm{\tiny H}}{\mathcal{V}})\,e\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny H}}

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny H}}}e

\displaystyle=0

(360b)

and

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny P}}}X

\displaystyle=-X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny P}}

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny P}}}X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}

\displaystyle=-{\mathcal{V}}\,\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny P}}

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny P}}}X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}

\displaystyle=0

(361a)

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny P}}}\omega

\displaystyle=(\partial_{X}{\mathcal{V}})\,\tau\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny P}}

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny P}}}\tau

\displaystyle=\omega\,\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny P}}+(\partial_{\textrm{\tiny H}}{\mathcal{V}})\,\tau\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny P}}

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny P}}}e

\displaystyle=\differential\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny P}}

(361b)

On-shell they generate diffeomorphisms along a vector field
\xi^{\mu}
by virtue of the relations
12
12

12

Additionally, we need a compensating Carroll boost generated by
\lambda=\omega_{\mu}\,\xi^{\mu}
.

\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny H}}=i_{\xi}\tau_{\mu}\,
and
\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny P}}=i_{\xi}e_{\mu}
:

\displaystyle\delta_{\xi}X

\displaystyle\approx\xi^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}X

\displaystyle\delta_{\xi}\omega_{\mu}

\displaystyle\approx\xi^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}\omega_{\mu}+\omega_{\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi^{\nu}

(362a)

\displaystyle\delta_{\xi}X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}

\displaystyle\approx\xi^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}

\displaystyle\delta_{\xi}\tau_{\mu}

\displaystyle\approx\xi^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}\tau_{\mu}+\tau_{\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi^{\nu}

(362b)

\displaystyle\delta_{\xi}X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}

\displaystyle\approx\xi^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}

\displaystyle\delta_{\xi}e_{\mu}

\displaystyle\approx\xi^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}e_{\mu}+e_{\nu}\partial_{\mu}\xi^{\nu}

(362c)

The Lie variations above follow from the gauge symmetries together
with the Carroll equations of motion displayed below in
(
363
) (
\approx
denotes on-shell equivalence).

15.2.1
Equations of motion

Varying the action (
357
) with respect to all fields yields the equations of motion.

\displaystyle\delta X

Carroll curvature:

\displaystyle\Omega=\differential\omega=-\partial_{X}{\mathcal{V}}(X,\,X_{\textrm{\tiny H}})\,\tau\wedge e

(363a)

\displaystyle\delta X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}

Carroll torsion:

\displaystyle T=\differential\tau+\omega\wedge e=-\partial_{\textrm{\tiny H}}{\mathcal{V}}(X,\,X_{\textrm{\tiny H}})\,\tau\wedge e

(363b)

\displaystyle\delta X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}

No intrinsic torsion:

\displaystyle T_{\mathrm{int}}=\differential e=0

(363c)

\displaystyle\delta\omega

Carroll metric:

\displaystyle\differential X+X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,e=0

(363d)

\displaystyle\delta\tau

Carroll Casimir:

\displaystyle\differential X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}+{\mathcal{V}}(X,\,X_{\textrm{\tiny H}})\,e=0

(363e)

\displaystyle\delta e

Auxiliary field:

\displaystyle\differential X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}-{\mathcal{V}}(X,\,X_{\textrm{\tiny H}})\,\tau-X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,\omega=0

(363f)

The first equation (
363a
) determines the Carroll curvature, which generally is non-zero but trivially vanishes whenever the potential is independent of the dilaton field. The second equation (
363b
) shows that on-shell Carroll torsion vanishes whenever the potential is independent of
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}
. The third equation (
363c
) reveals that there is never intrinsic torsion, regardless of how the potential is chosen. The fourth equation (
363d
) allows algebraically determining the spatial einbein (and hence the Carroll metric) in terms of the Carroll boost invariant scalars,
X
and
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}
. The fifth equation (
363e
) entails a conserved Casimir function, which we shall uncover below when discussing linear dilaton vacua. The final equation (
363f
) allows determining the auxiliary field
X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}
in terms of the potential
{\mathcal{V}}(X,\,X_{\textrm{\tiny H}})
and the geometric data extracted from the other five equations of motion or, alternatively, if
X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}
is gauge fixed suitably it provides an algebraic constraint relating
\tau
and
\omega
.

It can be useful to map solutions of different models to each other if they can be related by suitable Weyl rescalings. Therefore, consider a dilaton-dependent Weyl rescaling, parametrized by
\alpha
, of the Carroll metric

e\to\tilde{e}=e\,e^{\alpha(X)}

(364)

that leaves invariant the dilaton,
X\to\tilde{X}=X
. Such Weyl
rescalings are compatible with the absence of intrinsic torsion (this
would not be the case if the Weyl factor did depend on time).
Consistency with Carroll boosts demands that also
\tau
scales in the
same way as
e
,

\tau\to\tilde{\tau}=\tau\,e^{\alpha(X)}\,.

(365)

The Carroll metric equation (
363d
) implies that
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}
transforms inversely to
e
,

X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\to\tilde{X}_{\textrm{\tiny H}}=X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,e^{-\alpha(X)}

(366)

and consistency with Carroll boosts demands the same scaling for
X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}
.

X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}\to\tilde{X}_{\textrm{\tiny P}}=X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}\,e^{-\alpha(X)}

(367)

The Carroll Casimir equation (
363e
)

\differential X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}+{\mathcal{V}}(X,\,X_{\textrm{\tiny H}})\,e=0\qquad\leftrightarrow\qquad\differential\tilde{X}_{\textrm{\tiny H}}+\tilde{\mathcal{V}}(\tilde{X},\,\tilde{X}_{\textrm{\tiny H}})\,\tilde{e}=0

(368)

establishes the transformation behaviour of the potential

\mathcal{V}(X,\,X_{\textrm{\tiny H}})\to\tilde{\mathcal{V}}(\tilde{X},\,\tilde{X}_{\textrm{\tiny H}})=e^{-2\alpha(X)}\,\big({\mathcal{V}}(X,\,X_{\textrm{\tiny H}})-X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}^{2}\,\partial_{X}\alpha\big)\,.

(369)

The auxiliary field equation (
363f
) yields an inhomogeneous shift for
\omega
,

\omega\to\tilde{\omega}=\omega+(\partial_{X}\alpha)(X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\tau+X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}e)\,.

(370)

The Carroll torsion and curvature equations (
363a
,
363b
) are compatible with all the transformations above, replacing consistently everywhere quantities with their tilde counterparts.

Thus, dilaton-dependent Weyl rescalings (
364
) act pretty
much in the same way as in the Lorentzian case (see

[
134
]
) and can be used to introduce or eliminate a
kinetic potential function
U(X)
in potentials of the type
(
371
) below.

15.3
Second-order formulation

To set the stage for other discussions, we translate the
first-order/PSM formulation to the second-order formulation.

While the functional dependence of
\mathcal{V}
can, in principle, be arbitrary, we consider it to be of the form

\mathcal{V}(X,\,X_{\textrm{\tiny H}})=-\frac{U(X)}{2}X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}^{2}+V(X)

(371)

for some kinetic and potential function of the dilaton,
U(X)
and
V(X)
, respectively. (This is also the most commonly used form of the potential in Lorentzian 2d dilaton gravity
[
134
]
.)

To get the second-order formulation, one needs to integrate out

\omega
and
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}
by their own equations of motion (
363
).
For this we introduce the dual vectors
v^{\mu}
and
e^{\mu}
satisfying

\displaystyle v^{\mu}\tau_{\mu}=-1\qquad e^{\mu}e_{\mu}=1\qquad\delta^{\mu}_{\nu}=-v^{\mu}\tau_{\nu}+e^{\mu}e_{\nu}~.

(372)

The
\omega
-equation (
363d
) can be solved algebraically for
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}

X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}=-e^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}X~,

(373)

and also leads to a constraint
v^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}X=0
. The
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}
-equation (
363b
) is solved by splitting

\displaystyle\omega=\hat{\omega}+t+C\,e

(374)

with a part
\hat{\omega}
satisfying
\differential\tau+\hat{\omega}\wedge e=0
, a torsion part
t
, and an arbitrary undetermined function
C
. The latter embodies the usual ambiguity that the part
e^{\mu}\omega_{\mu}
of the Carroll spin connection is not entirely determined by the torsion constraints. In contrast to the higher dimensional setting, we shall see that it does not play the role of a Lagrange multiplier that sets the intrinsic torsion to zero but rather implements the constraint
v^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}X=0
. Explicitly, the different parts read

\displaystyle\hat{\omega}_{\mu}=-e^{\nu}\partial_{\mu}\tau_{\nu}+e^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}\tau_{\mu}:=-2e^{\nu}\partial_{[\mu}\tau_{\nu]}\qquad\qquad t=U(X)\,X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\tau

(375)

where the latter is determined by the
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}
-equation. Plugging these
solutions into the first-order action (
357
) with
(
358
) yields

I_{1^{\mathrm{st}}}=\frac{k}{2\pi}\int_{\mathcal{M}}\Big(X\,\differential\hat{\omega}-C\differential X\wedge e+X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}\differential e+\Big(-\frac{U(X)}{2}\big(e^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}X\big)^{2}+V(X)\Big)\,\tau\wedge e\Big)\,.

(376)

In the second and third terms one can already see the structure of two constraints arise. As a last step, we express the curvature two-form in the first term in terms of an affine connection. Following section
6.2
, this is done by solving vielbein postulates for a given boost connection. We choose to only do this for the part
\hat{\omega}
,

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}\tau_{\nu}+\hat{\omega}_{\mu}e_{\nu}-\Gamma^{\alpha}{}_{\mu\nu}\tau_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}v^{\nu}+\Gamma^{\nu}{}_{\mu\alpha}v^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=0

(377a)

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}e_{\nu}-\Gamma^{\alpha}{}_{\mu\nu}e_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}e^{\nu}+\hat{\omega}_{\mu}v^{\nu}+\Gamma^{\nu}{}_{\mu\alpha}e^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=0

(377b)

such that the Riemann tensor associated to
\Gamma^{\alpha}{}_{\mu\nu}

is related to the connection

\hat{\omega}
by

R^{\lambda}{}_{\sigma\mu\nu}=-2v^{\lambda}e_{\sigma}\partial_{[\mu}\hat{\omega}_{\nu]}
. The solution for the affine connection reads

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\lambda}{}_{\mu\nu}=-v^{\lambda}\partial_{(\mu}\tau_{\nu)}-v^{\lambda}\tau_{(\mu}\mathcal{L}_{v}\tau_{\nu)}+e^{\lambda}\partial_{\mu}e_{\nu}

(378)

which encodes the intrinsic torsion,

\displaystyle T^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}=2e^{\lambda}\partial_{[\mu}e_{\nu]}=2Ke^{\lambda}\tau_{[\mu}e_{\nu]}~.

(379)

Here, we defined
K=-e^{\mu}\mathcal{L}_{v}e_{\mu}
as the only component of the extrinsic curvature in 2d.
On the other hand, using the change of basis

\differential x^{\mu}=-v^{\mu}\tau+e^{\mu}e
, we write

\differential\hat{\omega}=\partial_{[\mu}\hat{\omega}_{\nu]}\differential x^{\mu}\wedge\differential x^{\nu}=2\partial_{[\mu}\hat{\omega}_{\nu]}e^{\mu}v^{\nu}\tau\wedge e
implying

\differential\hat{\omega}=\frac{R}{2}\,\tau\wedge e
where we
defined the curvature scalar
R=e^{\mu}e^{\nu}(R^{\lambda}{}_{\mu\lambda\nu}-v^{\rho}\tau_{\sigma}R^{\sigma}{}_{\mu\rho\nu})
analogous to (
136
) but with the arbitrary part associated to
C
already pulled out. Finally, we
define the volume form

\tau\wedge e=\tau_{\mu}e_{\nu}\differential x^{\mu}\wedge\differential x^{\nu}=\varepsilon^{\mu\nu}\tau_{\mu}e_{\nu}\differential^{2}x=\det(\tau,e)\,\differential^{2}x
.

Inserting these results into the first-order action
(
376
) yields the second-order Carroll dilaton gravity
action

\displaystyle I_{2^{\mathrm{nd}}}[e,\tau,C,X_{\textrm{\tiny P}},X]=\frac{k}{4\pi}\int_{\mathcal{M}}\,\mathcal{L}_{2^{\mathrm{nd}}}

(380)

with

\boxed{\phantom{\Bigg(}\mathcal{L}_{2^{\mathrm{nd}}}=\differential^{2}x\,\det(\tau,e)\,\Big(XR+2C\,v^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}X+2X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}K-U(X)\big(e^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}X\big)^{2}+2V(X)\Big)~.\phantom{\Bigg)}}

(381)

As we will show in subsection
15.4
, comparing with the literature
(e.g.
[
61
,
164
,
77
]
)
allows identifying this action with the magnetic Carroll theory. It is Carroll invariant if the Lagrange multipliers transform
under Carroll boosts as

\delta_{\lambda}C=-U\lambda e^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}X+\nabla_{\mu}(e^{\mu}\lambda)\qquad\qquad\delta_{\lambda}X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}=-\lambda e^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}X

(382)

where
\nabla
is the connection associated with
\hat{\omega}
via
the vielbein postulates. The left equality is compatible with the
transformation behaviour of
\omega
in (
360
) by using
(
374
). The right equality agrees with the
corresponding on-shell transformation in the first-order formalism
(
359
). One should keep in mind that the affine connection itself is not Carroll boost invariant in this case, similarly to (
137
).

15.4
Spherical reduction of magnetic Carroll gravity

For this, we start with the magnetic
limit of
(d+1)
-dimensional Einstein gravity (
340
), impose spherical symmetry,
and reduce the corresponding action by the angular variables. We
approach this in the second-order formulation, i.e., we work with the
spin connection expressed in terms of the vielbein variables by using
the torsion constraints. Nevertheless, we will not insert the
expressions explicitly to keep the notation cleaner. The

(d+1)
-dimensional torsion components read

T^{0}=\differential\tau+\omega^{a}\wedge e_{a}\qquad\qquad T^{a}=\differential e^{a}+\omega^{ab}\wedge e_{b}

(383)

where
a=1,...,d
and
\omega^{ab}=-\omega^{ba}
. By spherical
symmetry, we write the Carroll metric as

\displaystyle h_{MN}\differential x^{M}\differential x^{N}

\displaystyle=h_{\mu\nu}(x^{\sigma})\differential x^{\mu}\differential x^{\nu}+\Phi^{2}(x^{\sigma})\,\differential\Omega^{2}_{S^{(D-2)}}

(384)

where capital latin letters denote the higher dimensional spacetime indices.
It is convenient to phrase things in terms of a choice of vielbein,

h=e\otimes e+\delta_{lm}e^{l}\otimes e^{m}=\bar{e}\otimes\bar{e}+\Phi^{2}\delta_{lm}\bar{e}^{l}\otimes\bar{e}^{m}

(385)

where the internal indices take values
l,m=2,...,d
and in the
second equality we defined a transverse vielbein
\bar{e}^{l}
normalized to
the unit sphere
S^{(d-1)}
. To keep the notation simple we set

e^{1}\equiv e
,
\omega^{1}\equiv\omega
. The relations between the
vielbeins are

\displaystyle\bar{\tau}_{M}

\displaystyle=\tau_{M}

\displaystyle\bar{e}_{M}

\displaystyle=e_{M}

\displaystyle\bar{e}^{l}_{M}

\displaystyle=\Phi^{-1}e^{l}_{M}

(386)

\displaystyle\bar{v}^{M}

\displaystyle=v^{M}

\displaystyle\bar{e}^{M}

\displaystyle=e^{M}

\displaystyle\bar{e}_{l}^{M}

\displaystyle=\Phi\,e_{l}^{M}

(387)

where
\tau_{M}v^{M}=-1
,
e_{M}e^{M}=1
, and
e_{M}^{l}e_{m}^{M}=\delta^{l}_{m}
define
the dual versions. We assume throughout that the coordinates are
chosen in such a way that only
\bar{e}^{l}_{M}
depend on the internal
coordinates. The barred vielbeins are assumed to satisfy the
Carroll torsion constraints, which as a reminder, are not obtained
by setting all the torsion to zero but only those components that
allow the elimination of the spin connection from the first-order
action. We have

\differential\bar{\tau}+\bar{\omega}\wedge\bar{e}=0\qquad\qquad\differential\bar{e}^{l}+\bar{\omega}^{lm}\wedge\bar{e}_{m}=0

(388)

where the remaining torsion component
\differential\bar{e}
crucially is not
set to zero. The second of these equations corresponds to the
spherical part which is thus fixed to be torsion-free. Let us assume

\bar{\omega}=\omega
and
\bar{\omega}^{lm}=\omega^{lm}
.
Furthermore, we impose vanishing torsion in the unbarred space by the
same principle. In particular, this means that the following
conditions are imposed

T^{0}(e_{a},v)=T_{a}(e_{b},e_{c})=T^{0}(e_{a},e_{b})=T_{[a}(e_{b]},v)=0~.

(389)

This sets to zero all the higher-dimensional torsion except the components

T_{(a}(e_{b)},v)
which correspond to the intrinsic torsion (see section
7.1
). The conditions can
be solved for
\omega^{1l}=-\omega^{l1}
and
\omega^{l}
by

\displaystyle\omega^{l}=\varphi\,\bar{e}^{l}

\displaystyle\omega^{l1}=(e^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\Phi)\bar{e}^{l}

(390)

where
\varphi
is an arbitrary function. This can be inserted to compute the decomposition of the higher-dimensional curvature scalar

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}=-2v^{M}e_{a}^{N}\Omega^{a}_{MN}+e_{a}^{M}e_{b}^{N}\Omega^{ab}_{MN}

(391)

where

\Omega^{a}=\differential\omega^{a}+\omega^{ab}\wedge\omega_{b}\qquad\qquad\Omega^{ab}=\differential\omega^{ab}+\omega^{a}{}_{c}\wedge\omega^{cb}~.

(392)

Explicitly, the components are given by

\displaystyle\Omega^{1}

\displaystyle=\bar{\Omega}^{1}=\differential\bar{\omega}

\displaystyle\Omega^{1l}

\displaystyle=-\differential\,(e^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\Phi)\wedge\bar{e}^{l}=-\Omega^{l1}

(393)

\displaystyle\Omega^{l}

\displaystyle=\differential\varphi\wedge\bar{e}^{l}+(e^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\Phi)\bar{e}^{l}\wedge\bar{\omega}

\displaystyle\Omega^{lm}

\displaystyle=\bar{\Omega}^{lm}-(e^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\Phi)\bar{e}^{l}\wedge\bar{e}^{m}

(394)

leading to the scalar curvature taking the form

\mathcal{R}=-4v^{\mu}e^{\nu}\partial_{[\mu}\bar{\omega}_{\nu]}+\frac{2(d-1)}{\Phi}\Big((e^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\Phi)v^{\nu}\bar{\omega}_{\nu}-v^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\varphi-e^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}(e^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\Phi)\Big)\\
-(d-1)(d-2)\frac{(e^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\Phi)^{2}}{\Phi^{2}}+\frac{(d-1)(d-2)}{\Phi^{2}}

(395)

where we used
R_{S^{(d-1)}}=(d-1)(d-2)
. Plugging this result into the

(d+1)
-dimensional action (see
[
77
]
), using the
field redefinition

\displaystyle\Phi=\frac{d-1}{\lambda}X^{\frac{1}{d-1}},

(396)

and integrating over the angles yields

\displaystyle I_{2^{\textrm{nd}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16\pi G_{M}}\int\differential^{d+1}x\det(\tau,e^a)\,\mathcal{R}

(397)

\displaystyle=\frac{k}{4\pi}\int\differential^{2}x\det(\tau,e)\Big(4Xe^{\mu}v^{\nu}\partial_{[\mu}\bar{\omega}_{\nu]}-2\lambda\varphi X^{\frac{2-d}{1-d}}K+2\lambda\varphi\frac{d-2}{d-1}X^{\frac{1}{1-d}}v^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}X

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+2v^{\mu}\bar{\omega}_{\mu}e^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}X-2e^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}(e^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}X)-U(e^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}X)^{2}+2V\Big)

(398)

with
k
,
U
, and
V
defined as

\displaystyle k

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{d/2}}{2\Gamma(d/2)G_{M}}\Big(\frac{d-1}{\lambda}\Big)^{d-1}

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle=-\frac{d-2}{(d-1)X}

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle=\lambda^{2}\frac{d-2}{2(d-1)}X^{\frac{d-3}{d-1}}~.

(399)

The curvature terms simplify by noticing that

-2e^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}(e^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}X)=-2X\nabla_{\mu}a^{\mu}\qquad\qquad 2v^{\mu}\bar{\omega}_{\mu}e^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}X=2X\nabla_{\mu}a^{\mu}

(400)

up to total derivatives. These two terms, therefore, cancel in the
action. The quantity
\bar{\omega}
is the 2d Carroll boost connection
evaluated on a solution of the torsion constraints, i.e.,

\displaystyle\bar{\omega}=\hat{\omega}+Ce

(401)

with an undetermined component given by the arbitrary function

C(x^{\alpha})
. Together with the definition of the curvature
scalar
R=4e^{\mu}v^{\nu}\partial_{[\mu}\hat{\omega}_{\nu]}
the
action reads

\displaystyle I_{2^{\textrm{nd}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{k}{4\pi}\int\differential^{2}x\det(\tau,e)\Big(XR+2Cv^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}X+2X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}K-U(e^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}X)^{2}+2V\Big)

(402)

where we discarded all boundary terms and redefined the Lagrange
multipliers
C
,
X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}
as specific combinations of the free
functions
C
and
\varphi
,

C\to C-\varphi\lambda\frac{d-2}{d-1}X^{\frac{1}{1-d}}\qquad\qquad\varphi\to-\frac{X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}}{\lambda}X^{\frac{2-d}{d-1}}~.

(403)

This matches with the result (
381
) for the choice (
399
) such that one can interpret the two-dimensional theory as an effective description of the spherically symmetric sector of the higher-dimensional theory. This is analogous to the Lorentzian case, see, e.g.,
[
134
,
113
]
.

We shall see in the next subsection that taking the magnetic limit after spherically reducing Einstein gravity also leads to the same theory. It follows that spherical reduction and taking the Carroll limit commute. A similar result was found already for the Galilean case
[
225
]
.

15.5
Magnetic Carroll dilaton gravity from ultra-relativistic expansion

Here, we show that the second order action of 2d Carroll dilaton gravity (
380
) can also be understood as coming from an ultrarelativistic expansion of 2d dilaton gravity. The technique we use here is analogous to the one used in
[
151
]
for general relativity. We start by considering Lorentzian 2d dilaton gravity given by the second-order action
[
134
]

\displaystyle I_{\mathrm{rel.}}[g_{\mu\nu},X]=\frac{k_{\mathrm{rel.}}}{4\pi}c^{3}\int\differential^{2}x\,\sqrt{-g}\,\Big(X{\overset{\textrm{\tiny(LC)}}{R}}-U(X)(\partial X)^{2}+2V(X)\Big)

(404)

where we allow for arbitrary potentials
U(X)
and
V(X)
. The connection chosen here is just the Levi–Civitá connection associated to
g_{\mu\nu}
. Using the conventions of
[
151
]
, we
switch to pre-Carroll variables

\displaystyle g_{\mu\nu}=-c^{2}T_{\mu}T_{\nu}+E_{\mu}E_{\nu}

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu}=-\frac{1}{c^{2}}V^{\mu}V^{\nu}+E^{\mu}E^{\nu}

(405)

where

\displaystyle V^{\mu}T_{\mu}=-1

\displaystyle E^{\mu}E_{\mu}=1

\displaystyle E^{\mu}E_{\nu}-V^{\mu}T_{\nu}=\delta^{\mu}_{\nu}

(406)

and the other contractions are zero. The fields
T_{\mu}
,
V^{\mu}
,
E_{\mu}
,
E^{\mu}
and
X
are assumed to be Taylor-expandable in
c^{2}
. In particular, we have to leading-order (LO) the Carroll fields

V^{\mu}=v^{\mu}+\mathcal{O}(c^{2})\qquad\qquad E_{\mu}=e_{\mu}+\mathcal{O}(c^{2})\qquad\qquad X=X+\mathcal{O}(c^{2})\,,

(407)

where we denoted the leading-order term in the dilaton expansion by
the same letter. As the subleading terms in the
X
-expansion do not
play a role in the following and both sides are invariant under
Carroll boosts this is just a convenient definition. The first goal is
to rewrite all quantities in the relativistic action
(
404
) in terms of the variables on the left-hand side of
(
407
). The relativistic Levi–Civitá connection is
thereby organized in a specific way,

{\overset{\textrm{\tiny(LC)}}{\Gamma}}{}^{\rho}{}_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}g^{\rho\alpha}\left(\partial_{\mu}g_{\alpha\nu}+\partial_{\nu}g_{\alpha\mu}-\partial_{\alpha}g_{\mu\nu}\right)=\frac{1}{c^{2}}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(-2)}{C}}{}^{\rho}{}_{\mu\nu}+\tilde{C}^{\rho}{}_{\mu\nu}+S^{\rho}{}_{\mu\nu}+c^{2}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}{C}}{}^{\rho}{}_{\mu\nu}

(408)

where all orders in
c^{2}
transform tensorially except the
c^{0}
part. This part is further split into a connection
\tilde{C}^{\rho}{}_{\mu\nu}
satisfying

\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(\tilde{C})}{\nabla}{}_{\nu}E_{\mu}=0\qquad\qquad\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(\tilde{C})}{\nabla}{}_{\nu}V^{\mu}=0

(409)

and a tensorial part
S^{\rho}{}_{\mu\nu}
. In this way, a true
Carroll connection is obtained at leading order in the limit

c\to 0
(for further details see
[
151
]
). While one
has to keep in mind that the pre-Carroll variables
E_{\mu}
and

V^{\mu}
are still Lorentz-covariant, it is useful to treat them as if
they were Carroll in order to determine the split of the

\mathcal{O}(c^{0})
part of the relativistic Levi–Civitá connection into

\tilde{C}^{\rho}{}_{\mu\nu}
and
S^{\rho}{}_{\mu\nu}
. In other words,
we introduce a pre-Carroll connection
13
13

13

In 2d, there is no rotational part of the connection and the boost
part has only one component denoted by the one-form

\tilde{\Omega}
.

\tilde{\Omega}_{\mu}
on the frame bundle which
satisfies the Carroll vielbein postulates (see also (
377
))

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}T_{\nu}-\tilde{C}^{\lambda}{}_{\mu\nu}\,T_{\lambda}+\tilde{\Omega}_{\mu}E_{\nu}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}E_{\nu}-\tilde{C}^{\lambda}{}_{\mu\nu}\,E_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=0

(410)

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}V^{\nu}+\tilde{C}^{\nu}{}_{\mu\lambda}\,V^{\lambda}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}E^{\nu}+\tilde{C}^{\nu}{}_{\mu\lambda}E^{\lambda}+V^{\nu}\tilde{\Omega}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=0

(411)

where the off-diagonal equations are compatible with (
409
). Solving for
\tilde{C}^{\rho}{}_{\mu\nu}
in terms of
\tilde{\Omega}_{\mu}
and the vielbein leads to

\displaystyle\tilde{C}^{\lambda}{}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=-V^{\lambda}\big(\partial_{\mu}T_{\nu}+\tilde{\Omega}_{\mu}E_{\nu}\big)+\frac{1}{2}\,\Pi^{\lambda\rho}\big(\partial_{\mu}\Pi_{\nu\rho}+\partial_{\nu}\Pi_{\mu\rho}-\partial_{\rho}\Pi_{\mu\nu}\big)-\Pi^{\lambda\rho}T_{\nu}\mathcal{K}_{\mu\rho}

\displaystyle=-V^{\lambda}\big(\partial_{\mu}T_{\nu}+\tilde{\Omega}_{\mu}E_{\nu}\big)+E^{\lambda}\partial_{\mu}E_{\nu}

(412)

where
\Pi_{\mu\nu}=E_{\mu}E_{\nu}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\mu\rho}
is the extrinsic curvature. In 2d, the latter object only has one component,

\mathcal{K}_{\mu\nu}=\mathcal{K}E_{\mu}E_{\nu}=-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{L}_{V}(E_{\mu}E_{\nu})\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\mathcal{K}=2E^{\mu}V^{\nu}\partial_{[\mu}E_{\nu]}~.

(413)

As usual in a second-order formulation,
\tilde{\Omega}_{\mu}
is not an independent dynamical variable but is determined in terms of the vielbein such that its torsion vanishes. However, in a
(pre-)
Carroll geometry, this can only be achieved to a certain degree as there are torsion components independent of
\tilde{\Omega}_{\mu}
, corresponding to intrinsic torsion. One, therefore, sets only those torsion components to zero that depend on the pre-Carroll connection. In 2d, this leads to only one equation,

\differential T+\tilde{\Omega}\wedge E=0

(414)

solved by

\tilde{\Omega}_{\mu}=-2E^{\nu}\partial_{[\mu}T_{\nu]}+\gamma(x^{\alpha})E_{\mu}~.

(415)

Here,
\gamma
is some arbitrary function representing the remaining freedom in the pre-Carroll connection
[
61
,
77
]
. Inserting into (
412
) yields

\tilde{C}^{\lambda}{}_{\mu\nu}=-V^{\lambda}\partial_{(\mu}T_{\nu)}-V^{\lambda}T_{(\mu}\mathcal{L}_{V}T_{\nu)}+E^{\lambda}\partial_{\mu}E_{\nu}-\gamma V^{\lambda}E_{\mu}E_{\nu}

(416)

which is not torsion-free, but contains the expected intrinsic torsion
\tilde{C}^{\rho}{}_{[\mu\nu]}=E^{\rho}\partial_{[\mu}E_{\nu]}
even in the limit
c\to 0
. While the arbitrary function
\gamma
is set to zero in
[
151
]
, we keep it, for now, to see how it contributes at later stages of the expansion. We will see below that
\gamma
does not play a role.

If we go back to (
408
) and use the pre-Carroll variables we get at order
c^{0}

{\overset{\textrm{\tiny(LC)}}{\Gamma}}{}^{\lambda}{}_{\mu\nu}\Big|_{c^{0}}=-V^{\lambda}\partial_{(\mu}T_{\nu)}-V^{\lambda}T_{(\mu}\mathcal{L}_{V}T_{\nu)}+E^{\lambda}\partial_{\mu}E_{\nu}+E^{\lambda}E^{\alpha}T_{\nu}\mathcal{K}_{\mu\alpha}=:\tilde{C}^{\lambda}{}_{\mu\nu}+S^{\lambda}{}_{\mu\nu}\,.

(417)

Using (
416
), the expansion of the Levi–Civitá connection (
408
) has coefficients

\displaystyle{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(-2)}{C}}{}^{\rho}{}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=-V^{\rho}\mathcal{K}_{\mu\nu}

(418)

\displaystyle\tilde{C}^{\rho}{}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=-V^{\rho}\partial_{(\mu}T_{\nu)}-V^{\rho}T_{(\mu}\mathcal{L}_{V}T_{\nu)}+E^{\rho}\partial_{\mu}E_{\nu}-\gamma V^{\rho}E_{\mu}E_{\nu}

(419)

\displaystyle S^{\rho}{}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=E^{\rho}E^{\lambda}T_{\nu}\mathcal{K}_{\mu\lambda}+\gamma V^{\rho}E_{\mu}E_{\nu}

(420)

\displaystyle{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}{C}}{}^{\rho}{}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=-E^{\rho}E^{\alpha}T_{(\mu}(\differential T)_{\nu)\alpha}~.

(421)

The Riemann tensor associated with the Levi–Civitá connection is defined by

{\overset{\textrm{\tiny(LC)}}{R}}{}^{\lambda}{}_{\mu\nu\sigma}=\partial_{\nu}{\overset{\textrm{\tiny(LC)}}{\Gamma}}{}^{\lambda}{}_{\sigma\mu}+{\overset{\textrm{\tiny(LC)}}{\Gamma}}{}^{\lambda}{}_{\nu\beta}{\overset{\textrm{\tiny(LC)}}{\Gamma}}{}^{\beta}{}_{\sigma\mu}-\big(\nu\leftrightarrow\sigma\big)\,.

(422)

We work directly with the Ricci tensor in the following obtained by contracting first and third indices. It can be organized as

{\overset{\textrm{\tiny(LC)}}{R}}{}_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{c^{4}}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(-4)}{R}{}_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{c^{2}}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(-2)}{R}{}_{\mu\nu}+\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}{R}{}_{\mu\nu}+c^{2}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}{R}{}_{\mu\nu}+c^{4}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(4)}{R}{}_{\mu\nu}

(423)

with the coefficients up to
\mathcal{O}(c^{2})
given by

\displaystyle\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(-4)}{R}{}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=0

(424a)

\displaystyle\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(-2)}{R}{}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(\tilde{C})}{\nabla}{}_{\lambda}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(-2)}{C}}{}^{\lambda}{}_{\nu\mu}-2\tilde{C}^{\alpha}{}_{[\nu\lambda]}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(-2)}{C}}{}^{\lambda}{}_{\alpha\mu}+S^{\lambda}{}_{\lambda\beta}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(-2)}{C}}{}^{\beta}{}_{\nu\mu}-S^{\alpha}{}_{\nu\lambda}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(-2)}{C}}{}^{\lambda}{}_{\alpha\mu}

(424b)

\displaystyle\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}{R}{}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(\tilde{C})}{R}{}_{\mu\nu}+\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(\tilde{C})}{\nabla}{}_{\lambda}S^{\lambda}{}_{\nu\mu}-\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(\tilde{C})}{\nabla}{}_{\nu}S^{\lambda}{}_{\lambda\mu}-2\tilde{C}^{\lambda}{}_{[\nu\beta]}S^{\beta}{}_{\lambda\mu}-{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(-2)}{C}}{}^{\lambda}{}_{\nu\beta}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}{C}}{}^{\beta}{}_{\lambda\mu}-{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}{C}}{}^{\lambda}{}_{\nu\beta}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(-2)}{C}}{}^{\beta}{}_{\lambda\mu}

(424c)

\displaystyle\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}{R}{}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(\tilde{C})}{\nabla}{}_{\lambda}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}{C}}{}^{\lambda}{}_{\nu\mu}-2\tilde{C}^{\alpha}{}_{[\nu\lambda]}{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}{C}}{}^{\lambda}{}_{\alpha\mu}-{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}{C}}{}^{\lambda}{}_{\nu\beta}S^{\beta}{}_{\lambda\mu}-{\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}{C}}{}^{\lambda}{}_{\alpha\mu}S^{\alpha}{}_{\nu\lambda}~.

(424d)

We use these expressions to compute the Ricci scalar expansion

\displaystyle{\overset{\textrm{\tiny(LC)}}{R}}=-\frac{1}{c^{4}}V^{\mu}V^{\nu}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(-2)}{R}{}_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{c^{2}}\Big(E^{\mu}E^{\nu}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(-2)}{R}{}_{\mu\nu}-

\displaystyle V^{\mu}V^{\nu}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}{R}{}_{\mu\nu}\Big)

\displaystyle-V^{\mu}V^{\nu}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}{R}{}_{\mu\nu}+E^{\mu}E^{\nu}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}{R}{}_{\mu\nu}+\mathcal{O}(c^{2})

(425)

leading to

\displaystyle\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(-4)}{R}

\displaystyle=0

(426)

\displaystyle\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(-2)}{R}

\displaystyle=-\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(\tilde{C})}{\nabla}{}_{\mu}\left(V^{\mu}\mathcal{K}\right)+\mathcal{K}^{2}

(427)

\displaystyle\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(0)}{R}

\displaystyle=E^{\mu}E^{\nu}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(\tilde{C})}{R}{}_{\mu\nu}-\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(\tilde{C})}{\nabla}{}_{\rho}\Big(E^{\rho}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(\tilde{C})}{\nabla}{}_{\mu}E^{\mu}\Big)+\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(\tilde{C})}{\nabla}{}_{\rho}\big(V^{\rho}\gamma\big)-\mathcal{K}\gamma

(428)

\displaystyle=E^{\mu}E^{\nu}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(\tilde{C})}{R}{}_{\mu\nu}\Big|_{\gamma=0}-\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(\tilde{C})}{\nabla}{}_{\rho}\Big(E^{\rho}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(\tilde{C})}{\nabla}{}_{\mu}E^{\mu}\Big)~.

(429)

In the last equality, we used that the last two terms in (
428
) cancel with the
\gamma
-dependence in the first term such that all the
\gamma
-dependence in the last line is in the total derivative term. In the following, we use that the Carroll compatible derivative allows writing a total divergence as

\int\differential^{2}x\det(T,E)\,\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(\tilde{C})}{\nabla}{}_{\mu}X^{\mu}=-\int\differential^{2}x\det(T,E)\,\mathcal{K}T_{\mu}X^{\mu}

(430)

up to boundary terms
[
151
]
. We are ready to expand in
c^{2}
and rewrite the relativistic dilaton gravity action (
404
) in terms of the pre-Carroll variables.

I_{\textrm{\tiny rel.}}=c^{2}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}{I}+c^{4}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(4)}{I}+\mathcal{O}(c^{6})

(431)

The first two terms in this expansion are

\displaystyle\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}{I}

\displaystyle=\frac{k_{\textrm{\tiny rel}}}{4\pi}\int\differential^{2}x\,\det(T,E)\,\Big(\mathcal{K}V^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}X+U(V^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}X)^{2}\Big)

(432)

\displaystyle\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(4)}{I}

\displaystyle=\frac{k_{\textrm{\tiny rel}}}{4\pi}\int\differential^{2}x\,\det(T,E)\,\Big(XE^{\mu}E^{\nu}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(\tilde{C})}{R}{}_{\mu\nu}\Big|_{\gamma=0}-U(X)(E^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}X)^{2}+2V(X)

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt-X\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(\tilde{C})}{\nabla}{}_{\rho}\big(E^{\rho}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(\tilde{C})}{\nabla}{}_{\mu}E^{\mu}\big)\Big)~.

(433)

To extract the magnetic action, we rewrite
\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}{I}
as

\displaystyle\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}{I}

\displaystyle=\frac{k_{\textrm{\tiny rel}}}{4\pi}\int\differential^{2}x\,\det(T,E)\,\Big(\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{K}V^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}X+\frac{1}{2}\big(\mathcal{K}+2UV^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}X\big)V^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}X\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{k_{\textrm{\tiny rel}}}{4\pi}\int\differential^{2}x\,\det(T,E)\,\Big(-c^{4}\frac{2\mathcal{K}}{V^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}X}X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}^{2}+c^{2}2X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}\mathcal{K}

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt-c^{4}\frac{2V^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}X}{\mathcal{K}+2UV^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}X}C^{2}+c^{2}2CV^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}X\Big)

(434)

where on-shell evaluation of the auxiliary fields
X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}
and
C

reproduces the action in the first line. The introduction of these auxiliary
fields
14
14

14

This is sometimes also referred to as a Hubbard–Stratonovich transformation.
effectively redistributes the powers of
c
such that

\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}{I}
is converted into terms
contributing to
\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(4)}{I}
and

\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(6)}{I}
. Like in the higher dimensional case the magnetic theory is obtained
by taking the tantum gravity scaling (
321
), which in the present case amounts to defining

k:=k_{\textrm{\tiny rel}}c^{4}
and taking the limit
c\to 0
.
Effectively, this picks out the action

\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(4)}{I}
together with the auxiliary
field contributions from
\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(2)}{I}
at
leading order. In this case the connection
\tilde{C}
reduces to the
Carroll compatible connection
\Gamma
satisfying

\nabla(e_{\mu}e_{\nu})=0=\nabla v^{\mu}
, where

\nabla
is the associated derivative. The dust settles and
we obtain the magnetic Carroll dilaton gravity action

\displaystyle I_{\textrm{\tiny mag}}^{L}[e,\tau,\rho,X_{\textrm{\tiny P}},X]=\frac{k}{4\pi}\int\,\mathcal{L}^{L}_{\textrm{\tiny mag}}

(435)

with

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{L}_{\textrm{\tiny mag}}=\differential^{2}x\,\det(\tau,e)\,\Big(X(e^{\mu}e^{\nu}R_{\mu\nu}\big|_{\gamma=0}-\nabla_{\mu}a^{\mu})

\displaystyle+2C\,v^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}X+2X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}K

\displaystyle-U(X)(e^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}X)^{2}+2V(X)\Big)

(436)

where it was assumed that the potential
V
does not scale with
c

and the leading term of
\mathcal{K}
is denoted by
K
. The field

X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}
acts as a Lagrange multiplier setting the intrinsic torsion of

\Gamma^{\rho}{}_{\mu\nu}
given by the extrinsic curvature

K
to zero. On-shell this leaves us with a Carroll torsion-free
connection satisfying the requirements of
[
77
]
.
The spatial acceleration vector
a^{\mu}
is defined in (
30
). As divergence terms
\nabla_{\mu}X^{\mu}
are independent of the ambiguous
\gamma
-dependent term in the Carroll compatible connection, we see that
\gamma
does not enter this action. Therefore, we could have set it to zero from the beginning, without loss of generality, like in
[
151
]
.
The curvature term can be further massaged by using the identity

\displaystyle e^{\mu}e^{\nu}R_{\mu\nu}\big|_{\gamma=0}=-e^{\mu}e^{\nu}\nabla_{\mu}a_{\nu}-(e^{\mu}a_{\mu})^{2}=-\nabla_{\mu}a^{\mu}

(437)

which holds only in 2d. Using additionally the definition of the
Carroll curvature scalar

R=2e^{\mu}e^{\nu}R_{\mu\nu}\big|_{\gamma=0}
we find
that the Lagrange-2-form (

) matches with (
381
).

This completes our analysis of ultrarelativistic limits and spherical reductions, showing the commutativity of the diagram in figure

. For solving the equations of motion of the two-dimensional theory it is most convenient to work in the first order formulation of subsection
