15.6
Formulation as a Poisson–Sigma model

There is another, gauge-theoretic, formulation of 2d Carroll dilaton gravity that lies at the heart of the original construction in
[
136
]
. Since some statements are phrased succinctly in this PSM formulation, we briefly summarize its main aspects.

PSMs are topological, non-linear gauge theories in 2d

[
212
,
167
]
and arise as the most general
consistent deformation of Abelian BF-theories
[
169
]
. The
PSM bulk action
[
136
]

\displaystyle\boxed{I_{\textrm{\tiny PSM}}[A_{I},X^{I}]=\frac{k}{2\pi}\,\int_{\mathcal{M}}\Big(X^{I}\differential A_{I}+\frac{1}{2}\,P^{IJ}(X^{K})\,A_{I}\wedge A_{J}\Big)}

(438)

with the field content

A_{I}=(\omega,\,\tau,\,e)\qquad\qquad X^{I}=(X,\,X_{\textrm{\tiny H}},\,X_{\textrm{\tiny P}})

(439)

and the Poisson tensor

P^{IJ}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\\
0&0&{\mathcal{V}}(X,\,X_{\textrm{\tiny H}})\\
-X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}&-{\mathcal{V}}(X,\,X_{\textrm{\tiny H}})&0\end{pmatrix}

(440)

is equivalent to the first-order action (
357
). The scalar fields
X^{I}
are target space coordinates of a Poisson manifold, and for each of them, there is an associated gauge connection 1-form
A_{I}
. The non-linear Jacobi identities

P^{IL}\partial_{L}P^{JK}+P^{JL}\partial_{L}P^{KI}+P^{KL}\partial_{L}P^{IJ}=0

(441)

hold, essentially because the potential depends only on Carroll boost-invariant combination of the target space coordinates
X^{I}
, viz.,
X
and
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}
.

The action (
438
) is invariant under the non-linear gauge symmetries

\delta_{\lambda}X^{J}=\lambda_{I}\,P^{IJ}\qquad\qquad\delta_{\lambda}A_{I}=\differential\lambda_{I}+\big(\partial_{I}P^{JK}\big)\,A_{J}\lambda_{K}\,.

(442)

It is easy to verify that (
442
) with the choices above is equivalent to (
359
)-(
361
). Also, for the special case of a linear Poisson tensor,
P^{JK}=c^{JK}{}_{I}X^{I}
with structure constants
c^{IJ}{}_{K}
one recovers the non-abelian BF theories of subsection
15.1
.

PSMs have no local physical degrees of freedom. So, all physical
excitations can be considered holographically as boundary states.
Since the Poisson tensor is anti-symmetric and hence must have an even
rank, a Poisson tensor associated with a 3-dimensional target space
like in (
440
) necessarily has a non-trivial kernel.
Physically, this kernel corresponds to a conserved Casimir that can be
interpreted as mass, as we shall see in section
17
.

Chapter 5
Carroll black holes

\epigraphfontsize

“Aaaah, so a Kaffee is herrlich!

Jaaa, direkt unentbehrlich.”

— Wolfgang Ambros, Augustin

We are now ready to investigate the solution space to the theories of Carroll dilaton gravity introduced in the last chapter. Classically, the models may be solved in full generality and in closed form which is carried out in section
16
.
Afterwards, we argue that some solutions can be interpreted as black hole-like states given two ingredients: The first is a sensible notion of thermodynamics being defined for these solutions, which we shall come to in section
17
. The other key entity is a Carroll extremal surface, a Carroll analogue of a Lorentzian extremal surface (see e.g.
[
117
]
for their classical and quantum definitions). Lorentzian extremal surfaces arise as bifurcation surfaces on Killing horizons and are thus part of eternal black hole geometries. Despite the absence of horizons in Carroll geometries, the analogue of a bifurcation surface still exists, and we declare it to be a defining property of Carroll black holes. We shall be more precise and detailed in section
18
, but for now, a schematic formula that summarizes our main definition is

\text{Carroll black hole}\;=\;\text{Carroll extremal surface}\;+\;\text{Carroll thermal properties}\,.

(443)

Afterwards, in section
19
we turn to an evaluation of the tantum gravity partition function in the saddle-point approximation which provides another independent check for the thermodynamics.
Finally, we discuss several examples for Carroll black holes in different models in sections
20
,
21
and
22
. This chapter to a large amount follows
[
113
]
as well as
[
112
]
.

16
Solutions of Carroll dilaton gravity

In order to derive all classical solutions, the first-order/PSM formulation is advantageous. In the end, we translate our solutions to the second-order formulation.

16.1
Constant dilaton vacua

Constant dilaton vacua are defined to be solutions where
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}

vanishes everywhere. The equation that normally determines the Carroll
metric (
363d
) leads to constant dilaton (hence the name). The
Carroll Casimir equation (
363e
) shows that this constant cannot
be anything but has to solve the non-differential equation

{\mathcal{V}}(X,\,0)=0\,.

(444)

In particular, this means constant dilaton vacua need infinite finetuning of the dilaton field and may not even exist for some models (the simplest example is the Carroll CGHS model where
{\mathcal{V}}=\Lambda\neq 0
).

The Carroll curvature of all constant dilaton vacua is a constant times the volume-form,

\Omega=-\partial_{X}{\mathcal{V}}(X,\,0)\,\tau\wedge e\,.

(445)

Similar remarks apply to torsion. Finally, the auxiliary field equation (
363f
) implies that also
X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}
is some (arbitrary) constant.

Since constant dilaton vacua are non-generic, require infinite finetuning, and are not very rich in structure, we move on to the generic sector, the linear dilaton vacua.

16.2
Linear dilaton vacua

Linear dilaton vacua are solutions where
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}
does not vanish
everywhere. Thus, let us start by assuming a patch where

X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\neq 0
.
15
15

15

Below, many statements implicitly come with the
qualifier “assuming
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\neq 0
”.
This allows to solve the
Carroll metric equation (
363d
) as

e=-\frac{\differential X}{X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}}\,.

(446)

Inserting this result into the Carroll Casimir equation (
363e
) yields

\frac{1}{2}\,\differential\,(X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}^{2})-{\mathcal{V}}(X,\,X_{\textrm{\tiny H}})\,\differential X=0

(447)

which allows expressing
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}
as function of the dilaton
X
and of an
integration constant
M
. We refer to
M
as Carroll mass or
Carroll Casimir. The latter nomenclature was chosen since in the
PSM formulation, the function
M(X,\,X_{\textrm{\tiny H}})
spans precisely the kernel
of the degenerate Poisson tensor (
440
). I.e., if we went to
Casimir–Darboux coordinates the expression
M(X,\,X_{\textrm{\tiny H}})
would
correspond to the Casimir direction in the Poisson manifold.

To simplify the discussion, we assume, for now,
{\mathcal{V}}=V(X)
and return to more general cases in the end. It is useful to define the integrated potential

w(X):=\int^{X}V(y)\differential y

(448)

in terms of which the conserved Carroll mass is given by

M=w(X)-\frac{1}{2}\,X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}^{2}\qquad\qquad\differential M=0\,.

(449)

The equation that establishes no intrinsic torsion, (
363c
), is solved trivially,

\differential e=0\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad e=\differential r\,.

(450)

We use
r
as our Carroll radial coordinate without loss of generality.
16
16

16

This is true only if we disregard edge modes, which we do for the time being. Once a boundary is considered with specific boundary conditions imposed on the fields, there could be a loss of generality in assuming
e=\differential r
.

Expressing
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}
as a function of
X
using the Carroll mass (
449
) and inserting it into (
446
) yields a simple differential equation for the dilaton

\boxed{\phantom{\Bigg(}\frac{\differential X}{\mp\sqrt{2(w(X)-M)}}=\differential r\phantom{\Bigg)}}

(451)

where the signs refer to the two branches of the square-root function.

To solve the remaining equations, it is convenient to fix the Carroll boosts such that
X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}=0
, which is always possible locally. The auxiliary equation (
363f
) simplifies to a constraint

\omega=-\frac{V}{X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}}\,\tau

(452)

that renders the remaining two equations, for Carroll curvature (
363a
) and torsion (
363b
), identical to each other.

Thus, there is only one more equation we need to solve, e.g., the Carroll torsion equation (
363b
). By virtue of the constraint (
452
) it simplifies to

\differential\tau+\big(\partial_{X}\ln X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\big)\,\tau\wedge\differential X=0

(453)

solved by

\tau=-X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,\differential t\,.

(454)

Here, we used the scaling ambiguity
t\to\alpha\tilde{t}
with
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
to choose some time coordinate
t
and fixed the residual Carroll boost invariance by assuming
\tau
has no
\differential r
-component. The result (
454
) implies

\omega=V(X)\,\differential t

(455)

for the Carroll boost connection. As a consequence, the Carroll curvature of our solutions is given by

\boxed{\phantom{\Bigg(}\Omega=-V^{\prime}(X)\,\tau\wedge e=-V^{\prime}(X)\,\differential t\wedge\differential X\,.\phantom{\Bigg)}}

(456)

In summary, in the chosen gauge, the solution reads

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle=\textrm{given\;by\;integrating\;(\ref{eq:car9})}

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle=V(X)\,\differential t

(457a)

\displaystyle X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}

\displaystyle=\pm\sqrt{2(w(X)-M)}

\displaystyle\tau

\displaystyle=-X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,\differential t

(457b)

\displaystyle X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle e

\displaystyle=\differential r\,.

(457c)

Translating our solution to the second-order formulation as described in section
15.3
yields the metric

\boxed{\phantom{\Bigg(}\differential s^{2}=h_{\mu\nu}\,\differential x^{\mu}\differential x^{\nu}=e_{\mu}e_{\nu}\,\differential x^{\mu}\differential x^{\nu}=\differential r^{2}\phantom{\Bigg)}}

(458)

and the timelike vector field

\boxed{\phantom{\Bigg(}v=v^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}=\frac{1}{X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}}\,\partial_{t}=\pm\frac{1}{\sqrt{2(w(X)-M)}}\,\partial_{t}\,.\phantom{\Bigg)}}

(459)

The dilaton field
X
is still given by integrating (
451
).

Finally, we come back to more general cases when the potential
\mathcal{V}
does not only depend on the dilaton
X
but additionally depends on the boost invariant scalar
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}
. We discuss here the family of models (
371
) and refer by analogy to
[
142
]
for further generalizations.

We continue to fix the radial coordinate by
e=\differential r
, so the Carroll metric is still given by (
458
). Exploiting the Weyl rescalings discussed after (
364
), we find that we need a modified definition of the function
w
, namely

w(X):=\int^{X}e^{Q(y)}V(y)\differential y\qquad\qquad e^{Q(X)}:=e^{\int^{X}U(y)\differential y}\,.

(460)

The additive integration constant implicit in the function
w(X)
can be absorbed by shifting the mass
M
. The multiplicative integration constant implicit in
e^{Q(X)}
can be chosen to give this expression the desired physical dimensions, discussed in subsection
17.4
below.

Relatedly, the Carroll mass is changed slightly

M=w(X)-\frac{1}{2}\,X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}^{2}\,e^{Q(X)}

(461)

which changes also the boost invariant scalar

X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}=\pm\sqrt{2e^{-Q(X)}(w(X)-M)}\,.

(462)

The dilaton is obtained by integrating

\frac{\differential X}{\mp\sqrt{2e^{-Q(X)}(w(X)-M)}}=\differential r\,.

(463)

Fixing, again, Carroll boosts suitably recovers
X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}=0
and

\tau=-e^{Q(X)}\,X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,\differential t\,.

(464)

The analogue of the constraint (
452
) implies

\omega=e^{Q(X)}\,{\mathcal{V}}(X,\,X_{\textrm{\tiny H}})\,\differential t\,.

(465)

Finally, the timelike vector field is given by

v=v^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}=\pm\frac{1}{\sqrt{2e^{Q(X)}(w(X)-M)}}\,\partial_{t}\,.

(466)

Carroll curvature evaluates as

\Omega=-\big(V^{\prime}(X)-\tfrac{1}{2}X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}^{2}U^{\prime}(X)\big)\,\tau\wedge e=e^{Q(X)}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}^{2}U^{\prime}(X)-V^{\prime}(X)\big)\,\differential t\wedge\differential X\,.

(467)

16.3
Carroll Birkhoff theorem

In Lorentzian dilaton gravity, there is a generalized Birkhoff
theorem, in the sense that all solutions to all models have at least
one Killing vector, see e.g.
[
134
]
and references
therein.

In the Carroll case, we see similar features: in the constant
dilaton sector, all solutions have constant curvature and constant
scalar fields, so there is a sense in which these configurations are
maximally symmetric. However, let us focus on the less trivial linear
dilaton vacua.

A minimal requirement to define a Carroll Killing vector is that all boost-invariant fields are invariant under Lie transport along it. This establishes the conditions

{\mathcal{L}}_{\xi}h_{\mu\nu}={\mathcal{L}}_{\xi}v^{\mu}={\mathcal{L}}_{\xi}X=0\,.

(468)

The last condition implies
\xi^{r}=0
, the second condition yields
\partial_{t}\xi^{t}=0
, and the first condition imposes no further restriction. Thus, any vector field

\xi^{\mu}\,\partial_{\mu}=f(r)\,\partial_{t}

(469)

preserves the boost-invariant structure. Analogous to the arguments in subsection
7.3
one may additionally require invariance of the Cartan variables up to local tangent space transformations which are just Carroll boosts in this case. Invariance of the connection
\omega
leads to a second order ODE for
f(r)
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\omega-\differential\lambda=0\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\tau_{t}\partial_{r}^{2}f+\partial_{r}f(\omega_{t}+\partial_{r}\tau_{t})=0

(470)

where the Carroll boost parameter is given by
\lambda=-\tau_{t}\partial_{r}f
upon using (
359
). Further, for the solutions in the previous subsection we automatically have
\mathcal{L}_{\xi}X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}=0
but get another condition from
X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\xi}X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}-\lambda X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}=0\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\partial_{r}f=0

(471)

This just leaves us with
f=\text{cst.}
which is the generator of ordinary time translations. The remaining condition
\mathcal{L}_{\xi}\tau+\lambda e=0
is automatically satisfied. This Carroll Killing vector exists for all solutions of all 2d Carroll dilaton gravity models. We refer to this statement as “Carroll Birkhoff theorem”.

16.4
Singularities of Carroll manifolds

It is not the purpose of this work to provide a comprehensive
discussion of Carroll singularities. Nevertheless, we need to
confront three types of singularities since they naturally and rather
generically occur in 2d Carroll dilaton gravity.

1.

Carroll coordinate singularities

2.

Carroll curvature singularities

3.

Carroll structure singularities

The first type of coordinate singularity can arise much in the same
way as in general relativity. The prototypical example is
Schwarzschild-gauge, which in our context is obtained by using the
radial coordinate

\differential\rho=e^{Q(X)}\,\differential X

(472)

in terms of which the Carroll metric reads

h_{\mu\nu}\,\differential x^{\mu}\differential x^{\nu}=\frac{\differential\rho^{2}}{2e^{Q(X)}(w(X)-M)}\,.

(473)

There is a (coordinate-)singularity at zeros of
w(X)-M
. We shall use this singular coordinate system when comparing with Schwarzschild–Tangherlini solutions in section
20.3
.

Concerning curvature singularities, we merely note that they can (and
quite often do) arise, typically in the strong-coupling region where
the dilaton tends to zero. Perhaps there is nothing more to this type
of singularity than the observation that curvature can become infinite
if gravity is infinitely strong.

The third type is a singularity reminiscent of what happens at the
bifurcation sphere of the Schwarzschild black hole (see, e.g.,

[
144
]
), in the following sense. The attentive
reader will have realized already that there is a remaining issue in
our classification of solutions: first, we assumed
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}=0
everywhere
(constant dilaton vacua) and then we assumed
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\neq 0
everywhere
(linear dilaton vacua) but what if
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}
vanishes or diverges at
isolated loci? That this can happen is evident from the explicit
solution (
457
) for linear dilaton vacua: The equation
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}=0

can have solutions for certain values of the radial coordinate
r
,
depending on the choice of the potential and the value of the mass

M
. Thus, we have potentially singular points in the interior or the
boundary of linear dilaton vacua. The difference to the Lorentzian
case is that there the singularity analogous to the one at
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}=0
is
merely a coordinate singularity of Eddington–Finkelstein patches and
can be removed by going into, say, Kruskal coordinates, see
e.g.
[
176
]
. By contrast, in the Carroll case, there
is no coordinate system where both the metric and the vector field are
non-zero and finite at
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}=0
.

The physical interpretation of these potential singularities is
simple: the timelike vector field either blows up (
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\to 0
) or
collapses to zero (
|X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}|\to\infty
). We call this a singularity in
the Carroll structure. Note that curvature (
456
) may
remain finite at such loci.

In section
18
, where we define Carroll extremal surfaces, we employ these intriguing loci where
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}
vanishes, independently of the behaviour of curvature.

X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}=0\qquad\leftrightarrow\qquad e^{-Q(X)}(w(X)-M)=0

(474)

We move on to the global properties of Carroll thermal manifolds next, where such loci also play a decisive role.

16.5
Global aspects of Carroll thermal solutions

Before we provide further motivation and details, let us define a Carroll thermal (C-thermal) manifold:

Definition 16.1

(C-thermal manifolds)
.

()

C-thermal manifolds (in 2d) are smooth manifolds
\mathcal{M}
carrying a Carroll structure up to isolated points, with a boundary
\partial\mathcal{M}
diffeomorphic to
S^{1}
.

Thus, we are relaxing the original definition of a Carroll manifold

[
110
]
, which disallowed these isolated singularities in
the Carroll structure. For a more mathematically rigorous treatment of such situations we refer to
[
71
]
. As we do not investigate any form of matter coupled to this theory yet, the word “thermal” cannot be related to its full physical meaning. We shall see, however, in section
17
that it is still
natural to use this terminology because of a corresponding term
appearing in the first law. Moreover, it is shown in chapter
6
that once matter is introduced on such backgrounds it is in fact possible to have true thermal behaviour of its states. To amalgamate these conflicting indicators
in favour and against a Carroll version of temperature, we still refer to
these geometries as C-thermal.

In analogy to the Euclidean case, we compactify the orbits of a
Carroll Killing vector field
\xi
associated with time
translations. Taking
\xi=\partial_{t}
we identify
17
17

17

As in the Lorentzian case, we analytically continue to imaginary
time and change to complexified frame variables and spin connection,
see section
21
for an explicit example. Unlike the
Lorentzian case, this has no consequence on the signature, which
remains
(0,+)
. To reduce clutter we refrain from introducing Wick
rotated variables here.
points
p\in\mathcal{M}
along the action

p\sim e^{\beta\xi}\cdot p\qquad\qquad\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{+}

(475)

where
e^{\beta\xi}\,\cdot
means flowing the point
p
along the
integral curve of
\xi
by a parameter difference
\beta
.

Having in mind a Carroll analogue of Euclidean dilaton gravity, we
introduce a (cut-off) boundary at some large positive value of the
dilaton,
X(r_{c})=X_{c}
. The two topologies of interest to us are
cylinder and disk. C-thermal manifolds, by definition, require the
latter.

The natural topology for a 2d Carroll manifold with compactified
time direction is a cylinder, i.e., a direct product manifold of the
spatial line and the temporal cycle. Assuming the temporal cycle to be
finite and non-zero globally, only cylinders are possible. Thus, if we
insisted on the absence of Carroll structure singularities
C-thermal manifolds, which have disk topology, would be impossible.

A smooth disk is obtained by demanding the temporal cycle to shrink to
a point at the locus
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\to 0
such that the manifold is smooth
there. Before tackling this issue, we address general aspects of
Carroll manifolds with a Carroll structure singularity induced by

X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\to 0
in the centre of the disk.

The frame we define on such a manifold is given by

v=\frac{1}{e^{Q}X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}}\,\partial_{t}\qquad\qquad e=\partial_{r}

(476)

where the coordinate
t
is compactified,
t\sim t+\beta
. Similarly
to a frame adapted to polar coordinates on a Euclidean disk, it
becomes singular at the origin. This is not only because of the
divergence in
v
but also because the very notion of tangent and
radial directions cannot be defined at the origin. The way to still
obtain a global orthonormal frame field in the Euclidean case is to
perform an
SO(2)
-rotation to a Cartesian frame that can be extended
to the origin. In the general case, this could be done locally such
that, e.g., asymptotically one still has a polar frame while one
switches to a Cartesian one in a neighbourhood of the centre with
corresponding transition functions on the overlap.

In the Carroll case, however, this is not possible: The
transformations acting on the frame belong to the homogeneous Carroll
group
\text{Carr}(2,\mathbb{R})=ISO(1)
, which leaves
v
invariant.
So, starting with (
476
) asymptotically,
one necessarily arrives at a singular description of the origin. This
is just another way of stating the presence of a Carroll structure
singularity at this locus.

One can quantify this singularity by picking a loop around the origin

\gamma:[0,1]\to\mathcal{M}
parametrized by

\sigma\mapsto x^{\mu}(\sigma)
and computing its associated holonomy

H_{\gamma}(\omega)
for the connection (
465
). The
parallel transport equation

\displaystyle\frac{\differential}{\differential\sigma}V^{A}+\frac{\differential x^{\mu}}{\differential\sigma}\omega^{A}{}_{\mu B}V^{B}=0

(477)

is solved by

\displaystyle V^{A}\big(\gamma(\sigma=1)\big)=\exp[-\int_{0}^{1}\differential\sigma\,\dot{x}^{\mu}\omega_{\mu}\Big]^{A}{}_{B}\,V^{B}\big(\gamma(\sigma=0)\big)=\big(H_{\gamma}\big)^{A}{}_{B}V^{B}\big(\gamma(\sigma=0)\big)

(478)

where
\dot{x}^{\mu}
denotes
\frac{\differential}{\differential\sigma}x^{\mu}
and
\dot{x}^{\mu}\omega_{\mu}
is a matrix with components
\dot{x}^{\mu}\omega^{A}{}_{\mu B}
describing the representation of the homogeneous Carroll algebra on tangent space (see also subsection
7
). Using the solution (
465
) and choosing the loop
x^{\mu}(\sigma)=\big(\sigma\beta,r_{0}\big)
with
r_{0}=\text{const.}
we obtain

H_{\gamma}(\omega)=\begin{pmatrix}1&&-\beta e^{Q}\mathcal{V}(X,X_{\textrm{\tiny H}})\big|_{r=r_{0}}\\
0&&1\end{pmatrix}~.

(479)

Then, contracting
\gamma
to a point at the origin yields

\displaystyle\lim_{r_{0}\to 0}H_{\gamma}(\omega)=\begin{pmatrix}1&-\beta w^{\prime}(X)\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\Big|_{r=0}

(480)

where, without loss of generality, the integration constant in
(
463
) was chosen such that
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}(r=0)=0
. We stress that
while the Carroll spin connection is ambiguous and only defined up to
the addition of a term
\rho\,e
(see section
15.3
), this
ambiguity does not enter here because the loop is chosen such that

\dot{x}^{\mu}e_{\mu}=0
.

Let us return to the smoothness condition at the origin of the
disk. In a Euclidean theory of gravity, one requires that the closer

\gamma
approaches the origin, the more the holonomy approaches the
one of a flat disk. The geometry of the latter is given by

\displaystyle v^{\text{\tiny E,Disk}}=\frac{1}{r}\,\partial_{\varphi}\qquad\quad e^{\text{\tiny E,Disk}}=\partial_{r}\qquad\quad\big(\omega^{\text{\tiny E,Disk}}\big)^{a}{}_{b}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\differential\varphi\\
-\differential\varphi&0\end{pmatrix}

(481)

with the torsion-free spin connection
\omega^{\text{\tiny E,Disk}}
and
\varphi\sim\varphi+2\pi
. Explicitly, the condition reads

\displaystyle\lim_{r_{0}\to 0}H_{\gamma}(\omega^{\text{\tiny E}})\overset{!}{=}H_{\gamma}(\omega^{\text{\tiny E,Disk}})=\exp\begin{pmatrix}0&-2\pi\\
2\pi&0\end{pmatrix}

(482)

where
\omega^{E}
is some curved connection. This condition fixes the Hawking
temperature. We use the same definition for the Carroll
case: A flat Carroll disk is given by

\displaystyle v^{\text{\tiny C,Disk}}=\frac{1}{r}\,\partial_{\varphi}\qquad\quad e^{\text{\tiny C,Disk}}=\partial_{r}\qquad\quad\big(\omega^{\text{\tiny C,Disk}}\big)^{a}{}_{b}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\differential\varphi\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}~,

(483)

where the ambiguity in the spin connection has again been neglected because it does not contribute to the holonomy integral. The condition we arrive at is

\boxed{\phantom{\Bigg(}\lim_{r_{0}\to 0}H_{\gamma}(\omega)\overset{!}{=}H_{\gamma}(\omega^{\text{\tiny C,Disk}})=\exp\begin{pmatrix}0&-2\pi\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\phantom{\Bigg)}}

(484)

Therefore, in Carroll theories of gravity, the holonomy is never equal to the identity for contractible loops around the origin, which is precisely because of the Carroll structure singularity.

As another equivalent way to ensure a smooth disk, we use the
Gauss–Bonnet formula rewritten in first-order variables,

2\pi\chi=\int_{\mathcal{M}}\differential\omega-\int_{\partial\mathcal{M}}\omega

(485)

where implicitly, we assume the bulk term
\differential\omega
does not yield

\delta
-like contributions (corresponding to deficit
angles).
18
18

18

In general, there is an additional subtlety.
Namely, depending on the frame, one may need to subtract an auxiliary
connection
\omega_{0}
to ensure gauge invariance. The boundary
integral is equivalent to an integral of the second fundamental
form. However, in our chosen frame, this turns out to be
unnecessary.
This assumption, in general, is incorrect unless the
periodicity
\beta
takes certain values.

In other words, while often a formula like
(
485
)
is used
to compute
\chi
for given geometrical data on a manifold, we reverse
the logic: Taking
\chi=1
and demanding

\boxed{\phantom{\Bigg(}2\pi\overset{!}{=}\int_{\mathcal{M}}\differential\omega-\int_{\partial\mathcal{M}}\omega\phantom{\Bigg)}}

(486)

ensures that no conical defects appear while
\mathcal{M}
is
topologically a disk provided the periodicity
\beta
is chosen
appropriately, which we shall do in the next section. Here, the
boundary integral is understood along the surface
X=X_{c}
with
outward-pointing unit normal form
n=-X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}^{-1}\differential X
and with a
volume form
\text{vol}_{\partial\mathcal{M}}
induced by

\tau\wedge e=n\wedge\text{vol}_{\partial\mathcal{M}}
such that
Stokes’ theorem holds. In particular, this implies

\int_{\partial\mathcal{M}}\omega=-\int_{0}^{\beta}e^{Q}\mathcal{V}\differential t
.

While the spin connection is undefined at the origin of the disk,
the integrand of (
486
) is defined up to this isolated
point, and we can continuously extend

\differential\omega=2e^{\mu}v^{\nu}\partial_{[\mu}\omega_{\nu]}\tau\wedge e
to the origin. On-shell the limit

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to 0}2e^{\mu}v^{\nu}\partial_{[\mu}\omega_{\nu]}\big|_{\textrm{\tiny EOM}}=-w^{\prime\prime}(X)\big|_{r=0}

(487)

exists whenever
w^{\prime\prime}(X)|_{r=0}
is finite. Thus, we can continue
the integrand to the origin in such cases. As the resulting
contribution to the integral has measure zero, we find that the formula
(
486
) is not even sensitive to the Carroll structure
singularity and therefore provides another suitable device to probe
disk topology. We shall see in the next section how
(
486
) can be used to fix the Carroll temperature in
terms of the dilaton potential.

17
Carroll thermal properties

In this section, we discuss the C-thermal properties of the linear
dilaton solutions derived in section
16.2
. In subsection

17.1
, we derive the energy from the usual boundary charges.
In subsection
17.2
, we define two different notions of
temperature and show that they coincide with each other. In subsection

17.3
, we address entropy and the first law of Carroll
thermodynamics. In subsection
17.4
, we perform a
dimensional analysis to ensure dimensionless entropy. Finally,
in subsection
17.5
, we calculate the specific heat.

17.1
Energy

The canonical codimension-2 charge variations for a generic PSM
(
438
) are (see for instance Eq. (6.1) in
[
137
]
)

\displaystyle\not{\delta}\mathcal{Q}_{\lambda}=\frac{k}{2\pi}\,\lambda_{I}\,\delta X^{I}\Big|_{\partial\mathcal{M}}

(488)

with boundary condition-preserving gauge parameters
19
19

19

These
parameters, in general, depend on the fields, which can make this
expression non-integrable in field space. To account for this
possibility, we denote the charge variation by
\not{\delta}
.

\lambda_{I}
. We assume that the boundary conditions imposed on
X
and

X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}
are such that they allow arbitrary variations of the mass
parameter
M
. Since we do not want to be too specific about these
boundary conditions at this stage, we just impose that diffeomorphisms
generated by the Killing vector
\xi=\partial_{t}
are part of the
asymptotic symmetries that preserve the boundary conditions. The
associated gauge parameters are given by

\lambda_{X}=\omega_{t}=e^{Q}\mathcal{V}
,
\lambda_{H}=\tau_{t}=-e^{Q}X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}
,

\lambda_{P}=e_{t}=0
.

The charge variation (
488
) associated with unit time translations is given by

\delta\mathcal{Q}_{\partial_{t}}=\frac{k}{2\pi}\,\big(e^{Q}\mathcal{V}(X,X_{\textrm{\tiny H}})\,\delta X-e^{Q}X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,\delta X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\big)\big|_{\partial\mathcal{M}}=\frac{k}{2\pi}\,\delta M

(489)

where in the last equality, we used the (variation of the) Casimir relation (
461
). Since
M
is totally conserved,
\differential M=0
, it does not matter where this quantity is evaluated, which is why we dropped the indicator
|_{\partial\mathcal{M}}
.

The charge (
489
) is integrable in field space and gives a simple expression for the energy,
E=\mathcal{Q}_{\partial_{t}}
, in terms of the mass parameter:

\boxed{\phantom{\Bigg(}E=\frac{k}{2\pi}\,M\phantom{\Bigg)}}

(490)

17.2
Temperature

Following the discussion in section
16.5
we impose
equation (
486
) to ensure having a Carroll disk
without any defects. Inserting the solutions of section

16.2
and choosing an orientation such that

\tau\wedge e=:e^{Q}\differential t\differential X
we find

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{M}}\differential\omega

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathcal{M}}\partial_{X}\mathcal{V}\,\tau\wedge e=\int_{0}^{\beta}\int_{X_{\textrm{\tiny min}}}^{X_{c}}\differential t\differential X\partial_{X}\Big(U(w-M)-\partial_{X}w\Big)

(491)

\displaystyle=\beta\Big(U(w-M)-\partial_{X}w\Big)\Big|^{X_{c}}_{X_{\textrm{\tiny min}}}

(492)

\displaystyle\int_{\partial\mathcal{M}}\omega

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{\beta}e^{Q}\mathcal{V}\differential t\Big|^{X_{c}}=-\beta\Big(\partial_{X}w-U(w-M)\Big)\Big|^{X_{c}}

(493)

such that (
486
) reads

\displaystyle 2\pi\overset{!}{=}\beta\,\partial_{X}w\big|_{X_{\textrm{\tiny min}}}~.

(494)

Here
X_{\textrm{\tiny min}}
is the value of the dilaton at the locus
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}=0
, taking the positive branch in (
451
). Interpreting
\beta=T^{-1}
as inverse Carroll temperature establishes

\boxed{\phantom{\Bigg(}T=\frac{w^{\prime}(X_{\textrm{\tiny min}})}{2\pi}\,.\phantom{\Bigg)}}

(495)

The result for the Carroll temperature (
495
) is equivalent to the corresponding Lorentzian result for the Hawking temperature of 2d dilaton gravity with the same potentials (
371
).

In addition to this topological derivation of Carroll temperature, there is also a definition in terms of Carroll surface gravity.

\nabla_{\mu}\big(e^{Q}e^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}X\big)\big|_{X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}=0}=:\hat{\kappa}\,e_{\mu}\big|_{X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}=0}

(496)

The quantity in parentheses is proportional to
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}
on-shell and thus vanishes at
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}=0
. In this sense, the definition of
\kappa
in (
496
) is analogous to the definition of surface gravity in a Lorentzian theory. Taking the solutions (
457
) yields
\hat{\kappa}=w^{\prime}(X_{\textrm{\tiny min}})
. Therefore, we recover the anticipated relation

T=\frac{\hat{\kappa}}{2\pi}

(497)

between Carroll temperature
T
and Carroll surface gravity
\hat{\kappa}
.

17.3
Entropy and first law

As the last missing piece for the first law, let us inspect the
definition of the entropy along the lines of Wald
[
230
]
.
Working in the covariant phase space formalism of first-order Carroll
dilaton gravity (see, e.g.,
[
205
]
) the symplectic
form is given by

\varpi(\delta_{1}\phi,\delta_{2}\phi)=\frac{k}{2\pi}\Big(\delta_{2}X^{I}\delta_{1}A_{I}-\delta_{1}X^{I}\delta_{2}A_{I}\Big)

(498)

where we used PSM variables (
439
) for convenience and denote the collection of fields by
\phi
. Contracting in a diffeomorphism generated by some vector field
\xi
on the worldsheet and evaluating on-shell yields the fundamental theorem of covariant phase space

\varpi(\delta\phi,\delta_{\xi}\phi)\approx\differential\Big(\delta Q_{\xi}-Q_{\delta\xi}-i_{\xi}\Theta(\delta\phi)\Big)=:\differential\not{\delta}\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}

(499)

where
Q_{\xi}
is the Noether–Wald charge. The variation of the codimension-2 charges is given by

\not{\delta}\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}=\frac{k}{2\pi}\xi^{\mu}A_{I\,\mu}\,\delta X^{I}~,

(500)

which just reproduces the special case
\lambda_{I}=A_{I\mu}\xi^{\mu}
of the more general result (
488
).
We choose
\xi
to be the Carroll Killing vector associated with unit time translations,

\xi=\partial_{t}

(501)

which implies
\varpi(\delta\phi,\delta_{\xi}\phi)=0
. Additionally, we pick a constant time hypersurface
\Sigma
extending from the point
\mathcal{E}=\{p\in\mathcal{M}:X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}=0\}
in the interior to a point
\mathcal{B}\in\partial\mathcal{M}
on the asymptotic boundary such that
\partial\Sigma=\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{B}
. Integrating (
499
) over
\Sigma
leads to the on-shell identity

\int_{\Sigma}\varpi(\delta\phi,\delta_{\xi}\phi)\approx\int_{\Sigma}\differential\not{\delta}\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}=\not{\delta}\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}\Big|_{\mathcal{B}}-\not{\delta}\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}\Big|_{\mathcal{E}}=0~.

(502)

Explicitly,
\not{\delta}\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}
reads on-shell

\displaystyle\not{\delta}\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}=\frac{k}{2\pi}\Big(e^{Q}\Big(V-\frac{U}{2}X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}^{2}\Big)\delta X-e^{Q}X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\delta X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\Big)

(503)

and evaluates at the two points of
\partial\Sigma
to

\displaystyle\not{\delta}\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}\Big|_{\mathcal{B}}

\displaystyle=\frac{k}{2\pi}\,\delta M

\displaystyle\not{\delta}\mathcal{Q}_{\xi}\Big|_{\mathcal{E}}

\displaystyle=\frac{k}{2\pi}e^{Q}V\,\delta X\Big|_{\mathcal{E}}~.

(504)

From (
502
) we therefore find

\displaystyle\frac{k}{2\pi}\,\delta M=\Big(\frac{e^{Q(X)}V(X)}{2\pi}\,k\,\delta X\Big)\Big|_{\mathcal{E}}

(505)

which together with the results (
490
) and (
495
) takes the form of a first law,

\delta E=T\,\delta S~.

(506)

The new thermodynamic quantity defined here is the entropy

\boxed{\phantom{\Bigg(}S:=k\,X_{\textrm{\tiny min}}\,.\phantom{\Bigg)}}

(507)

and arises as a Noether charge, just as in the relativistic case. Its functional form in terms of the dilaton also matches precisely with the one of relativistic dilaton gravity
[
130
]
. In words, entropy is given by the value of the dilaton at the Carroll extremal surface (defined in the next section), times the coupling constant.

17.4
A word about dimensions

If we assign standard units to all variables in the action, then
entropy turns out to have a non-standard dimension of velocity. The
quickest way to see this is first to verify that the connection

\omega
necessarily has the dimension of inverse velocity, assuming
that
\tau
has time dimension and
e
length dimension. This
statement follows from the Carroll torsion equation (
363b
). The
first term in the action (
357
) has a dimension of

[kX\omega]
and, in units where
\kappa=1
, this combination must be
dimensionless. This follows from
\kappa^{-1}
being the prefactor of the exponent in a putative tantum gravity partition function (see also section

19
). Thus, we find that the dimension of entropy
(
507
),

[S]=[kX]=\frac{\textrm{length}}{\textrm{time}}=\textrm{velocity}
, is unusual.

If one wants to recover a dimensionless entropy (e.g. measured in

e
-bits), one has to introduce a velocity as a conversion factor. In
Carroll theories, there is no natural choice for such a conversion
factor, but if viewed as an expansion of a Lorentzian theory, we have
a velocity available, namely the speed of light. Even for intrinsic
Carroll theories, we shall assume the presence of some quantity
with the dimension of velocity and convert time into length. This
assumption permits
\tau
to have length dimension and hence
\omega

to be dimensionless. In the following, we denote the length dimensions
by integers
[\bullet]=n
, meaning that the corresponding quantity

\bullet
has length dimension
n
(if
n
is negative, the quantity

\bullet
has corresponding inverse length dimension). For instance,
our choice above means
[e]=[\tau]=[r]=[t]=1
and
[\omega]=0
.

The remaining freedom is which length dimension to assign to the
dilaton field. From an intrinsic 2d perspective, the only natural
choice is to assume dimensionless dilaton,
[X]=0
, implying also

[k]=0
. The dimensions of all other quantities follow from this
assignment and compatibility with the equations of motion
(
363
):
[{\mathcal{V}}]=-2
,
[X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}]=[X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}]=[M]=[w]=[v]=-1
,

[\Omega]=0
,
[e^{Q}]=+1
. The only subtlety (known already from the
Lorentzian counterpart) is the last assignment and can be attributed
to a length dimension carried by the (otherwise irrelevant)
multiplicative integration constant inherent to the definition of

e^{Q}
, see (
460
).
20
20

20

Since
[X]=0
but
[{\mathcal{V}}]=-2
, the potential generically
contains some relevant coupling constant. We can always use an
appropriate power of that constant for the multiplicative
integration constant in
e^{Q}
.

We deduce the dimensions of our thermodynamical quantities as

[E]=[T]=-1
and
[S]=0
. In particular, the entropy is dimensionless,
and inverse temperature
\beta
has length dimension consistently with
our starting point of assigning time a length dimension.

17.5
Specific heat

With the quantities obtained so far, we define a Carroll specific heat as

C:=\frac{\differential E}{\differential T}=T\,\frac{\differential S}{\differential T}

(508)

yielding

C=k\,\frac{w^{\prime}(X_{\textrm{\tiny min.}})}{w^{\prime\prime}(X_{\textrm{\tiny min.}})}~.

(509)

The equivalence of this expression to the Lorentzian case
[
139
]
is worth highlighting. Assuming positive temperature,
w^{\prime}(X_{\textrm{\tiny min.}})>0
, specific heat is positive if and only if
w^{\prime\prime}(X_{\textrm{\tiny min.}})>0
.

Having investigated the C-thermal properties of linear dilaton solutions with Carroll structure singularities, we define Carroll extremal surfaces and Carroll black holes in the next section.

18
Carroll extremal surfaces

In this section, we introduce the geometric notion of Carroll extremal
surfaces, guided by corresponding Lorentzian results. We start in
subsection
18.1
with a translation of standard (relativistic)
extremal surfaces into the PSM formulation. We copy this definition in
subsection
18.2
and apply it to define Carroll extremal
surfaces. We translate back this definition into first- and
second-order formulations of Carroll gravity in subsection

18.3
. Finally, we collect our results to define Carroll black
holes in subsection
18.4
.

18.1
Standard extremal surfaces in PSM formulation

Our first task is to translate the notion of an extremal surface (both
null expansions vanish, see, e.g.
[
144
]
) into the
PSM formulation. For relativistic 2d dilaton gravity in the PSM
formulation the Poisson tensor is given by
[
142
]

P^{IJ}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-X^{+}&X^{-}\\
P^{+\text{\tiny X}}=-P^{\text{\tiny X}+}&0&{\mathcal{V}}(X,\,X^{+}X^{-})\\
P^{-\text{\tiny X}}=-P^{\text{\tiny X}-}&P^{-+}=-P^{+-}&0\end{pmatrix}

(510)

and the worldsheet metric is written in terms of the zweibein as

\differential s^{2}=2e^{+}e^{-}~.

(511)

On-shell the latter is given by

\differential s^{2}=2e^{Q}\differential v\,\differential X+2e^{2Q}X^{+}X^{-}\,\differential v^{2}

(512)

where
Q
is a known function of the dilaton
X
and of an integration
constant, the mass
M
. The Lorentz invariant combination
X^{+}X^{-}

also can be expressed as such a function, using the conserved Casimir
inherent to the Poisson tensor (
510
). The metric
(
512
) allows expressing worldsheet features in terms of
conditions on the target space coordinates
X^{I}
. In the table below
we summarize the relativistic interpretation of various loci on the
worldsheet in terms of the signs of
X^{\pm}
.

signs

X^{+}>0

X^{+}<0

\boldsymbol{X^{+}=0}

X^{-}>0

anti-trapped

anti-normal

marginally anti-trapped

X^{-}<0

normal

trapped

marginally trapped

\boldsymbol{X^{-}=0}

marginally anti-trapped

marginally trapped

extremal

For Kruskal-type of spacetimes “normal” refers to the outside
region, “anti-normal” to the second outside region, “trapped” to
the black hole region, “anti-trapped” to the white hole region,
“marginally trapped” and “marginally anti-trapped” to the
bifurcate Killing horizon and “extremal” to the bifurcation point.
See Fig.
5
for a reminder.

marginally trapped
marginally anti-trapped
normal
anti-normal
anti-trapped
trapped
extremal

Figure 5:
Kruskal diagram of Lorentzian eternal black hole

A nice way of expressing what is special about extremal surfaces from a PSM perspective is to consider the action of relativistic boosts on the target space coordinates,

\delta_{\lambda}X=0\qquad\qquad\delta_{\lambda}X^{\pm}=\mp X^{\pm}\,\lambda\,.

(513)

Comparing with the table above, marginally trapped or anti-trapped surfaces are fixed lines (though not fixed-point lines) under boosts, since e.g. every locus
X^{+}=0
is mapped to another locus where
X^{+}=0
. This provides a target space notion of marginally
(anti-)
trapped surfaces as fixed lines under boosts.

Similarly, by inserting the definition of extremal loci from the table above, we see that extremal surfaces are fixed points with respect to boosts: all the target space coordinates are invariant under boosts on the extremal locus.

PSM definition of relativistic extremal surfaces.

Relativistic extremal surfaces are loci in the PSM target space that are fixed points under relativistic boosts.

This is the kind of property we were after. We have a definition of
extremal surfaces as loci in the target space where the Poisson tensor
is invariant under the gauge symmetries associated with boosts. Since
we do not need the boosts to be relativistic for this definition to
apply, it readily generalizes to Carroll boosts and thus allows
defining Carroll extremal surfaces, which we shall do in the next
subsection. Before doing so, we translate back the definition above
into a more familiar language.

In the second-order formulation, the target space coordinates
X^{\pm}
do not exist but are replaced on-shell by directional derivatives of the dilaton field projected along the vielbein components.

X^{\pm}\approx\pm e^{\mu}_{\pm}\partial_{\mu}X

(514)

An extremal surface in the second-order formulation is thus a locus where the dilaton has a saddle point or an extremum.

\textrm{relativistic\;extremal\;surface:}\qquad e^{\mu}_{\pm}\partial_{\mu}X=0\qquad\qquad X>0

(515)

This is compatible with higher-dimensional intuition if 2d dilaton gravity is viewed as a dimensional reduction from higher-dimensional gravity. The condition of positive
X
was added to eliminate ludicrous cases of fake extremal surfaces, which from a higher-dimensional perspective are spherical coordinate singularities, and from a 2d perspective, are singular loci where the effective gravitational coupling diverges.
21
21

21

A simple example of such a fake extremal surface is the centre of 4d Minkowski space in spherical coordinates, with
X=r^{2}
. The quantity
e^{\mu}_{\pm}\partial_{\mu}X\propto\pm\partial_{r}X=2r
vanishes at the origin
r=0
.

Similarly, we define (non-extremal) eternal black holes in terms of thermal and target space properties:

PSM definition of relativistic eternal black holes.

Relativistic eternal black holes are thermal states with finite entropy that have a relativistic extremal surface.

Thermality is needed to exclude extremal black holes, finite entropy
is needed to exclude constant dilaton solutions, and the presence of a
relativistic extremal surface is needed to ensure there is a special
locus in target space that lies on the bifurcate Killing horizon of
the worldsheet geometry. We are ready for the Carroll
generalization of extremal surfaces and black holes.

18.2
Carroll extremal surfaces in PSM formulation

Trying to mimic the relativistic classification of loci is less rich
in the Carroll case, since the target space coordinate
X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}
does not
appear on the right-hand side of the Carroll boost transformation laws
(
359
), which we re-display here for convenience.

\delta_{\lambda}X=\delta_{\lambda}X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}=0\qquad\qquad\delta_{\lambda}X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}=X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,\lambda

(516)

As expected, there is no natural notion of a Carroll horizon (since “everything moves with the speed of light”). However, there still is the notion of an extremal surface, as evident from (
516
), namely when
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}=0
. The three different cases are summarized in the table below. (We label negative
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}
as “normal” since, in most applications,
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}
is negative between the asymptotic region and the extremal locus.)

signs

X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}>0

X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}<0

\boldsymbol{X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}=0}

anti-normal

normal

extremal

Thus, we have a similar definition of Carroll extremal surfaces as in
the relativistic case.
22
22

22

Perhaps also
|X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}|=\infty
has a similar interpretation, but since
such loci typically are not part of the physical Carroll spacetime,
we disregard this possibility here. These loci could appear at
asymptotic boundaries, for instance, separating future and past null
infinity.

PSM definition of Carroll extremal surfaces.

Carroll extremal surfaces are loci in the PSM target space that are fixed points under Carroll boosts.

Note that every line of constant
X
and
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}
(but varying
X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}
) is
a fixed-line under Carroll boosts, so in that sense, every such line
is “marginally (anti-)trapped” and the whole Carroll geometry could
be viewed as a “horizon”. On this “horizon” there can still be an
exceptional point, the Carroll extremal surface, reminiscent of the
bifurcation surface of relativistic black holes.

There is no Carroll analogue of Carter–Penrose diagrams, but we
can still draw diagrams similar to Fig.
5
to highlight
different regions in the Carroll manifold with different signs of

X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}
. Naturally, the diagrams in Fig.
6
are less rich in
structure since there are fewer possibilities in the table above
compared to the Lorentzian case. While we chose to draw the lines at
45
∘
(as a reminder that null hypersurfaces have Carroll
structures), at this stage there is no significance to this angle.
From a limiting perspective, one can understand these diagrams as
emerging from infinite boosts of
t=\mathrm{const.}
hypersurfaces
(“wormholes”) of Lorentzian black hole Carter–Penrose diagrams.

The three diagrams in Fig.
6
represent the same entity and
emphasise different ways of boosting the constant time slice of the
parent Carter–Penrose diagram. For instance, in the right diagram,
the
t=\mathrm{const.}
hypersurface in the parent Carter–Penrose diagram
is boosted all the way to the future event horizon to both sides of
the extremal surface. We shall elaborate on the relation to the
wormhole picture in a higher-dimensional context in section

21
.

18.3
Carroll extremal surfaces in first- and second-order formulations

In the second-order version, the quantity
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}
does not exist, but
through the equations of motion (
363
) it is related on-shell
to the directional derivative of the dilaton field projected onto the
spatial inverse vielbein,

X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\approx-e^{\mu}\,\partial_{\mu}X\,.

(517)

Thus, in the second-order formulation, the criterion for an extremal surface is that the directional derivative of the dilaton field projected onto the spatial inverse vielbein vanishes.

\boxed{\phantom{\Bigg(}\textrm{Carroll\;extremal\;surface:}\qquad e^{\mu}\,\partial_{\mu}X=0\qquad\qquad X>0\phantom{\Bigg)}}

(518)

This definition is analogous to the relativistic one (
515
) and is on-shell Carroll boost invariant, since
\delta_{\lambda}e^{\mu}\,\partial_{\mu}X=-\lambda v^{\mu}\,\partial_{\mu}X\approx X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\lambda\,v^{\mu}e_{\mu}=0
. Note that one could add to (
518
) for free the condition
v^{\mu}\,\partial_{\mu}X=0
since
X
does not depend on
t
on-shell.

18.4
Carroll black holes

Equipped with our definition of extremal surfaces, we define Carroll black holes.

Definition of Carroll black holes.
Carroll black holes are C-thermal states with finite entropy that have a Carroll extremal surface.

In particular, we need the condition of finite entropy to exclude
Carroll constant dilaton solutions, which definitely should not be
referred to as Carroll black holes. In the next section we turn to the evaluation of the tantum gravity partition function for these solutions.

19
Evaluating the tantum gravity partition function

Now that we have explored the classical solution space to the action (
357
) and given physical interpretation to the massive states, we would like to investigate how the tantum gravity partition function for these models can be evaluated
[
112
]
. We shall see that in a saddle-point approximation it indeed reproduces the correct thermodynamics.

The used argument is based on the Euclidean path integral approach to Einstein gravity, as formulated by Gibbons and Hawking in
[
131
]
. Their pivotal insight involves expanding the complete gravitational action
\Gamma[g]
, including relevant boundary terms, around a classical saddle-point
g_{\textrm{\tiny cl}}
. Which boundary terms are relevant is determined by demanding a well-defined variational principle,
\delta\Gamma[g_{\textrm{\tiny cl}};\,\delta g]=0
, as well as a finite on-shell action
\Gamma[g_{\textrm{\tiny cl}}]
. Under these hypotheses, the Euclidean partition function of general relativity

\displaystyle Z_{\textrm{GR}}=\int\mathcal{D}g\,\exp(-\frac{1}{\hbar}\,\Gamma_{\textrm{GR}} [g])\approx\exp(-\frac{1}{\hbar}\,\Gamma_{\textrm{GR}}[g_{\textrm{\tiny cl}}])

(519)

is classically well-approximated by the exponential of the on-shell action provided
\hbar
is sufficiently small. We need to specify boundary conditions before reducing the partition function to its classical approximation. In the simplest case (on which we shall focus below), this amounts to fixing the periodicity of Euclidean time, which in turn can be interpreted as the inverse temperature
[
131
]
together with some fall-offs for the dynamical fields as they approach the boundary.

We shall do the same for the tantum gravity partition function using the example of two-dimensional Carroll dilaton gravity. According to the scaling limit (
320
) we now use
\kappa
as a semi-classical expansion parameter and write

\displaystyle Z_{\textrm{TG}}=\int\mathcal{D}A_{I}\mathcal{D}X^{I}\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{\kappa}\Gamma_{\textrm{TG}}[A_I,X^I]\Big)

(520)

where

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\textrm{TG}}[A_{I},X^{I}]=I_{1^{\mathrm{st}}}+I_{\partial\mathcal{M}}

(521)

is the first order action (
357
) augmented by some boundary term that makes the variational principle well-defined, a notion that will be made explicit below. While it is indeed possible to show that the exponent in (
520
) arises from a limiting procedure
[
112
]
we shall work directly in the limiting theory and show its internal consistency. Notably, the prefactor of the action in (
520
) depends on the product of the two TG coupling constants (
320
) (since
k\sim G_{M}^{-1}
). Thus, even though we formally sent
\hbar\to\infty
in the Bronstein cube, there is still a semi-classical limit corresponding to sufficiently small
\kappa
23
23

23

The semi-classical limit of the action (
409
) exists whenever the product
\kappa G_{M}
is small, which leads to two possible limits, either
\kappa\to 0
while keeping
G_{M}\sim k^{-1}
finite, or
G_{M}\to 0
while keeping
\kappa
finite. However, the latter possibility has to be discarded since it is at odds with our hypotheses to keep the Schwarzschild radius and the energy finite.
. In other words, as long as
\kappa
is sufficiently small as compared to the action, we can expect a well-defined saddle-point approximation to the Euclidean path integral. While in the other sections we set
\kappa=1
we shall keep it here for the sake of explicitness.

The choice of boundary conditions is usually motivated by the physical situation one wants to study. In the present case, we would like to choose them such that the solutions found in section
16.2
are allowed but not much more than that. Also, as often done for dilaton gravity models
[
139
]
, we choose the boundary
\partial\mathcal{M}
to be a dilaton isosurface at some large
X=X_{c}>>1
. It is therefore practical to take the dilaton itself as a radial coordinate. The solutions from section
16.2
for generic
U(X)
and
V(X)
then read

\begin{gathered}X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}=-\sqrt{2e^{-Q}(w-M)}\\
v=e^{-Q}\,\frac{1}{X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}}\,\partial_{t}\end{gathered}\qquad\qquad\begin{gathered}X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}=0\\
\tau=-X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,e^{Q}\,\differential t\end{gathered}\qquad\qquad\begin{gathered}\omega=e^{Q}\,\mathcal{V}\,\differential t\\
e=-\frac{1}{X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}}\,\differential X\end{gathered}

(522)

where
M
is again a real constant parameter and the functions
Q(X)
and
w(X)
are defined by (
460
). The fall-off conditions are chosen such that the following variations are allowed

\begin{gathered}\delta X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}=-\frac{\delta M}{X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}e^{Q}}\\
\delta X=0\end{gathered}\qquad\qquad\begin{gathered}\delta X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}=0\\
\delta\tau=\frac{\delta M}{X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}}\,\differential t\end{gathered}\qquad\qquad\begin{gathered}\delta\omega=U\,\delta M\,\differential t\\
\delta e=-\delta M\,\frac{\differential X}{X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}^{3}e^{Q}}\end{gathered}

(523)

where all the components have to be thought of as functions of
X
. Following the discussion in subsection
17.4
, we assume that the coordinate
t
has dimensions of length and is periodically identified
t\sim t+\beta\kappa
where

\displaystyle\beta\kappa:=\lim_{X_{c}\to\infty}\oint\tau\Big|_{X=X_{c}}

(524)

is the proper length of the temporal cycle at infinity and
\beta
is the inverse temperature measured in units of inverse energy (
k_{B}=1
). Keeping
\beta
fixed completes our set of boundary conditions and defines a canonical ensemble.

Since the dominant saddle-points typically correspond to smooth classical solutions
[
138
]
, we can use the tools of subsection
16.5
to single out a unique
M
for a given
\beta
by the implicit relation

\beta^{-1}=T=\frac{\kappa}{2\pi}w^{\prime}(X_{\textrm{\tiny ext.}}(M))~,

(525)

where
X_{\textrm{\tiny ext.}}(M)
is the solution to the equation
w(X)=M
. To determine the boundary term in the action
\Gamma_{\textrm{TG}}
we need to make sure that the present class of solutions indeed extremizes the action on the given space of allowed field configurations and that the on-shell value of the action is finite, i.e.,

\displaystyle\delta\Gamma_{\textrm{TG}}\,\big|_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{on-shell}}=0

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\textrm{TG}}\,\big|_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{on-shell}}<\infty~.

(526)

The way these conditions are evaluated in practice is to keep the cutoff
X_{c}
finite at first and only send
X_{c}
to infinity at the very end. We start by an ansatz for the boundary action

\displaystyle I_{\partial\mathcal{M}}=\frac{k}{2\pi}\int_{\partial\mathcal{M}}\Big(\alpha_{1}\,X\,\omega+\alpha_{2}\,X_{H}\,\tau+\alpha_{3}\,\tau\,\sqrt{2\,w\,e^{-Q}}\Big)

\displaystyle\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{R}

(527)

with three undetermined constants
\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
,
i=1,2,3
. Using that a large class
24
24

24

This class includes all Schwarzschild–Tangherlini–Reissner–Nordström–AdS black holes as well as Witten and JT black holes and deformations thereof. See
[
139
]
for details.
of dilaton gravity models obeys

\lim\limits_{X\to\infty}w(X)=\infty

(528)

we can write the variation of the full action on-shell as a boundary term

\displaystyle\delta\Gamma_{\textrm{\tiny TG}}\Big|_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{on-shell}}=\frac{k}{2\pi}\int_{\partial\mathcal{M}}\!\!\!\differential t\,\Big((\alpha_{1}+1)XU+2\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{3}+1+\mathcal{O}(X_{c}^{-1})\Big)\,\delta M\overset{!}{=}0

(529)

which fixes the value of the first coefficient,
\alpha_{1}=-1
, and further gives the algebraic constraint
2\alpha_{2}=\alpha_{3}-1
. The on-shell action then simplifies to

\begin{split}\Gamma_{\textrm{\tiny TG}}&\Big|_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{on-shell}}=\\
&\frac{k}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\beta\kappa}\!\!\!\differential t\,\int_{X_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{ext.}}}^{X_{c}}\!\!\!\differential X\,\partial_{X}\Big(X\partial_{X}w(X)-XU(X)(w-M)-2w(X)\Big)\\
&\quad+\frac{k}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\beta\kappa}\!\!\!\differential t\,\Big(-X\partial_{X}w+XU(w-M)+(\alpha_{3}+1)w-M+\mathcal{O}(X_{c}^{-1})\Big)\\
&=\frac{\beta\kappa k}{2\pi}\Big(2M-\frac{2\pi T}{\kappa}X_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{ext.}}\Big)+\frac{k}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\beta\kappa}\!\!\!\differential t\,\Big((\alpha_{3}-1)w-M+\mathcal{O}(X_{c}^{-1})\Big)\end{split}

(530)

where we used (
525
) in the last equality. One can see that the on-shell action is finite in the limit
X_{c}\to\infty
if we set
\alpha_{3}=1
. In summary, we have

\alpha_{1}=-1\qquad\qquad\alpha_{2}=0\qquad\qquad\alpha_{3}=1\,.

(531)

The holographically renormalized first-order action therefore reads

\boxed{\begin{aligned} \Gamma_{\textrm{\tiny TG}}\,[A_{I},X^{I}]=\frac{k}{2\pi}\int_{\mathcal{M}}\Big(X\,\differential\omega&+X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,(\differential\tau+\omega\wedge e)+X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}\,\differential e+\mathcal{V}\,\tau\wedge e\Big)\\
&-\frac{k}{2\pi}\int_{\partial\mathcal{M}}\Big(X\,\omega-\tau\,\sqrt{2e^{-Q}w}\Big)\end{aligned}}

(532)

and in the limit
X_{c}\to\infty
evaluates on-shell to

\Gamma_{\textrm{\tiny TG}}\Big|_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{on-shell}}=\frac{\beta k\kappa}{2\pi}\Big(M-\frac{2\pi T}{\kappa}X_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{ext.}}\Big)\,.

(533)

The free energy of the solutions (
522
) is then

\displaystyle F=\frac{1}{\kappa\beta}\Gamma_{\textrm{\tiny TG}}\Big|_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{on-shell}}=\frac{k}{2\pi}\Big(M-\frac{2\pi T}{\kappa}X_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{ext.}}\Big)=F(T)

(534)

and upon using the standard thermodynamic relations

\displaystyle S=-\frac{\partial F}{\partial T}

\displaystyle E=F+TS

(535)

one precisely recovers the thermodynamic quantities of section
17
(with
\kappa
reinstalled),

E=\frac{k}{2\pi}M\qquad\qquad S=\frac{k}{\kappa}X_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{ext.}}~.

(536)

This makes it evident that these relations hold for generic dilaton gravity models and shows the consistency of the partition function with the first law.

The analysis in this section was done for the two-dimensional setting but to some extent these results also apply to the spherically symmetric sector of higher-dimensional magnetic Carroll gravity. After all, we saw in subsection
15.4
that the latter admits a classically equivalent formulation in terms of a two-dimensional Carroll dilaton gravity theory. However, since in the Lorentzian setting dimensional reduction and quantization do not commute
[
128
]
(“dimensional reduction anomaly”) we in general have to expect a similar behaviour in a Carroll setting. Therefore, one should think of the spherically reduced theory as a separate quantum gravity model, the classical limit of which coincides with the classical limit of spherically symmetric magnetic Carroll gravity. In the saddle-point approximation, on the other hand, the dimensional reduction anomaly plays no role since the latter is a 1-loop effect and a consequence of the UV behavior of the theory when coupled to matter.

Our conclusions of this section are in line with previous results in
the literature: taking the standard Carroll limit in thermal partition functions leads to infinities which are ultimately related to a degeneracy in the energy spectrum
[
122
,
96
,
93
]
. For instance, for the theory of a free electric scalar field (
269
) in a finite volume a naïve evaluation of an expression like
\text{Tr}(e^{-\beta H})
involves a sum over momenta. But, since the Hamiltonian does not contain any spatial gradients, any choice of momentum leads to the same energy making the sum diverge. This is in contrast to the Euclidean case where the contributions of large momenta are exponentially damped.
Proposals to circumvent this problem are introducing an imaginary chemical potential
[
200
]
to regulate this sum or keeping a UV-regulator in the form of a finite lattice spacing
[
93
]
. However, in all of these treatments
\hbar
was left untouched. Following the discussion in section
13
, our notion of thermodynamics for Carroll black holes precisely uses this handle of scaling
\hbar
to obtain a sensible notion of thermodynamics for Carroll theories.

We now turn to several examples of Carroll black hole geometries and investigate their thermodynamical properties.

constant dilaton vacua and focus exclusively on the linear dilaton
sector. Here, we work again in units where
\kappa=1
.

20.1
Carroll JT model

The Jackiw–Teitelboim (JT) model
[
170
,
220
]
was the first 2d model of gravity. It is
particularly elegant, as it allows a reformulation as non-abelian BF
theory
[
168
,
83
]
, in contrast to nearly
all other 2d dilaton gravity models. All its solutions are locally
(A)dS
2
so that JT gravity is tailor-made for a holographic
description
[
215
,
184
,
69
,
149
,
12
,
153
,
82
]
. Especially the SYK/JT correspondence

[
175
,
208
,
209
,
185
]

has reinvigorated the interest in JT gravity and its holographic
description. Due to its simple BF formulation (see also subsection
15.1
), the JT model was the
starting point for Carroll limits of 2d dilaton gravity

[
136
]
. Here, we summarize the key results for the
Carroll JT (CJT) model, and in subsection
20.2
, we discuss
boundary conditions for CJT.

The CJT model is given by the Lagrangian (
358
) with the potentials

U_{\textrm{\tiny CJT}}(X)=0\qquad\quad V_{\textrm{\tiny CJT}}(X)=\frac{1}{\ell^{2}}\,X\,.

(537)

The function
w
defined in (
448
) for CJT is given by

w_{\textrm{\tiny CJT}}(X)=\frac{X^{2}}{2\ell^{2}}\,.

(538)

Applying our general analysis of section
17
to the choice (
537
) yields the linear dilaton solutions (we fix the integration constant coming from integrating (
451
) without loss of generality by a shift of the origin of the spatial coordinate
r
, and we take the positive branch of the square-root function)

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,e^{r/\ell}+M\ell^{2}\,e^{-r/\ell}

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle=\frac{X}{\ell^{2}}\,\differential t

(539a)

\displaystyle X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\ell}\,e^{r/\ell}+M\ell\,e^{-r/\ell}

\displaystyle\tau

\displaystyle=-X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,\differential t

(539b)

\displaystyle X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle e

\displaystyle=\differential r\,.

(539c)

Translating the 1-forms into second-order notation, the solution above reads

\differential s^{2}=\differential r^{2}\qquad\qquad v=\frac{2\ell\,e^{-r/\ell}}{2M\ell^{2}\,e^{-2r/\ell}-1}\,\partial_{t}\qquad\qquad X=\frac{1}{2}\,e^{r/\ell}+M\ell^{2}\,e^{-r/\ell}\,.

(540)

​​​​​​
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}=0

\quad M<0

Figure 7:
Target space picture of Carroll JT by plotting the level sets of (
461
), restricted to the region
X\geq 0
. Extremal points are red circles and exist only for
M>0
. The solutions given in (

) cover the lower half of this diagram.

In Fig.
7
we depict a constant
X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}
slice of the PSM target space associated with the CJT model.
25
25

25

For positive mass, at the Carroll extremal surface
X=\ell\sqrt{2M}
the solution can be joined to one where
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\to-X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}
and hence
v\to-v
.
The spectrum of CJT falls into three classes, depending on the sign of the mass parameter
M
:

•

M<0
: no Carroll black hole, since
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}<0
everywhere, reminiscent of the global AdS
2
solution of JT

•

M=0
: limiting case, where
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\to 0
as
r\to-\infty
, reminiscent of the Poincaré horizon of the massless JT solution

•

M>0
: Carroll black holes, since
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}=0
has the solution
X=\ell\sqrt{2M}
or, equivalently,
r=\frac{\ell}{2}\,\ln(2M\ell^2)
, reminiscent of black hole solutions of JT

We focus on the positive mass sector since it features Carroll extremal surfaces. Furthermore, to have positive entropy we restrict to the branches with
X>0
.

Energy, entropy, temperature, and specific heat of these solutions are given in Table
2
, and the first law is satisfied, as shown in section
17.3
. Expressing the entropy as a function of the energy shows a relation similar to the Cardy formula for a chiral half of a 2d conformal field theory,

\displaystyle S=\frac{\pi^{2}c\,T}{3}=2\pi\sqrt{\frac{c\,E}{6}}

(541)

provided the central charge is chosen as

\displaystyle c=\frac{6k\ell^{2}}{\pi}~.

(542)

This is again reminiscent of the relativistic case
[
137
]
.

20.2
Example of boundary conditions for CJT

One can interpret (
540
) as radial Gaussian coordinates and provide a “Fefferman–Graham” expansion for the vector field and the dilaton

v=2\ell e^{-r/\ell}\,\big(-1+{\mathcal{O}}(e^{-2r/\ell})\big)\,\partial_{t}\qquad\qquad X=\frac{1}{2}\,e^{r/\ell}\,\big(1+{\mathcal{O}}(e^{-2r/\ell})\big)

(543)

where the leading terms are fixed, and the subleading terms contain state-dependent information. Similarly to JT gravity, there are numerous inequivalent choices for boundary conditions
[
137
]
. It is not our intention to exhaustively discuss the possibilities for CJT gravity. Instead, we provide just one example for boundary conditions and leave a more comprehensive study for future work.

The Brown–Henneaux-like boundary conditions

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,e^{r/\ell}+M(t)\,\ell^{2}e^{-r/\ell}

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\ell^{2}}\,e^{r/\ell}\,\differential t+{\mathcal{O}}(e^{-r/\ell})

(544a)

\displaystyle X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\ell}\,e^{r/\ell}+M(t)\,\ell\,e^{-r/\ell}

\displaystyle\tau

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\ell}\,e^{r/\ell}\,\differential t+{\mathcal{O}}(e^{-r/\ell})

(544b)

\displaystyle X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle e

\displaystyle=\differential r

(544c)

with
\delta M\neq 0
are preserved by the gauge transformations (
359
)-(
361
) with gauge parameters
\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny P}}=0
and

\lambda=e^{r/\ell}\,\frac{\eta}{\ell}+2\ell M(t)\eta\,e^{-r/\ell}\qquad\qquad\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny H}}=e^{r/\ell}\,\eta-2\ell^{2}M(t)\eta\,e^{-r/\ell}

(545)

where
\eta
is the transformation parameter. The equations of motion (
363
) are solved by the field configuration (
544
), up to subleading terms (which can be determined in closed form, if desired). On-shell the mass function
M(t)
is given by the Casimir
M=\frac{X^{2}}{2\ell^{2}}-\frac{X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}^{2}}{2}
.

The variation of the boundary charges

\delta\mathcal{Q}[\lambda_{I}]=\frac{k}{2\pi}\,\big(\lambda\,\delta X+\lambda_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,\delta X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}+\lambda_{{\textrm{\tiny P}}}\,\delta X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}\big)

(546)

in the present case can be integrated (in field space) to a single boundary charge

\mathcal{Q}[\eta]=\frac{k\ell}{\pi}\,\eta\,M(t)

(547)

which is finite as the radial coordinate approaches the asymptotic boundary,
r\to\infty
. On-shell it is also conserved,
\partial_{t}\mathcal{Q}[\eta]\approx 0
. We assumed here a slicing of the phase space where
\eta
is state-independent (see, e.g.,
[
2
,
142
]
for a discussion of different phase space slicings in Lorentzian 2d dilaton gravity).

The asymptotic symmetry algebra trivially is abelian in the present case since we have only one boundary charge, namely the Casimir
M
.

\{\mathcal{Q}[\eta_{1}],\,\mathcal{Q}[\eta_{2}]\}\approx\delta_{\eta_{2}}\mathcal{Q}[\eta_{1}]\approx\frac{k\ell}{\pi}\,\eta_{1}\,\delta_{\eta_{2}}M=0

(548)

20.3
Carroll–Schwarzschild black hole, 2d perspective

As reviewed in section
15.2.1
, spherical reduction of
Einstein gravity leads to a specific 2d dilaton gravity model, the
solutions of which reproduce the Schwarzschild black hole. There is an
expansive history of spherically reduced gravity
[
57
,
224
,
56
,
221
]
that predates the
developments of 2d dilaton gravity. Here, we consider the Carroll
limit of the Schwarzschild black hole from a 2d perspective. See section
21
for a 4d perspective.

The spherically reduced Carroll–Schwarzschild (CS) model is given by the potentials
(
399
), which for
d=3
are

\displaystyle U_{\text{\tiny CS}}(X)=-\frac{1}{2X}

\displaystyle V_{\text{\tiny CS}}(X)=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{4}~.

(549)

The functions
w_{\text{\tiny CS}}
and
e^{Q_{\text{\tiny CS}}}
are

\displaystyle e^{Q_{\text{\tiny CS}}}=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{X}}

\displaystyle w_{\text{\tiny CS}}(X)=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{4}\sqrt{X}

(550)

where we chose the integration constant of the second
integral in (
460
) accordingly. This model is described by a target space diagram
given in Fig.
8
. The solutions never take negative values of
the dilaton. Moreover, the black hole sector of the model is given by

M>0
as all other solutions do not lead to states with finite entropy

S\sim X_{\text{\tiny ext.}}
.

​​​​​​
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}=0

(554)

where

)]. The Carroll second-order variables read

where the vector field is normalized asymptotically,
\lim_{X\to\infty}v=-\partial_{t}
. For simplicity, in these solutions, the ambiguity in the torsion-free spin connection was fixed to
C=0
. To bring this into a more familiar form, we can define the radial coordinate

\displaystyle\mathsf{r}^{2}=X

(556)

which together with the Schwarzschild mass
m=\frac{M}{2}
leads to

v=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\frac{2m}{\mathsf{r}}}}\partial_{t}\qquad\qquad h=\frac{\differential\mathsf{r}^{2}}{1-\frac{2m}{\mathsf{r}}}~.

(557)

The Carroll thermodynamic quantities for the Carroll–Schwarzschild black hole

\displaystyle E=\frac{k}{\pi}\,m

\displaystyle T=\frac{1}{8\pi m}

\displaystyle S=4km^{2}

(558)

).
