20.4
Carroll CGHS

The Callan–Giddings–Harvey–Strominger (CGHS) model

[
74
]
is a 2d toy model for black hole evaporation. It
consists of a Lorentzian 2d dilaton gravity action with potentials

U=0
,
V=\Lambda=\textrm{const.}
plus some minimally coupled scalar
fields as carriers of the Hawking quanta. In the present discussion, we always
neglect interactions with matter, so when we refer to the CGHS model
or its Carroll counterpart, we solely mean the geometric part of the
model without matter. Besides the JT model, the CGHS model is arguably
the simplest 2d dilaton gravity model. A more precise version of this
statement is that only the JT and the CGHS model permit a
reinterpretation of the corresponding PSM as non-abelian BF theory.
This is the main reason why the CGHS model was the first one to
receive a holographic interpretation
[
9
]
after the
JT model.

The Carroll limit of the CGHS model (CCGHS) has the same potentials

U_{\text{\tiny CCGHS}}=0\qquad\qquad V_{\text{\tiny CCGHS}}=\Lambda=\text{const.}>0\,.

(560)

The solutions of the linear dilaton sector are

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle=\frac{\Lambda}{2}r^{2}+\frac{M}{\Lambda}

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle=\Lambda\differential t

(561)

\displaystyle X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}

\displaystyle=-\Lambda r

\displaystyle\tau

\displaystyle=-X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\differential t

(562)

\displaystyle X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle e

\displaystyle=\differential r

(563)

leading to a flat Carroll spacetime, i.e.,
R=0
. Here, the radial
coordinate was fixed such that
r=0
corresponds to
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}=0
.
Investigating the spectrum of this model shows that Carroll extremal
surfaces exist only for positive values of
M
(see Fig.
9
).

​​​​​​
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}=0
X
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}
M>0
M=0
M<0

Figure 9:
Target space picture of Carroll CGHS with
X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}=0
, restricted to the region
X\geq 0
. Extremal points are red circles and exist only for
M>0
. The lower half is described by the solutions (
561
)-(
563
).

The various Carroll thermodynamic quantities of these solutions are given in Table
2
. As the temperature is fixed to a single specific value in terms of the model-dependent constant
\Lambda
, the specific heat diverges.

20.5
Carroll Witten black hole

The Witten black hole
[
186
,
115
,
235
]
emerges from 2d string theory. From the worldsheet
perspective, it is described by an SL
(2,\mathbb{R})/U(1)
gauged
WZW-model. Interpreting the vanishing of the
\beta
-functions of this
conformal field theory as target space equations of motion yields as
target space action a Lorentzian 2d dilaton gravity model with
potentials
U=-\frac{1}{X}
and
V=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\,X
, where

\lambda^{2}\propto 1/\alpha^{\prime}
with the inverse string tension

\alpha^{\prime}
. As common in the literature, we use the phrase
“Witten black hole” as a label for the conformal field theory, the target space
theory, and the positive mass spectrum of solutions to the latter.
The Euclidean continuation of the Witten black hole is the famous
cigar geometry,
\differential s^{2}=\differential r^{2}+\tanh^{2}r\,\differential\tau^{2}
. For more
details on the Witten black hole, see section 2.1.2 in

[
134
]
and Refs. therein.

The Carroll limit of the Witten black hole features the same potentials

U_{\text{\tiny CWBH}}=-\frac{1}{X}\qquad\qquad V_{\text{\tiny CWBH}}=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\,X>0

(564)

and is referred to as Carroll Witten black hole. Analogously to its
Lorentzian avatar (see e.g.
[
116
]
), it emerges as

D\to\infty
limit of the CST black hole and is conformally related to
the CCGHS model by a dilaton-dependent Weyl rescaling.

In the linear dilaton sector, the CWBH solutions

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle=\frac{2M}{\lambda^{2}}\,\cosh^{2}\frac{\lambda r}{2}

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle=\Big(\lambda^{2}-\frac{M}{X}\Big)\,\differential t

(565)

\displaystyle X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{\lambda^{2}X^{2}-2MX}

\displaystyle\tau

\displaystyle=-\frac{X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}}{X}\differential t

(566)

\displaystyle X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle e

\displaystyle=\differential r

(567)

lead to the same thermodynamical behaviour as the CCGHS model. In fact,
all thermodynamic formulas are equivalent for CCGHS and CWBH upon
replacing
\Lambda\to\tfrac{\lambda^{2}}{2}
.

21
Carroll–Schwarzschild black hole, 4d perspective

In this section, we elaborate on the 4d perspective of the Carroll–-Schwarzschild black hole and the associated wormhole picture. While the following discussion easily generalizes to higher dimensions (section
20
), we focus, for clarity, on
3+1
dimensions.

The Schwarzschild line element is given by

\differential s^{2}=-\left(1-\frac{\mathsf{r}_{s}}{\mathsf{r}}\right)c^{2}\differential t^{2}+\frac{\differential\mathsf{r}^{2}}{1-\frac{\mathsf{r}_{s}}{\mathsf{r}}}+\mathsf{r}^{2}\differential\Omega^{2}_{S^{2}}

(568)

where
\mathsf{r}_{s}=\frac{2mG_{N}}{c^{2}}
and
\differential\Omega^{2}_{S^{2}}
is the metric on the round 2-sphere.

In the tantum gravity limit, where
\mathsf{r}_{s}
stays finite by construction we arrive at
[
197
,
151
]

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\frac{\mathsf{r}_{s}}{\mathsf{r}}}}\,\partial_{t}

\displaystyle h

\displaystyle=\frac{\differential\mathsf{r}^{2}}{1-\frac{\mathsf{r}_{s}}{\mathsf{r}}}+\mathsf{r}^{2}\,\differential\Omega^{2}_{S^{2}}

(569)

This is the lifted version of (
557
) and forms a solution of magnetic Carroll gravity
[
146
,
197
]
. The
extension with a non-vanishing cosmological constant was described
in
[
198
]
.

It is instructive to rewrite this configuration in terms of isotropic coordinates
obtained by the (double cover) coordinate transformation
\mathsf{r}\mapsto\rho=\rho(\mathsf{r})

given by

\mathsf{r}=\frac{\mathsf{r}_{s}}{4}\,\Big(\rho+\frac{1}{\rho}+2\Big)

(570)

resulting in the Carroll wormhole geometry

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=-\frac{\rho+1}{\rho-1}\,\partial_{t}

\displaystyle h

\displaystyle=\mathsf{r}_{s}^{2}\left(\frac{(\rho+1)^{2}}{4\rho^{2}}\right)^{2}\left({\differential}\rho^{2}+\rho^{2}\,\differential\Omega^{2}_{S^{2}}\right)\,.

(571)

The spatial Carroll metric
h
is
\rho\rightarrow 1/\rho
symmetric, and
v
changes sign under this map, corresponding to the well-known fact that the Killing time runs opposite in the universe on the other side of the wormhole (see Fig.
10
).

Let us scan for Carroll extremal surfaces, cf. section
18
.
By definition, they satisfy
e^{M}_{a}\partial_{M}X=0
, which is the
condition that these surfaces are invariant under any linear
deviations, regardless of the direction. The dilaton
X
is the surface
area of the
2
-spheres that foliate our spherically symmetric
spacetime. For (
569
) it is given by
X=\mathsf{r}^{2}
[with
\lambda=2
in
(
396
)]. This means that, using the conventions of
section
15.4
, only the radial part of the
inverse vielbein
e^{M}_{1}\partial_{M}=:e^{M}\partial_{M}
leads to a nontrivial
condition

\displaystyle e^{M}\partial_{M}X=\left(1-\frac{\mathsf{r}_{s}}{\mathsf{r}}\right)\partial_{\mathsf{r}}X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}0\,.

(572)

The Carroll extremal surface is at
\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{r}_{s}
or, equivalently, at

\rho=1
(see Fig.
10
).

\rho=1
\rho<1
\rho>1

Figure 10:
Sketch of spatial Carroll wormhole
geometry (
571
). It corresponds to the spatial
wormhole geometry of the maximally extended Schwarzschild black hole
that cuts through the bifurcation sphere. In red, where
\rho=1

(
\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{r}_{s}
), we have encircled the Carroll extremal surface at the wormhole’s throat.

From the 4d perspective, it is natural to assign the dilaton the length dimension
[X]=2
, which implies
[\mathsf{r}]=1
and
[k]=-2
(cf. the discussion in section
17.4
). The relativistic entropy, temperature, and energy of the Schwarzschild black hole are given by

S_{\textrm{\tiny rel}}=\frac{\pi c^{3}\,\mathsf{r}^{2}_{s}}{\hbar G_{N}}\qquad\qquad T_{\textrm{\tiny rel}}=\frac{\hbar c}{4\pi\,\mathsf{r}_{s}}\qquad\qquad E_{\textrm{\tiny rel}}=\frac{c^{4}}{2G_{N}}\,\mathsf{r}_{s}

(573)

where we restored all conversion factors except for Boltzmann’s constant, which we fix to one. The tantum gravity limit (
320
) leads to the leading-order Carroll quantities

S=\frac{\pi\,\mathsf{r}^{2}_{s}}{G_{M}}\qquad\qquad T=\frac{1}{4\pi\,\mathsf{r}_{s}}\qquad\qquad E=\frac{1}{2G_{M}}\,\mathsf{r}_{s}\,.

(574)

where we set
\kappa=\hbar c=1
again. The results (
574
) coincide with the general results in 2d derived in section
17
, using the 2d-4d dictionary (note that our choices imply
e^{Q}=1/(2\sqrt{X})
)

k=\frac{\pi}{G_{M}}\qquad\qquad X=\mathsf{r}^{2}\qquad\qquad X_{\textrm{\tiny min}}=\mathsf{r}_{s}^{2}\qquad\qquad w(X)=\sqrt{X}\,.

(575)

These dimensions work as required since in units where
\kappa=1
both
G_{M}
and
X
have dimensions of square metres. So entropy is dimensionless, while temperature and energy are measured in Joule.

We show next that the expressions (
574
) can be computed using 4d geometric arguments that are similar to what one does in general relativity. As shown above, the Carroll entropy is proportional to the area of the wormhole’s throat. At this locus we have
h|_{\rho=1}=\mathsf{r}_{s}^{2}\differential\Omega^{2}_{S^{2}}
. As discussed above and in section
17
, we ensured that
S
is dimensionless.

We next turn to temperature. The 4d Carroll boost and rotation connections
\omega^{a}
and
\omega^{ab}=-\omega^{ba}
are solutions to the Cartan zero torsion-equations (cf. sections
7
and
14.2
)

\differential\tau+\omega^{a}\wedge e^{a}=0=\differential e^{a}+\omega^{ab}\wedge e^{b}\,.

(576)

For the Carroll wormhole solution (
569
), we choose the vielbeins

\tau=f(\mathsf{r})\,\differential t\qquad\qquad e^{1}=f^{-1}(\mathsf{r})\,\differential\mathsf{r}\qquad\qquad e^{l}=\mathsf{r}\,\bar{e}^{l}

(577)

where
l=2,3
correspond to the 2-sphere tangent space directions,
\bar{e}^{l}
are round unit 2-sphere vielbeins, and we defined
f(\mathsf{r})=(1-\mathsf{r}_{s}/\mathsf{r})^{1/2}
. The most general solution to these equations is

\omega^{1}=f^{\prime}\tau+C^{11}\,e^{1}+C^{1l}e^{l}\qquad\qquad\omega^{l}=C^{l1}e^{1}+C^{lm}e^{m}\qquad\qquad\omega^{1l}=f\bar{e}^{l}

(578)

and
\omega^{lm}=\bar{\omega}^{lm}
is the connection on the unit 2-sphere.

We next consider the pullback of (
576
) onto the 2d submanifold obtained by fixing
a point on the 2-sphere. In order to avoid clutter, we denote the
pullbacks of the vielbeins by the same symbols. If the 2-sphere has
coordinates
\theta,\phi
we are considering the manifold

\theta=\theta_{0}
and
\phi=\phi_{0}
where
\theta_{0}
and
\phi_{0}
are
constants. The equations (
576
) on this submanifold become

\differential\tau+\omega^{1}\wedge e^{1}=\differential e^{1}=0

(579)

with
\tau=f(\mathsf{r})\differential t
,
e^{1}=f^{-1}\differential\mathsf{r}
and
\omega^{1}=f^{\prime}\tau+C^{11}\,e^{1}
.

To define the Carroll temperature
T
, we first Wick rotate by the prescription

t=it_{\textrm{\tiny W}}\qquad\qquad\tau=i\tau_{\textrm{\tiny W}}\qquad\qquad\omega^{1}=i\omega^{1}_{\textrm{\tiny W}}\qquad\qquad v=iv_{\textrm{\tiny W}}

(580)

where it is understood that the conversion factor has already been
absorbed such that the length dimensions are

[\tau_{\textrm{\tiny W}}]=[t_{\textrm{\tiny W}}]=1
and

[\omega_{\textrm{\tiny W}}]=0
(again, see section

17.4
). The Wick rotation does not change the signature
of the geometry and the holonomy of the manifold (unlike in general
relativity) but it allows us to consider (
579
)
for a periodic
t_{\textrm{\tiny W}}
. Now,
t_{\textrm{\tiny W}}
has
the dimension of a length such that it can be compactified with period

1/T
. In the Wick rotated setting,
(
579
) becomes

\differential\tau_{\textrm{\tiny W}}+\omega^{1}_{\textrm{\tiny W}}\wedge e^{1}=0
,
where
\tau_{\textrm{\tiny W}}=f(\mathsf{r})\differential t_{\textrm{\tiny W}}
has
dimensions of length and

\omega^{1}_{\textrm{\tiny W}}=f^{\prime}\tau_{\textrm{\tiny W}}+C^{11}e^{1}
is dimensionless. The 2d geometry described by

t_{\textrm{\tiny W}}\sim t_{\textrm{\tiny W}}+1/T
and

\mathsf{r}_{s}<\mathsf{r}<\mathsf{r}_{c}
where
\mathsf{r}_{c}
is some arbitrary number is a
cigar-like geometry that we will denote by
\Sigma
. The boundary of

\Sigma
is given by the circle at
\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{r}_{c}
. The Carroll boost
connection
\omega^{1}_{\textrm{\tiny W}}
contains the undetermined
function
C^{11}
. We assume that
C^{11}
is globally
well-defined on the cigar geometry so that it is periodic in

t_{\textrm{\tiny W}}
. Then, a direct calculation tells us

\int_{\Sigma}\differential\omega^{1}_{\textrm{\tiny W}}-\int_{\partial\Sigma}\omega_{\textrm{\tiny W}}^{1}=\frac{1}{2\mathsf{r}_{s}}\frac{1}{T}

(581)

where
\partial\Sigma
is the circle at
\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{r}_{c}
. The bulk
orientation is chosen such that

\differential t_{\textrm{\tiny W}}\wedge\differential r=:\differential t_{\textrm{\tiny W}}\differential r

and the boundary orientation is induced similarly to section

16.5
. The left-hand side is
2\pi
times the Euler
character
\chi
of
\Sigma
which is topologically a disk so that

\chi=1
. This procedure recovers the result for temperature announced in (
574
).

In
[
197
]
the energy
E
of the Carroll solution
discussed here was computed, where the author additionally set
16\pi G_{M}=1
. Restoring this constant the result is the same as for the
Schwarzschild black hole, namely

E=\frac{\mathsf{r}_{s}}{2G_{M}}=2TS

(582)

in agreement with (
574
). Of course, the first law

\delta E=T\,\delta S

(583)

is obeyed.

All the thermodynamical relations above follow immediately from the general analysis of section
17
, using the 2d-4d dictionary (
575
).

22
Charged and rotating Carroll black holes

All examples so far can be understood entirely in terms of 2d Carroll
dilaton gravity or one of its dimensional uplifts to higher
dimensions. In this section, we go beyond this case by considering
charged or rotating Carroll black holes (mostly from a dimensionally
reduced, 2d, perspective), where a generalization to 2d
Carroll–Maxwell dilaton gravity is required.

22.1
General remarks on charged Carroll black holes in 2d

In the PSM formulation, adding a Maxwell field amounts to adding
another target space coordinate
Y
and adding to the Poisson tensor
an extra row and column of zero entries. The potential in
(
358
) can depend on this additional target space coordinate
as well, and the Lagrange 2-form acquires an additional term

Y\,\differential A
, where
A=A_{\mu}\,\differential x^{\mu}
is the Maxwell gauge field
1-form.

{\mathcal{L}}=Y\,\differential A+X\,\differential\omega+X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,\big(\differential\tau+\omega\wedge e\big)+X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}\,\differential e+{\mathcal{V}}(X,\,X_{\textrm{\tiny H}},\,Y)\,\tau\wedge e

(584)

The additional

U(1)
gauge symmetry generated by some transformation parameter

\Lambda
acts trivially on all fields except on the Maxwell gauge
field,
\delta_{\Lambda}A=\differential\Lambda
. The equations of motion
(
363
) are essentially unchanged, with the replacement

{\mathcal{V}}(X,\,X_{\textrm{\tiny H}})\to{\mathcal{V}}(X,\,X_{\textrm{\tiny H}},\,Y)
. There are two additional
equations of motion from varying with respect to
Y
and
A
:

\displaystyle\delta Y:

\displaystyle\differential A

\displaystyle=-\frac{\partial{\mathcal{V}}(X,\,X_{\textrm{\tiny H}},\,Y)}{\partial Y}\,\tau\wedge e

(585)

\displaystyle\delta A:

\displaystyle\differential Y

\displaystyle=0

(586)

The quantity
Y
on-shell is a second conserved Casimir,

Y=q=\textrm{const.}

(587)

and physically corresponds to a conserved
U(1)
charge (though in
some applications, it might have a different interpretation, e.g., as
angular momentum in higher dimensions).

If the Lagrange 2-form (
584
) emerges from some
Carroll limit, it could happen that the first and last terms are
multiplied by some powers of the speed of light
c
. In that case, we
can always first rescale
Y
with an appropriate factor of
c
to
eliminate
c
from the last term and then rescale
A
with an
appropriate factor of
c
to eliminate
c
from the first term. Thus,
without loss of generality, we assume there are no explicit
factors of
c
in the Lagrange 2-form (
584
).

Solving the equations of motion can be done exactly as in section

16
. The solutions for the spatial metric and the temporal
vector field will, in general, depend not only on the mass parameter
M

but also on the
U(1)
charge
q
. As a consequence of this
dependence, there can be BPS-like bounds and extremality conditions.
(Since we already use the word “extremal” to denote Carroll extremal
surfaces, we call the confluent case “degenerate” instead.)
Relatedly, new constant dilaton vacua can emerge and typically have
an interpretation as “near horizon extremal geometries”. (To clarify
also here the vocabulary, we refer to such geometries as
“near-Carroll-extremal-surface degenerate geometries” in a
Carroll context.) Other than these marginal changes, our general
discussion of section
16
applies.

A prototypical form of the potential is given by

{\mathcal{V}}(X,\,X_{\textrm{\tiny H}},\,Y)=\hat{{\mathcal{V}}}(X,\,X_{\textrm{\tiny H}})-\frac{Y^{2}}{4F(X)}\,.

(588)

In this case, the charge
q
on-shell is related to the electric field,
E=\ast\differential A
, and the dilaton,

q=Y=2F(X)\,\ast\differential A=2F(X)\,E

(589)

where we used the Carroll–Hodge-
\ast
relation
\ast(\tau\wedge e):=1
. For a definition of this operator, we refer to
[
118
]
. Charge conservation implies

\differential\big(F(X)\,\ast\differential A\big)=0\,.

(590)

Integrating out the scalar field
Y
by its own equation of motion yields a (non-minimally coupled) Maxwell term in the Lagrange 2-form (with the usual expression for the field strength,
F_{\mu\nu}=\partial_{\mu}A_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}A_{\mu}
).

{\mathcal{L}}=X\,\differential\omega+X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,\big(\differential\tau+\omega\wedge e\big)+X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}\,\differential e+\hat{\mathcal{V}}(X,\,X_{\textrm{\tiny H}})\,\tau\wedge e+F(X)\underbrace{(\ast\differential A)\differential A}_{\sim F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}\,\textrm{vol}}

(591)

Consistently, varying (
591
) with respect to the Maxwell
connection
A
yields the equation of motion (
590
). For
models that come from a dimensional reduction of higher-dimensional
Einstein–Maxwell theories the coupling function
F(X)
typically is
linear in the dilaton since the Maxwell term gets the same volume
factor as the curvature term
X\,\differential\omega
.

Finally, we note that often it is sufficient to consider the charge

q
as a parameter in the action rather than as a constant of motion.
In that case, one can use the potential (
588
) with
Y

replaced by its on-shell value
q
.

22.2
Carroll–Reissner–Nordström

There are different paths to obtaining CRN black holes. We found it
simplest to first reduce spherically Schwarzschild to 2d, then take
the Carroll limit, and finally, add a Maxwell field. This means we take
the Schwarzschild results for the potential
\hat{\mathcal{V}}
and set

F(X)=X
,

{\mathcal{V}}_{\textrm{\tiny CRN}}(X,\,X_{\textrm{\tiny H}},\,Y)=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{4}+\frac{X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}^{2}}{4X}-\frac{Y^{2}}{4X}\,.

(592)

Without loss of generality, we set
\lambda=2
.

In the coordinates introduced in section
16
, the CRN solution is given by

\differential s^{2}=\differential r^{2}=\frac{\differential X^{2}}{X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}^{2}}\qquad\qquad v=\frac{2\sqrt{X}}{X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}}\,\partial_{t}

(593)

and

X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}=\pm\sqrt{4X+4\,q_{e}^{2}-8m\sqrt{X}}\,,

(594)

where
q_{e}=\frac{q}{2}
is the electric charge and
m=\frac{M}{2}
is the mass. We again chose the integration constant in (
460
) such that
e^{-Q}=2\sqrt{X}
to achieve an asymptotic normalization of the vector field,
\lim_{X\to\infty}v=-\partial_{t}
.
The solution for the gauge field follows from (
585
),

\differential A=\frac{q_{e}}{2X}\,\tau\wedge e

(595)

which in Coulomb gauge leads to the usual Coulomb-potential

A=\frac{q_{e}}{\sqrt{X}}\,\differential t\,.

(596)

The Carroll limit on the gravity side is a magnetic limit, while the
Carroll limit on the Maxwell side is an electric limit, so this is an
example of electric Carroll Maxwell theory coupled to magnetic Carroll
gravity.
Carroll extremal surfaces in the CRN geometry arise at two loci,

\sqrt{X}_{\pm}=m\pm\sqrt{m^{2}-q_{e}^{2}}

(597)

provided the mass is positive and the charge obeys the BPS-bound

|q_{e}|\leq m\,.

(598)

When saturated,
q_{e}^{2}=m^{2}
, the two loci coalesce to a single degenerate Carroll extremal surface with vanishing Carroll temperature, similar to extremal Reissner–Nordström black holes.

While the CS model (see table
2
) does not have any constant dilaton vacuum solution, the CRN model has such a solution for the value of the dilaton

X=q_{e}^{2}\,.

(599)

Since this is the same value the dilaton takes at the degenerate extremal Carroll surface in the confluent case, one can interpret this constant dilaton vacuum analogously to the Robinson–Bertotti solution, i.e., as near-Carroll-extremal-surface degenerate geometry. Generalizations of CRN to arbitrary dimension is straightforward; one
just has to replace the first two terms in the potential
(
592
) by the corresponding CST potentials corresponding
to the desired dimension, see table
2
. We end our investigations on charged Carroll black holes here and refer to the outlook chapter
7
for more discussions and generalizations.
In the next subsection, we
address a different pertinent example, namely Carroll BTZ.

22.3
Carroll BTZ

It is not obvious how to take a Carroll limit of the BTZ
black hole
[
41
,
40
]
. We take the following
route. First, we Kaluza–Klein reduce along the azimuthal angle

\varphi
to obtain the 2d Achúcarro–Ortiz model

[
1
]
and only then we take the Carroll limit.

The first step leads to a charged 2d dilaton gravity model, where the
Maxwell field is the one appearing in the Kaluza–Klein ansatz
(
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in\{0,1\}
)

\differential s^{2}=g_{\alpha\beta}(x^{\gamma})\,\differential x^{\alpha}\differential x^{\beta}+X^{2}(x^{\gamma})\,\big(\differential\varphi+A_{\alpha}(x^{\gamma})\,\differential x^{\alpha}\big)^{2}

(600)

and the associated
U(1)
charge is the BTZ angular momentum
J
. The dimensionally reduced 2d model has the Achúcarro–Ortiz potential (see section 6.3 in
[
139
]
)

V_{\textrm{\tiny AO}}(X,\,Y)=\frac{X}{\ell^{2}}-\frac{Y^{2}}{X^{3}}

(601)

where
\ell
is the 3d AdS radius, which we set to one,
\ell=1
. On-shell
Y=J
.

The second step consists of importing the potential
(
601
) into generic 2d Carroll dilaton
gravity (
358
). This yields Carroll black
hole solutions we refer to as CBTZ. They are given by

\differential s^{2}=\differential r^{2}=\frac{\differential X^{2}}{X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}^{2}}\qquad\qquad v=\frac{1}{X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}}\,\partial_{t}

(602)

with

X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}=\pm\sqrt{X^{2}+\frac{J^{2}}{X^{2}}-2M}\,.

(603)

The solution for the gauge field follows from (
585
),

\differential A=\frac{2J}{X^{3}}\,\tau\wedge e

(604)

which, in Coulomb gauge, leads to

A=\frac{J}{X^{2}}\,\differential t\,.

(605)

As expected, there are two loci with Carroll extremal surfaces,

X^{2}_{\pm}=M\pm\sqrt{M^{2}-J^{2}}

(606)

provided the mass is positive and the angular momentum obeys the BPS bound

|J|\leq M\,.

(607)

When saturated,
J^{2}=M^{2}
, the Carroll extremal surface degenerates and has vanishing Carroll temperature, similar to extremal BTZ.

In summary, the Carroll BTZ black hole (
602
)-(
605
) defined here is the Carroll limit of the Achúcarro–Ortiz model, which in turn is a Kaluza–Klein reduction of the BTZ black hole. The Carroll BTZ black hole is a positive mass solution of 2d Carroll–Maxwell dilaton gravity (
584
) with the potential function (
601
), subject to the BPS bound (
607
).

Chapter 6
Quantum effects: The Carroll Hawking effect

\epigraphfontsize

“I have noticed that even those who assert that everything is predestined and that we can change nothing about it still look both ways before they cross the street.”

— Stephen Hawking, Black holes and baby universes, and other essays

In the previous chapter, we introduced Carroll black holes as certain exact solutions of 2d Carroll dilaton gravity that admit Carroll thermal properties. In particular, all these solutions have a finite Carroll temperature which is defined by entirely geometric ingredients. However, at this point it is not clear how far the analogy with a true, physical temperature goes. We shall further investigate this here by coupling a Carroll matter field theory to such backgrounds and observing how the vacuum states of its quantized version behave. As we shall show, given certain regularity conditions, the asymptotic energy density acquires a non-zero value which is compatible with the Stefan–Boltzmann law. Therefore, in this semi-classical regime the Carroll black hole temperature may indeed be associated with the temperature of a thermal state of a matter field coupled to it. This chapter is based on the original work
[
11
]
.

Before we delve into the Carroll version, we shall start with a brief summary of the Lorentzian/Euclidean argument to familiarize ourselves with the tools. One needs to keep in mind that at all stages the background geometry remains fixed and classical, the Hawking effect and its Carroll avatar are one-loop effects of the matter theory.

23
Quantum states on Lorentzian static backgrounds

While Hawking’s original derivation
[
158
]
proceeded by quantizing the modes of a scalar field directly, we shall follow the approach by Christensen and Fulling
[
85
]
which uses the Weyl anomaly of a conformally coupled field theory to infer properties of its vacuum states.

Consider the two-dimensional description of a 4d Schwarzschild black hole. It is given by a 2d metric together with a dilaton that is a function of the areal radius
\mathsf{r}
,

\displaystyle\differential s^{2}=-\xi(\mathsf{r})\differential t^{2}+\frac{\differential\mathsf{r}^{2}}{\xi(\mathsf{r})}

\displaystyle X=\mathsf{r}^{2}

(608)

where
\xi(\mathsf{r})=1-\frac{\mathsf{r}_{s}}{\mathsf{r}}
. The uplift to the 4d Schwarzschild metric is given by a similar expression as in the Carroll case (see sections
15.4
and
21
),

\displaystyle ds^{2}_{(4d)}=\differential s^{2}+X(\mathsf{r})\differential\Omega^{2}_{S^{(2)}}~.

(609)

Next, denoting the two-dimensional metric components by
G_{\mu\nu}
, consider a massless scalar field that minimally couples to
G_{\mu\nu}
. It is given by the action

\displaystyle I_{m}[\phi]=-\frac{1}{2}\int\differential^{2}x\sqrt{G}\,G^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial_{\nu}\phi

(610)

which classically enjoys Weyl invariance under
\delta_{\rho}G_{\mu\nu}=2\rho G_{\mu\mu}
and
\delta_{\rho}\phi=0
, i.e.
\delta_{\rho}I_{m}=0
. Since we are interested in a corresponding quantum theory, we shall now define the path integral for this theory. The Weyl symmetry will acquire an anomaly in that case.

23.1
Path integral and conformal anomaly

For the derivation of the Weyl anomaly we switch to Euclidean signature. The Euclidean partition function for the massless scalar is given by

\displaystyle Z[G]=e^{-W}=\int\mathcal{D}\phi\,e^{-I^{E}_{m}[\phi,G]}

(611)

where
I^{E}_{m}[\phi]
is the Euclidean action corresponding to (
610
) by performing a Wick rotation
t\to it
and setting
I_{m}=iI^{E}_{m}
. The vacuum expectation value of the stress-energy tensor
\mathfrak{T}^{\mu\nu}
is obtained by taking a functional variation with respect to the background metric,

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{T}^{\mu\nu}\rangle=\frac{2}{\sqrt{G}}\frac{\delta W}{\delta G_{\mu\nu}}~.

(612)

For defining the path integral above it is important to choose the measure
\mathcal{D}\phi
. This may be done in several inequivalent ways and must be handled with care if one requires some covariance aspects of the background to be preserved. Since we are in a setting where ultimately dynamical gravity could be coupled to the matter fields, it is natural to require diffeomorphism invariance to be preserved. The path integral measure is then defined by

\displaystyle 1=\int\mathcal{D}\phi\,e^{-\frac{1}{2}\int\differential^{2}x\,\sqrt{G}\phi^{2}}

(613)

which violates Weyl invariance. We stress that this is a choice motivated by the specific background. There are indeed situations where this choice does not lead to a sensible definition
[
143
]
, since the fall-off of the configurations
\phi
might not be strong enough to render the exponent in (
613
) finite. In such cases, one may instead define a Weyl invariant measure which, however, is not invariant under general diffeomorphisms
[
173
,
171
,
14
]
. In general, there is no way to define a measure that preserves both symmetries. Sticking to the former choice (
613
) we formally evaluate the partition function by

\displaystyle W[G]=\frac{1}{2}\log\det(L)~.

(614)

Since this expression is divergent, it needs to be evaluated in a regularized way before extracting correlation functions which can be done elegantly using heat-kernel methods
[
226
,
134
]
. We are specifically interested into an infinitesimal Weyl variation which was computed in the above references to be

\displaystyle\delta_{\rho}W=\frac{1}{24\pi}\int\differential^{2}x\,\sqrt{G}\,R\,\rho

(615)

where
R
is the Ricci scalar associated to
G_{\mu\nu}
. Together with (
612
) this implies

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{T}^{\mu}{}_{\mu}\rangle=\frac{1}{24\pi}R

(616)

which is the well-known expression for the two-dimensional Weyl anomaly on a Riemannian background. Since diffeomorphism invariance is preserved the conservation of the stress-energy tensor continues to hold at the quantum level,

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mu}\langle\mathfrak{T}^{\mu\nu}\rangle=0~.

(617)

As a next step we solve the two Ward identities (
616
), (
617
) for the vacuum expectation values
\langle\mathfrak{T}^{\mu\nu}\rangle
on static backgrounds.

23.2
Hawking effect

Switching back to Lorentzian signature it is convenient to change the coordinates
(t,\mathsf{r})
to
x^{\pm}
defined by

\displaystyle x^{\pm}=\frac{t\pm z}{\sqrt{2}}

\displaystyle\frac{\differential z}{\differential\mathsf{r}}=\frac{1}{\xi(\mathsf{r})}

(618)

such that the metric in (
608
) takes the form

\displaystyle\differential s^{2}=-2e^{2\Omega}\differential x^{+}\differential x^{-}

\displaystyle\Omega=\frac{1}{2}\log\xi(\mathsf{r})~.

(619)

The only non-vanishing Christoffel symbols are
\Gamma^{\pm}{}_{\pm\pm}=2\partial_{\pm}\Omega
, and we may write the two equations (
617
) as

\displaystyle\partial_{-}\langle\mathfrak{T}_{++}\rangle+\partial_{+}\langle\mathfrak{T}_{+-}\rangle-2\partial_{+}\Omega\langle\mathfrak{T}_{+-}\rangle

\displaystyle=0

(620)

\displaystyle\partial_{+}\langle\mathfrak{T}_{--}\rangle+\partial_{-}\langle\mathfrak{T}_{+-}\rangle-2\partial_{-}\Omega\langle\mathfrak{T}_{+-}\rangle

\displaystyle=0~.

(621)

Since we consider static backgrounds for which
\partial_{+}=-\partial_{-}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\partial_{z}
they may be simplified to

\displaystyle\partial_{z}\langle\mathfrak{T}_{\pm\pm}\rangle-\partial_{z}\langle\mathfrak{T}_{+-}\rangle+2\partial_{z}\Omega\langle\mathfrak{T}_{+-}\rangle=0~.

(622)

On the other hand, the trace Ward identity (
616
) implies

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{T}_{+-}\rangle=-\frac{1}{48\pi}e^{2\Omega}R=\frac{\partial_{z}^{2}\Omega}{24\pi}

(623)

such that insertion into (
622
) leads to two ordinary differential equations for
\langle\mathfrak{T}_{\pm\pm}\rangle
. The general solution is

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{T}_{\pm\pm}\rangle=\frac{1}{24\pi}\Big(\partial_{z}^{2}\Omega-(\partial_{z}\Omega)^{2}\Big)+t_{\pm}

\displaystyle t_{\pm}\in\mathbb{R}

(624)

where
t_{\pm}
are two integration constants. They parametrize a family of static quantum states of the model. Expressing the solution in terms of the metric function,
\xi(\mathsf{r})
this is

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{T}_{\pm\pm}\rangle=\frac{1}{96\pi}\Big(2\xi\partial_{\mathsf{r}}^{2}\xi-(\partial_{\mathsf{r}}\xi)^{2}\Big)+t_{\pm}

\displaystyle t_{\pm}\in\mathbb{R}~.

(625)

In this two-parameter family of states there are three special cases which differ in their regularity properties at infinity and at the horizon. First, note that
\langle\mathfrak{T}_{++}\rangle
describes the matter flux through surfaces of constant
x^{-}
which are ingoing null rays. Similarly,
\langle\mathfrak{T}_{--}\rangle
may be understood as describing outgoing matter flux. Expanding (
625
) for
r\to\infty
we find that the asymptotic values of these fluxes are given by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{T}_{--}\Big|_{\mathcal{I}_{+}}=t_{-}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{T}_{++}\Big|_{\mathcal{I}_{-}}=t_{+}

(626)

where
\mathcal{I}_{\pm}
denote future and past null infinity. On the other hand, one may expand the fluxes around the horizon at
\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{r}_{s}
. For this it is necessary to switch to regular coordinates there which is achieved by the Kruskal patch (see, e.g.,
[
144
]
for a review),

\displaystyle U=-e^{-x^{-}/\sqrt{2}\mathsf{r}_{s}}

\displaystyle V=e^{x^{+}/\sqrt{2}\mathsf{r}_{s}}~.

(627)

The future horizon
\mathcal{H}_{+}
is then given by the locus
U=0
while the past horizon
\mathcal{H}_{-}
is at
V=0
. Taking as an example the outgoing flux component we find

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{T}_{UU}\rangle

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{\differential U}{\differential x^{-}}\Big)^{-2}\langle\mathfrak{T}_{--}\rangle~.

(628)

This component is finite at
\mathcal{H}_{-}
but expands around
\mathcal{H}_{+}
as

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{T}_{UU}\rangle\sim\frac{2\mathsf{r}_{s}^{4}}{e^{2}(\mathsf{r}-\mathsf{r}_{s})^{2}}\Big(t_{-}-\frac{1}{96\pi\mathsf{r}_{s}^{2}}\Big)-\frac{4\mathsf{r}_{s}^{3}}{e^{2}(\mathsf{r}-\mathsf{r}_{s})}\Big(t_{-}-\frac{1}{96\pi\mathsf{r}_{s}^{2}}\Big)+\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{r}-\mathsf{r}_{s})^{0}

(629)

which diverges unless we choose the integration constant as
t_{-}=1/(96\pi\mathsf{r}_{s}^{2})
. A similar expansion for
\langle\mathfrak{T}_{++}\rangle
around the past horizon also gives a divergence unless we choose
t_{+}=1/(96\pi\mathsf{r}_{s}^{2})
. Therefore, demanding regularity at
\mathcal{H}_{\pm}
leads to a non-zero flux at
\mathcal{I}_{\pm}
. The three prominent choices for the constants
t_{\pm}
emerging from this behaviour are given in table
3
.

t_{+}

t_{-}

Properties

Boulware
[
68
]

0

0

no flux at
\mathcal{I}_{\pm}
, sing. at
\mathcal{H}_{\pm}

Unruh
[
224
]

0

\displaystyle 1/(96\pi r_{s}^{2})

only outgoing flux, reg. at
\mathcal{H}_{+}

Hartle–Hawking
[
152
]

\displaystyle 1/(96\pi r_{s}^{2})

\displaystyle 1/(96\pi r_{s}^{2})

flux at
\mathcal{I}_{\pm}
, reg. at
\mathcal{H}_{\pm}

Table 3:
Three prominent quantum states for a scalar field on a Schwarzschild background.

They are describing different physical situations.

The Boulware state forbids flux at infinity but pays the price of being singular along the entire horizon. Its physical significance lies in the description of quantum states around a static star. The analog of the Unruh state was in fact used by Hawking in his original derivation for particle creation around black hole horizons
[
158
]
. It describes the late-time behaviour of a collapsing star, there is no flux coming in from
\mathcal{I}_{-}
. This state is singular at the past horizon which is, however, not relevant in Hawking’s original scenario of a collapsing star. The white hole region is just regarded an unphysical artefact in that case. The Hartle–Hawking state describes the configuration of an eternal black hole being coupled to a thermal bath at infinity. This is the only state that is regular across the entire horizon of the maximally extended spacetime, in particular at the bifurcation sphere. The temperature of the bath is fixed in terms of the horizon radius of the black hole as the Hawking temperature

\displaystyle T=\frac{1}{4\pi\mathsf{r}_{s}}

(630)

such that the asymptotic flux follows the two-dimensional Stefan–Boltzmann law

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{T}_{++}\rangle\Big|_{\mathcal{I}_{-}}=\frac{\pi}{6}T^{2}=\langle\mathfrak{T}_{--}\rangle\Big|_{\mathcal{I}_{+}}~.

(631)

In other words, the asymptotic matter flux corresponds to true black-body radiation at a specific temperature fixed by the black hole geometry. Similarly, the matter energy density measured by a static observer at infinity is

\displaystyle\lim_{\mathsf{r}\to\infty}\langle\mathcal{E}\rangle=\lim_{\mathsf{r}\to\infty}\langle\mathfrak{T}^{t}{}_{t}\rangle=\frac{\pi}{6}T^{2}~.

(632)

We shall find a similar conclusion for the Carroll case to which we turn now.

24
Carroll–Hawking effect from a limit

As a first approach to investigate whether some analogue of the Hawking effect exists for Carroll black holes, we perform a limit from the Lorentzian case. The background we are interested in is the Carroll Schwarzschild black hole as described in section
20.3
. In its two-dimensional formulation it is given by

\displaystyle h_{\mu\nu}\differential x^{\mu}\differential x^{\nu}=\frac{\differential\mathsf{r}^{2}}{\xi(\mathsf{r})}

\displaystyle\tau=\sqrt{\xi(\mathsf{r})}\differential t

\displaystyle v=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\xi(\mathsf{r})}}\partial_{t}

\displaystyle X=\mathsf{r}^{2}

(633)

where
\xi(\mathsf{r})=1-\frac{\mathsf{r}_{s}}{\mathsf{r}}
with
\mathsf{r}_{s}=2m
.

Consider the vacuum expectation values of the stress-energy energy tensor associated to a massless scalar field as in the previous section. For taking the limit
c\to 0
it is important to reinsert
c
into these expressions, i.e., we define the light-cone coordinates by

\displaystyle x^{\pm}=\frac{ct\pm z}{\sqrt{2}}~.

(634)

With this modification one arrives again at the components of a Lorentzian stress-energy tensor

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{T}_{\pm\pm}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{96\pi}\Big(2\xi\partial_{\mathsf{r}}^{2}\xi-(\partial_{\mathsf{r}}\xi)^{2}\Big)+t_{\pm}

\displaystyle t_{\pm}\in\mathbb{R}

(635)

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{T}_{+-}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{48\pi}\xi\partial_{\mathsf{r}}^{2}\xi

(636)

which solve the diffeomorphism and anomalous Weyl Ward identities. Note, that no explicit factors of
c
appear in these expressions. We need to transform these components back to a static coordinate system to make them compatible with the chosen background. As a final step, we adapt the index structure to obtain a proper Carroll stress-energy tensor with one upper and one lower index (see eq. (
188
)). This leads to

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{T}^{t}{}_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{24\pi}\Big(\frac{1}{4\xi}(\partial_{\mathsf{r}}\xi)^{2}-\partial_{\mathsf{r}}^{2}\xi-12\pi\frac{t_{+}+t_{-}}{\xi}\Big)

(637a)

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{T}^{t}{}_{\mathsf{r}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{t_{-}-t_{+}}{2\,c\,\xi^{2}}

(637b)

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{T}^{\mathsf{r}}{}_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{c}{2}(t_{-}-t_{+})

(637c)

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{T}^{\mathsf{r}}{}_{\mathsf{r}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{24\pi}\Big(-\frac{(\partial_{\mathsf{r}}\xi)^{2}}{4\xi}+12\pi\frac{t_{+}+t_{-}}{\xi}\Big)

(637d)

In this limit,
\langle\mathfrak{T}^{t}{}_{r}\rangle\rightarrow\infty
unless we assume

\displaystyle t_{+}-t_{-}=ct_{0}

(638)

with some fixed constant
t_{0}
. With this assumption, we obtain

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{T}^{t}{}_{t}\rangle\to\langle\mathbf{T}^{t}{}_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{24\pi}\Big(\partial_{\mathsf{r}}^{2}\xi-\frac{(\partial_{\mathsf{r}}\xi)^{2}}{4\xi}+\frac{24\pi t_{+}}{\xi}\Big)

(639a)

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{T}^{t}{}_{\mathsf{r}}\rangle\to\langle\mathbf{T}^{t}{}_{\mathsf{r}}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\frac{t_{0}}{\xi^{2}}

(639b)

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{T}^{\mathsf{r}}{}_{t}\rangle\to\langle\mathbf{T}^{\mathsf{r}}{}_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle=0

(639c)

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{T}^{\mathsf{r}}{}_{\mathsf{r}}\rangle\to\langle\mathbf{T}^{\mathsf{r}}{}_{\mathsf{r}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{24\pi}\Big(-\frac{(\partial_{\mathsf{r}}\xi)^{2}}{4\xi}+\frac{24\pi t_{+}}{\xi}\Big)~.

(639d)

This result satisfies the Carroll boost and diffeomorphism Ward identities presented in section
10.1
,

\displaystyle h_{\mu\alpha}\langle\mathbf{T}^{\mu}{}_{\nu}\rangle v^{\nu}=0

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mu}\langle\mathbf{T}^{\mu}{}_{\nu}\rangle=0

(640)

where we already inserted
K_{\mu\nu}=0
for the Carroll Schwarzschild background. The trace Ward identity stays anomalous after the limit. Taking the limit of the flux components (
635
) leads to

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{T}_{\pm\pm}\rangle:=\lim_{c\to 0}\langle\mathfrak{T}_{\pm\pm}\rangle=\frac{1}{96\pi}\Big(2\xi\partial_{r}^{2}\xi-(\partial_{r}\xi)^{2}\Big)+t_{+}

(641)

which shows that both fluxes have to agree,
\langle\mathbf{T}_{++}\rangle=\langle\mathbf{T}_{--}\rangle
. This is unlike the situation we saw previously in the Lorentzian theory (see section
23.2
), where
\langle\mathfrak{T}_{\pm\pm}\rangle
would be associated with in- and outgoing matter fluxes. They could be chosen independently of each other, according to the physical situation at hand. The fact that both fluxes have to agree in the present case is just another manifestation of no (net) energy flux being possible in a Carroll theory
[
95
]
. It furthermore implies that not all vacuum choices of the analogous Lorentzian theory are possible anymore. In particular, local Carroll boost-invariance is inconsistent with the Unruh vacuum.
Thus, there are only two of the three special cases in table
1
remaining, Boulware and Hartle–Hawking, which may be distinguished by the energy density at infinity and at the Carroll extremal surface. Its general expression reads

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{E}\rangle=-\tau_{\mu}\langle T^{\mu}{}_{\nu}\rangle v^{\nu}=\frac{1}{24\pi}\Big(\partial_{r}^{2}\xi-\frac{(\partial_{r}\xi)^{2}}{4\xi}\Big)+\frac{t_{+}}{\xi}

(642)

demanding a vanishing
\langle\mathcal{E}\rangle
at infinity fixes
t_{+}=0
. However, analogous to the Lorentzian case the energy density blows up at the Carroll extremal surface which corresponds to the Boulware vacuum. Making instead the unique choice
t_{+}=\frac{1}{96\pi{\mathsf{r}}_{s}^{2}}
renders
\langle\mathcal{E}\rangle
regular at
\mathsf{r}_{s}
and defines the Carroll analog of the Hartle–Hawking vacuum with asymptotic energy density

\lim_{\mathsf{r}\to\infty}\langle\mathcal{E}\rangle=\frac{1}{96\pi\mathsf{r}_{s}^{2}}=\frac{\pi}{6}\,T^{2}~.

(643)

In the second equality we used the result for the Carroll temperature
T
of the Carroll–Schwarzschild background,
T^{-1}=4\pi\mathsf{r}_{s}
as derived in section
20.3
. This equality is our main result
[
10
]
and shows that the asymptotic energy density (
643
) is compatible with the 2d Stefan-Boltzmann law.

25
The conformal anomaly in Carroll theories

Instead of taking Carrollian limits, we consider in this section the magnetic scalar action (
275
) from the start and solve the Carroll Ward identities, analogous to the procedure in the Lorentzian case (see section
23.2
). We set the potential
V(\phi)=0
for simplicity. Plugging it into the path integral yields

\displaystyle Z=\int\mathcal{D}\chi\mathcal{D}\phi\exp\Big(-I_{\textrm{M}}[\chi,\pi]\Big)=e^{-W}

(644)

where

\displaystyle I_{M}[\chi,\phi]=-\int\differential^{d+1}x\,\mathfrak{e}\,\Big(\chi v^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\phi-\frac{1}{2}h^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial_{\nu}\phi\Big)~.

(645)

Integrating out
\chi
produces a functional
\delta
-function
\delta(v^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\phi)
so that we remain with a path integral over a 1d time-independent scalar field, but with a Jacobian factor
\mathcal{J}=(\det(v^\mu\partial_\mu))^{-1}
. The operator
v^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}
contains a derivative along the time direction but no derivative along the spatial direction. This means that the operator is not elliptic, so there is no regular method known to us to define its determinant
27
27

27

This situation is similar to one with a Faddeev–Popov determinant in axial gauge on a curved background
[
227
]
.
. Since a direct method fails, we try a less direct one.

We assume that the path integral (
644
) exists (see also the more recent treatment
[
228
]
) and write a general variation with respect to the background fields as

\displaystyle\delta W=\int_{\mathcal{M}}\tau\wedge e\,\big(\langle T_{(\tau)}^{\mu}\rangle\delta\tau_{\mu}+\langle T_{(e)}^{\mu}\rangle\delta e_{\mu}+\langle T_{(\omega)}^{\mu}\rangle\delta\omega_{\mu}\big)

(646)

where the functions
\langle T_{(\tau)}^{\mu}\rangle
,
\langle T_{(e)}^{\mu}\rangle
and
\langle T_{(\omega)}^{\mu}\rangle
are the response functions to varying
\tau
,
e
and
\omega
, respectively. In particular, a Weyl variation of the effective action is

\displaystyle\delta_{\rho}W=\int_{\mathcal{M}}\tau\wedge e\,\big(\langle T_{(\tau)}^{\mu}\rangle\delta_{\rho}\tau_{\mu}+\langle T_{(e)}^{\mu}\rangle\delta_{\rho}e_{\mu}+\langle T_{(\omega)}^{\mu}\rangle\delta_{\rho}\omega_{\mu}\big)~.

(647)

Ultimately, the background is given by some solution of magnetic Carroll dilaton gravity, which we shall again take to be the 2d Carroll–Schwarzschild solution. In a first-order formulation the background field content is therefore given by
(\tau,e,\omega)
and
(X,X_{\textrm{\tiny H}},X_{\textrm{\tiny P}})
(cf. section
15.2
) and we also assumed in (
646
) that the matter theory does not couple to the latter background data
28
28

28

A common generalization of this is to allow a coupling to the dilaton
X
, which typically arises from spherically reducing higher-dimensional matter models
[
134
]
.
. Demanding that the conformal anomaly is local and Carroll boost invariant, there are only two choices with the correct mass dimension,

\displaystyle\delta_{\rho}W=\int_{\mathcal{M}}\;\rho\big(\alpha_{1}\,\differential\omega+\alpha_{2}\differential e\big)

\displaystyle\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathbb{R}

(648)

where we used that under a Carroll boost
\delta_{\lambda}\omega=\differential\lambda
. Moreover, since the term
\differential e
vanishes for any solution to magnetic Carroll dilaton gravity it does not lead to anomalous terms in the Weyl Ward identity. We therefore drop this term from now on. The dimensionless coefficient
\alpha_{1}
stays undetermined at this point and will be fixed later by demanding consistency with the asymptotic energy density derived in the previous section.

For computing the Weyl Ward identity we first need to prescribe the transformation behaviour of the Carroll boost connection
\omega
. While, in general, there is some freedom in choosing this transformation if the background theory is unspecified, we may use the Weyl-transformation properties of 2d Carroll dilaton gravity introduced in section
15.2
as a guidance. Demanding on-shell consistency with (
370
) we propose

\displaystyle\delta_{\rho}\omega=\big(-\tau e^{\mu}+ev^{\mu}\big)\partial_{\mu}\rho~.

(649)

Indeed, one may check that for a dilaton dependent
\rho(X)
this reduces to (
370
) once the equations of motion and the gauge condition
X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}=0
are used. On static backgrounds infinitesimally close to flat Carroll spacetime this also makes the Weyl anomaly match with the result of
[
30
]
. Finally, the expression (
648
) with
\alpha_{2}=0
can be shown to be consistent in the sense of Bardeen and Zumino
[
44
]
, i.e.,

\displaystyle[\delta_{\rho_{1}},\delta_{\rho_{2}}]W=0~.

(650)

This is another reason for setting
\alpha_{2}=0
, since this term would not lead to a consistent anomaly. Using that the frame variables transform as in section
9
with
z=1
, i.e.
\delta_{\rho}\tau=\rho\tau
and
\delta_{\rho}e=\rho e
, we may determine the Weyl Ward identity as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{e}\big(\langle T^{\mu}_{(\tau)}\rangle\tau_{\mu}+\langle T^{\mu}_{(e)}\rangle e_{\mu}\big)-\partial_{\alpha}\Big(\mathfrak{e}\langle T^{\mu}_{(\omega)}\rangle(-\tau_{\mu}e^{\alpha}+e_{\mu}v^{\alpha})\Big)=\alpha_{1}2\mathfrak{e}e^{\mu}v^{\nu}\partial_{[\mu}\omega_{\nu]}~.

(651)

Similarly, the Carroll boost and diffeomorphism Ward identities are given by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{e}\langle T_{(\tau)}^{\mu}\rangle e_{\mu}+\partial_{\mu}(\mathfrak{e}\langle T^{\mu}_{(\omega)}\rangle)=0

(652)

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}\Big(\mathfrak{e}\langle T^{\mu}_{(\tau)}\rangle\tau_{\alpha}+\mathfrak{e}\langle T^{\mu}_{(e)}\rangle e_{\alpha}+\mathfrak{e}\langle T^{\mu}_{(\omega)}\rangle\omega_{\alpha}\Big)=\mathfrak{e}\langle T^{\mu}_{(\tau)}\rangle\partial_{\alpha}\tau_{\mu}+\mathfrak{e}\langle T^{\mu}_{(e)}\rangle\partial_{\alpha}e_{\mu}+\mathfrak{e}\langle T^{\mu}_{(\omega)}\rangle\partial_{\alpha}\omega_{\mu}~.

(653)

We proceed with solving these identities for the 2d Carroll Schwarzschild black hole of section
20.3
. The background fields are given by (
633
) together with

\displaystyle\omega=\frac{2\mathsf{r}-\mathsf{r}_{s}}{\mathsf{r}^{2}}\differential t~.

(654)

Note, that in these coordinates we have
\mathfrak{e}=1
. We may first use the Carroll boost- and Weyl Ward identities to algebraically solve for
T^{\mu}_{(\tau)}
,

\displaystyle T_{(\tau)}=\Big[-\frac{\mathsf{r}^{2}\langle T_{(e)}^{\mathsf{r}}\rangle+\xi^{3}\alpha_{1}}{\mathsf{r}^{2}\xi^{2}}+\frac{\mathsf{r}(\xi^{2}-1)\langle T_{(\omega)}^{t}\rangle+\xi^{2}\mathsf{r}^{2}\partial_{\mathsf{r}}\langle T_{(\omega)}^{t}\rangle}{\mathsf{r}^{2}\xi}\Big]\partial_{t}-\xi\partial_{\mathsf{r}}\langle T_{(\omega)}^{\mathsf{r}}\rangle\partial_{\mathsf{r}}~.

(655)

Inserting into the diffeomorphism Ward identities then yields two equations in four unknowns,

\displaystyle 2\mathsf{r}^{2}\xi^{2}\partial_{\mathsf{r}}^{2}\langle T_{(\omega)}^{\mathsf{r}}\rangle+(\mathsf{r}-3\mathsf{r}\xi^{2})\partial_{\mathsf{r}}\langle T_{(\omega)}^{\mathsf{r}}\rangle+2\xi^{2}

\displaystyle\langle T_{(\omega)}^{\mathsf{r}}\rangle=0

(656)

\displaystyle\partial_{\mathsf{r}}\langle T_{(e)}^{\mathsf{r}}\rangle+\frac{1-\xi^{2}}{2\mathsf{r}\xi^{2}}\langle T_{(e)}^{\mathsf{r}}\rangle+\alpha_{1}\frac{\xi^{3}-\xi}{2\mathsf{r}^{3}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\xi^{2}-3\xi^{4}-1}{2\mathsf{r}^{2}\xi}\langle T_{(\omega)}^{t}\rangle+\frac{\xi^{3}-\xi}{2\mathsf{r}}\partial_{\mathsf{r}}\langle T_{(\omega)}^{t}\rangle

(657)

which may be solved for
\langle T_{(\omega)}^{\mathsf{r}}\rangle
and
\langle T_{(e)}^{\mathsf{r}}\rangle
once
\langle T_{(\omega)}^{t}\rangle
is given. The component
\langle T_{(e)}^{t}\rangle
does not feature in these equations and may be chosen arbitrarily. Thus, in contrast to the Lorentzian case, we do not have enough conditions to define all components of the Carroll stress-energy tensor. We leave a more general analysis of the space of solutions to future work (see also chapter
7
) and for now just focus on the simple choices

\displaystyle\langle T_{(\omega)}^{t}\rangle=0

\displaystyle\langle T_{(e)}^{t}\rangle=q(\mathsf{r})

(658)

where
q(\mathsf{r})
is some function of
\mathsf{r}
. Equations (
656
) and (
657
) may then be solved by

\displaystyle\langle T_{(\omega)}^{\mathsf{r}}\rangle=0

\displaystyle\langle T_{(e)}\rangle=q(\mathsf{r})\partial_{t}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\xi}}\Big(c_{0}+\alpha_{1}\frac{3\mathsf{r}_{s}^{2}-4\mathsf{r}_{s}\mathsf{r}}{24\mathsf{r}^{4}}\Big)\partial_{\mathsf{r}}

(659)

with an integration constant
c_{0}
. In summary, this provides us with a class of static solutions to the Ward identities (
651
), (
652
) and (
653
) given by

\displaystyle\langle T_{(\omega)}\rangle=0

\displaystyle\langle T_{(e)}\rangle=q(\mathsf{r})\partial_{t}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\xi}}\Big(c_{0}+\alpha_{1}\frac{3\mathsf{r}_{s}^{2}-4\mathsf{r}_{s}\mathsf{r}}{24\mathsf{r}^{4}}\Big)\partial_{\mathsf{r}}

\displaystyle T_{(\tau)}=-\frac{\mathsf{r}^{2}\langle T_{(e)}^{\mathsf{r}}\rangle+\xi^{3}\alpha_{1}}{\mathsf{r}^{2}\xi^{2}}\partial_{t}

(660)

parametrized by
c_{0}
and
q(\mathsf{r})
. Let us look at the physical properties of the corresponding states. We may construct a conserved current for each background symmetry analogous to what was done in section
10.2
. A symmetry is defined by a vector field
\zeta
that leaves the background invariant up to local Carroll transformations (see also the discussions in sections
7.3
and
16.3
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}\tau+\delta_{\lambda(\zeta)}\tau=0

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}e=0

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}\omega+\delta_{\lambda(\zeta)}\omega=0

(661)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}X=0

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}=0

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\zeta}X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}+\delta_{\lambda(\zeta)}X_{\textrm{\tiny P}}=0~.

(662)

One can then show by using the generalized covariant conservation equation (
653
) that the current density defined by

\displaystyle\langle j^{\mu}\rangle=\mathfrak{e}\langle T_{(\tau)}^{\mu}\rangle\tau_{\nu}\zeta^{\nu}+\mathfrak{e}\langle T_{(e)}^{\mu}\rangle e_{\nu}\zeta^{\nu}+\mathfrak{e}\langle T_{(\omega)}^{\mu}\rangle\omega_{\nu}\zeta^{\nu}+\mathfrak{e}\langle T_{(\omega)}^{\mu}\rangle\lambda(\zeta)

(663)

is indeed conserved,
\partial_{\mu}\langle j^{\mu}\rangle=0
. Since for our background time translations
\zeta=\partial_{t}
are such a symmetry with
\lambda(\zeta)=0
we obtain an associated energy density

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{E}\rangle=\langle j^{t}\rangle=\langle T_{(\tau)}^{t}\rangle\sqrt{\xi}+\langle T_{(\omega)}^{t}\rangle\omega_{t}

(664)

which for states with (
660
) is regular at
\mathsf{r}\to\mathsf{r}_{s}
if the integration constant is fixed as

\displaystyle c_{0}=\frac{\alpha_{1}}{24\pi\mathsf{r}_{s}^{2}}~.

(665)

This in turn produces an asymptotic energy density

\lim_{r\to\infty}\langle\mathcal{E}\rangle=-\frac{\alpha_{1}}{24r_{s}^{2}}

(666)

which coincides precisely with the Carroll–Hartle–Hawking energy density (
643
) for
\alpha_{1}=-\frac{1}{4\pi}
. As a consequence of the Carroll boost Ward identity, there is again no energy flux allowed for the chosen state, i.e.,
\partial_{t}\langle\mathcal{E}\rangle=0
.

While this derivation is not entirely independent of the previous one, since it required choosing the anomaly coefficient
\alpha_{1}
appropriately it still suggests a similar behaviour of Carroll field theories coupled to two-dimensional Carroll black holes: The states of such field theories acquire temperature dependent expectation values of the asymptotic energy density once regularity at the Carroll extremal surface is enforced. However, in neither case can the Carroll black hole evaporate since the energy flux is identically vanishing. We shall conclude our analysis here and refer to the outlook chapter
7
for further discussions.

Chapter 7
Conclusion and Outlook

We shall conclude our explorations in the world of Carroll symmetries by pointing out a few open ends and suggesting several directions that may be worthwhile to investigate in future research.

Rotating Carroll black holes in 3d

While we gave a possible definition for a rotating Carroll black hole in 3d in subsection
22.3
, the Carroll BTZ black hole, this approach was somewhat indirect: It used an intrinsically two-dimensional description in terms of Carroll dilaton gravity obtained after performing a Kaluza–Klein reduction of three-dimensional Einstein gravity with
\Lambda<0
. For convenience, we present this solution here again,

\displaystyle h_{\mu\nu}\differential x^{\mu}\differential x^{\nu}=\frac{\differential X^{2}}{X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}^{2}}

\displaystyle v^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}=-\frac{1}{X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}}\partial_{t}

\displaystyle\tau_{\mu}\differential x^{\mu}=X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}\,\differential t

\displaystyle A=\frac{J}{X}\differential t

(667)

where
X_{\textrm{\tiny H}}=-\sqrt{X^{4}-2MX^{2}+J^{2}}/X
. From a 2d point of view, the angular momentum ends up being encoded into an additional constant of motion
J
that is just the
U(1)
charge of a 2d electric Carroll Maxwell field. One may ask whether there still exists an uplift of these two-dimensional variables that is a solution to some higher-dimensional theory of Carroll gravity. A difficulty arising here is a certain asymmetry in the Carroll limit: The 2d metric part is described by a magnetic limit, while the Maxwell part is described by an electric limit. It is not clear how taking such an “electromagnetic limit” should work directly in the 3d setting, where there is really just a metric and no Maxwell field.

Nevertheless, recent investigations of three-dimensional magnetic AdS-Carroll gravity
[
19
]
show that there do exist solutions with non-zero angular momentum. The latter is thereby defined as an asymptotic boundary charge, and the corresponding bulk geometries exhibit thermal properties that indeed satisfy a first law, albeit with an entropy term that has a sign more reminiscent of cosmological solutions. In this regard, those geometries behave differently from the ones discussed here suggesting that the three-dimensional theory we are looking for is not magnetic Carroll gravity.

Rotating Carroll black holes in higher dimensions

So far, we did not touch a higher-dimensional version of rotating Carroll black holes such as a Carroll limit of the Kerr spacetime. A naive limit of the latter in, e.g., Boyer–Lindquist coordinates may be attempted by first writing the Lorentzian metric in terms of the energy
E
and the coupling constant
G_{M}
, which we both want to hold fixed (see section
13
),

\displaystyle\differential s^{2}=-\Big(1-\frac{2EG_{M}r}{\Sigma}\Big)c^{2}\differential t^{2}-\frac{4EG_{M}ar\sin^{2}\theta}{\Sigma}

\displaystyle c\,\differential t\differential\phi+\frac{\Sigma}{\Delta}\differential r^{2}+\Sigma\differential\theta^{2}

(668)

\displaystyle+\Big(r^{2}+a^{2}+\frac{2EG_{M}a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta}{\Sigma}\Big)\sin^{2}\theta\differential\phi^{2}

where
\Delta=r^{2}-2G_{M}Er+a^{2}
and
\Sigma=r^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta
. The angular momentum parameter is given by
a=Jc/E
, and one observes two possible ways of taking the limit on the level of this constant: Either we fix
J
, which implies
a\to 0
in the limit and thus reduces the geometry to the Carroll–Schwarzschild spacetime. Alternatively, we might fix
a
, which yields a diagonal Carroll metric

\displaystyle h_{\mu\nu}\differential x^{\mu}\differential x^{\nu}=\frac{\Sigma}{\Delta}\differential r^{2}+\Sigma\differential\theta^{2}+\Big(r^{2}+a^{2}+\frac{2EG_{M}a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta}{\Sigma}\Big)\sin^{2}\theta\differential\phi^{2}~.

(669)

However, such a metric does not solve the constraints of magnetic Carroll gravity as introduced in section
14.2
. In particular, one can show that the Ricci scalar of the associated ADM-metric is non-zero,
R^{(3)}[h_{ij}]\neq 0
. Thus, it seems that there is no Carroll limit of the Kerr spacetime that is a solution of magnetic Carroll gravity and at the same time carries non-zero angular momentum. This was made more precise in a recent no-go theorem
[
177
]
.

There is one catch in this story that we would like to point out, and that constitutes a possibly interesting research avenue: It addresses Carroll geometries as limits of Lorentzian geometries. However, there is evidence that not all features of Carroll theories can be obtained from a
c\to 0
limit, e.g., on a level of representation theory
[
120
]
or on a level of quantum field theories
[
93
]
. In other words, there might still be a possibility of defining a truly rotating higher-dimensional black hole in magnetic Carroll gravity - it just does not arise from a limit. Further evidence in support of this assertion comes from an asymptotic symmetry analysis
[
197
]
, which shows that there do exist well-defined boundary charges associated to angular momentum. It could be interesting to start from there and try to construct geometries that exhibit non-vanishing values of these charges.

Charged Carroll black holes

Similarly to the Carroll BTZ black hole, we defined charged Carroll black holes in a spherically reduced theory after taking the Carroll limit. It would be pleasing to see that first taking such a limit
in higher dimensions and then performing a spherical reduction
leads to the same 2d solutions. Additionally, in
[
96
]
the combined magnetic gravity and magnetic Maxwell limits were investigated for a four-dimensional Reissner–Nordström black hole carrying both electric and magnetic charge. It would be interesting to examine how these features manifest after performing a spherical reduction and to determine what kind of effective two-dimensional model emerges in this case.

Generalizations to completely different types of charged Carroll black
holes are possible as well and can be done on a case-by-case basis.
Examples that come to mind are 2d type 0A string theory with equal
number
q_{e}
of electric and magnetic D0 branes
[
106
,
147
]
and the dimensionally reduced Chern–Simons term

[
150
,
135
]
.

Carroll fields coupled to the connection

We have seen in section
7
that, once requiring a Carroll geometry to carry a torsion-free and metric-compatible connection, there are still independent degrees of freedom remaining in a part of the connection. This is in contrast to the Riemannian case. On a (magnetic) Carroll gravity level, this part is quite important, since it plays the role of the canonical momenta (
348
) in an ADM formulation or the one of Lagrange multipliers in a second order formulation (see eq. (
345
)), ensuring local Carroll boost invariance of the action.

When analyzing field theories on such backgrounds, it is natural to also consider couplings to this part of the connection. We presented an example of a scalar field with this property in (
277
), but otherwise focused on the simplified case in which this coupling is absent. It would be interesting to explore this possibility in more detail and to study explicit solutions of the resulting theories. Since the Carroll boost Ward identity acquires additional terms in this setting (see section
10.2
), statements such as the absence of energy flux will have to be reconsidered.

A specific application that comes to mind in this case is holography in asymptotically flat spacetimes. While there is additional conformal symmetry present in that case, the undetermined part of the connection still has a similar structure and encodes the gravitational shear. Thus, any holographically dual theory should also couple to this field if it is to carry information about the radiative structure of spacetime.

Asymptotic symmetries of 4d asymptotically flat spacetimes

Related to the previous point, we have seen in section
11
how an intrinsically Carroll derivation of stress-energy tensor brackets is able to reproduce the asymptotic symmetries of three-dimensional asymptotically flat spacetimes for Einstein gravity. The only input from holography was the presence of a Carroll boost anomaly which could be related to a certain central extension being switched on. It would be interesting to perform a similar computation for the four-dimensional case. An important difference in that regard is the additional coupling to the gravitational shear, which introduces further response functions in the variation of the partition function
[
154
]
.
A Carroll derivation of the BMS charge brackets—originally obtained from bulk considerations in
[
51
]
—could provide a deeper understanding of their geometric origin and illuminate how these structures emerge naturally within the Carroll framework. This may also shed further light on the holographic principle applied to asymptotically flat spacetimes.

One-loop effects on Carroll Schwarzschild spacetime

We have seen in section
25
that the Ward identities of a Carroll quantum field theory on a 2d Carroll Schwarzschild background involve additional response functions
\langle T_{(\omega)}^{\mu}\rangle
associated to varying the Carroll boost connection. We only considered a special family of states where these responses identically vanish - this allowed us to connect with Carroll versions of the Hartle–Hawking or Boulware states obtained as a limit from the Lorentzian setting. It could be worthwile to study more general scenarios where
\langle T_{(\omega)}^{\mu}\rangle\neq 0
and see which possibilities arise from demanding regularity at the Carroll extremal surface or at infinity. Given the discussions in the previous paragraphs about rotating Carroll geometries, it is plausible that there exist cases which do not follow from any limit of a Lorentzian theory.

Tantum gravity limit on the field theory side

We mostly looked at the tantum gravity limit from a bulk perspective, since we were interested in the solutions of its magnetic Carroll gravity approximation. Following the typical procedure in holography, one might also ask about possible asymptotic falloff conditions and the associated asymptotic symmetries in order to find out more about a putative holographically dual theory. There are already some investigations in these directions in two
[
136
]
, three
[
19
]
and four spacetime dimensions
[
198
]
, mostly on the level of asymptotic symmetries. It would also be interesting to investigate whether, for example in the case of the Carroll JT model, the Cardy-like formula derived in subsection
20.1
admits an interpretation from a dual viewpoint based on microscopic state counting. Understanding whether such a statistical perspective exists could shed further light on the thermodynamic structure of Carroll black holes.

Carroll de Sitter

Finally, we mention that one may also look at Carroll limits of cosmological solutions. As a simple scenario consider again a description in terms of two-dimensional Carroll dilaton gravity (
357
) where, e.g., the choice of potentials

\displaystyle U(X)=-\frac{1}{2X}

\displaystyle V(X)=1-\Lambda X

(670)

together with
X=\mathsf{r}^{2}
leads to the Carroll metric data

\displaystyle\differential s^{2}=\frac{\differential\mathsf{r}^{2}}{1-\frac{M}{\mathsf{r}}-\frac{\Lambda}{3}\mathsf{r}^{2}}

\displaystyle v=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{{1-\frac{M}{\mathsf{r}}-\frac{\Lambda}{3}\mathsf{r}^{2}}}}\partial_{t}~.

(671)

For
\Lambda>0
, this describes a Carroll limit of a Schwarzschild–de Sitter black hole in the static patch. It would be interesting to investigate the thermodynamics and Carroll extremal surfaces of this spacetime and explore whether it is again possible to get a smooth solution for some
M
and choice of periodicity
\beta
, i.e., try to apply the methods we used in subsection
16.5
.
